# Nearby Star Systems — Exoplanet Catalog

A comprehensive catalog of 67 star systems with detailed planetary data for over 310 bodies (planets and moons). Zones 1-4 cover 56 systems within 100 light-years of Earth. Zone 5 covers 11 systems at the galactic center near Sagittarius A*, ~26,000 light-years from Earth — the destination of Scenario 5: The Expedition.

**Life-bearing worlds:** 11 independent origins of life discovered across the catalog, plus one engineered chimeric biosphere (Crucible). Includes 3 worlds with oxygen-producing photosynthetic biospheres and breathable atmospheres.

---

## Zone 1: The Near Stars (0-12 light-years)

The nearest stellar neighborhood. These are the first interstellar destinations — reachable with early fusion drives, each system a frontier outpost before the deeper sky opens up. Zone 1 contains 18 star systems, 3 life-bearing worlds, and over 70 planets and moons ranging from molten hellscapes to frozen dwarfs to tantalizing near-Earths.

---

### 1. Alpha Centauri (Rigil Kentaurus)
- **Distance:** 4.37 ly (Proxima: 4.24 ly)
- **Galactic Position:** (20.8, -20989.0) ly
- **Galactic Velocity:** (205.1, 11.8) km/s
- **Galactic Orbit:** SMA: 19,776.3 ly | Ecc: 0.083 | ω: 129.6° | M₀: 134.1° | Period: 170.5 Myr
- **Spectral Types:** G2V / K1V / M5.5Ve
- **Stars:**
  - Alpha Centauri A: Mass 1.10 M☉ | Radius 1.22 R☉ | Luminosity 1.52 L☉
  - Alpha Centauri B: Mass 0.91 M☉ | Radius 0.86 R☉ | Luminosity 0.50 L☉
  - Proxima Centauri (Alpha Centauri C): Mass 0.12 M☉ | Radius 0.15 R☉ | Luminosity 0.0017 L☉

The closest star system to Sol. A triple system: the bright binary pair A and B orbit each other at 11-36 AU, while the dim red dwarf Proxima traces a vast, loosely bound orbit around them at roughly 12,000 AU. This is humanity's first interstellar landfall, and it does not disappoint — a parallel solar system orbiting A, a compact trio around B, and the momentous discovery of life at Proxima. Arriving here feels like coming home to a mirror universe.

#### Planets of Alpha Centauri A

**Ab — "Caldera"** (Hot rocky Mercury-analog)
- Orbital: 0.30 AU | Ecc: 0.08 | Period: 53 days
- Physical: 0.45 M⊕ | Radius: 0.80 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.70 g | Temp: 625 K
- Atmosphere: None
- Habitability: 5/100
- Science: 30
- Resources: Metal Ore (rich, refractory metals), Regolith, Rare Earth Elements
- Solar Power: 16.89×
> A sun-scorched cinder locked in a slow 3:2 spin-orbit resonance, much like Mercury. The dayside reaches 700 K — hot enough to pool tin and lead in crater floors as gleaming metallic lakes. The nightside plunges below 100 K. A terminator band of shattered basalt separates these extremes. Rich in refractory metals: tungsten, iridium, and osmium concentrations far exceeding anything found in Sol's asteroid belt. Landing here means racing the terminator in a heat-shielded rover, mining in the brief twilight windows where temperatures are merely brutal rather than lethal.

**Ac — "Phosphor"** (Venus-analog, thick CO₂ atmosphere)
- Orbital: 0.72 AU | Ecc: 0.03 | Period: 198 days
- Physical: 1.85 M⊕ | Radius: 1.18 R⊕ | Gravity: 1.33 g | Temp: 510 K (surface ~740 K with greenhouse)
- Atmosphere: 85 atm — CO₂ 96%, N₂ 3.2%, SO₂ 0.015%, trace H₂O | Scale height: 14.8 km
- Habitability: 4/100
- Science: 75
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Atmospheric CO₂, Rare Earth Elements, Uranium Ore
- Solar Power: 2.93×
> Alpha Centauri's Venus is larger and angrier than ours. A crushing blanket of carbon dioxide wraps the planet in perpetual amber twilight, the sky a sickly yellow-orange seen through the sulfuric acid cloud decks at 50-70 km altitude. Surface pressure would flatten an unshielded habitat in seconds. Volcanic plains stretch to every horizon, studded with shield volcanoes taller than Olympus Mons. The upper atmosphere, however, is surprisingly mild — cloud-top habitats at 55 km altitude would experience 0.5 atm, 300 K, and gentle breezes. Phosphor is a terraforming dream and an engineering nightmare, a world that dares you to try.

**Ad — "New Cascadia"** (Temperate super-Earth in habitable zone)
- Orbital: 1.12 AU | Ecc: 0.04 | Period: 388 days
- Physical: 2.8 M⊕ | Radius: 1.32 R⊕ | Gravity: 1.61 g | Temp: 275 K
- Atmosphere: 1.6 atm — N₂ 72%, CO₂ 18%, Ar 5%, H₂O 4%, O₂ 0.8% | Scale height: 6.9 km
- Habitability: 62/100
- Science: 200
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Water (shallow seas), Atmospheric CO₂, Rare Earth Elements, Uranium Ore
- Solar Power: 1.21×
> The jewel of Alpha Centauri A. A super-Earth sitting squarely in the habitable zone of a solar twin, New Cascadia is tantalizingly close to habitable but not quite there. The thick CO₂-rich atmosphere creates a moderate greenhouse effect, keeping surface temperatures between 265-295 K depending on latitude. Liquid water exists — vast shallow seas cover 40% of the surface, stained rust-brown by dissolved iron minerals. The atmosphere is not breathable; that 18% CO₂ would kill an unprotected human in minutes. But the pressure is manageable, the gravity merely heavy, and the water is real. With atmospheric processing to crack CO₂ into oxygen and carbon feedstock, this world could become the first true interstellar colony. No native life has been detected despite exhaustive surveys — the oxygen present is photochemical, not biological. The sky is a pale peach color under the yellow-white sun that looks almost, but not quite, like home.

> **Moon: "Patera"** (Impact-formed rocky moon, of New Cascadia)
> - Orbital: 60,000 km from New Cascadia | Period: 1.8 days
> - Physical: 0.008 M⊕ | Radius: 0.22 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.17 g | Temp: 265 K
> - Atmosphere: 0.0003 atm — CO₂, trace SO₂ | Scale height: 12 km
> - Habitability: 3/100
> - Science: 25
> - Resources: Regolith, Uranium Ore
> - Solar Power: 1.21×
> > A modest but geologically interesting moon formed by a giant impact early in New Cascadia's history — the same mechanism that created Earth's Moon. Patera retains a wisp of atmosphere from ongoing volcanic outgassing, thin enough to be invisible but thick enough to detect spectroscopically. The surface shows ancient magma seas long since solidified, with younger volcanic provinces clustered along fracture zones where tidal heating from New Cascadia maintains a warm interior. From New Cascadia's surface, Patera appears roughly twice the angular diameter of Earth's Moon — a constant companion in the peach-tinted sky. The moon's formation likely stripped volatiles from the young New Cascadia, contributing to the planet's relatively thin atmosphere and low ocean coverage.

**Ae — "Aridane"** (Mars-analog, thin atmosphere)
- Orbital: 1.82 AU | Ecc: 0.07 | Period: 808 days
- Physical: 0.52 M⊕ | Radius: 0.83 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.75 g | Temp: 206 K
- Atmosphere: 0.012 atm — CO₂ 91%, N₂ 5%, Ar 3% | Scale height: 10.2 km
- Habitability: 35/100
- Science: 75
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Water (subsurface ice), Atmospheric CO₂, Rare Earth Elements
- Solar Power: 0.46×
> A cold, dry world with a wispy CO₂ atmosphere and rust-red regolith — Mars transposed to another star. Aridane is slightly larger than Mars and retains a weak magnetic field from a still-cooling core, giving it marginally better radiation protection. Ancient river channels scar the northern hemisphere, evidence of a warmer, wetter past. Subsurface ice deposits are extensive. The thin atmosphere allows spectacular views of Alpha Centauri B as a brilliant evening star, and on clear nights, Proxima is visible as a faint red point. A natural staging base for outer system operations, with enough gravity to stay healthy and enough resources to sustain a colony.

**Af — "Boreas"** (Cold Neptune-class ice giant)
- Orbital: 3.6 AU | Ecc: 0.12 | Period: 2,248 days
- Physical: 14.2 M⊕ (0.045 M_J) | Radius: 3.4 R⊕ | Temp: 118 K (cloud-top)
- Atmosphere: Hydrogen-helium envelope, methane ice clouds | Scale height: 28 km
- Habitability: 0/100 (no solid surface)
- Science: 0
- Resources: Gas Giant Atmosphere
- Solar Power: 0.12×
> A blue-green ice giant at the outer edge of A's stable planetary zone, beyond which the gravitational influence of Alpha Centauri B begins to perturb orbits. Boreas is a mini-Neptune: a thick hydrogen-helium atmosphere overlying a mantle of water, ammonia, and methane ices compressed into exotic high-pressure phases. Methane in the upper atmosphere gives it a vivid teal color. Wind speeds reach 400 m/s in the equatorial bands. No moons of significant size have been detected — B's perturbations likely stripped them long ago. Boreas serves primarily as a fuel depot: atmospheric skimming for hydrogen and helium-3.

#### Planets of Alpha Centauri B

**Bb — "Cinderfall"** (Lava world)
- Orbital: 0.15 AU | Ecc: 0.02 | Period: 18.4 days
- Physical: 3.2 M⊕ | Radius: 1.38 R⊕ | Gravity: 1.68 g | Temp: 1,050 K
- Atmosphere: 0.003 atm — Na, K, SiO vapor | Scale height: 42 km
- Habitability: 2/100
- Science: 50
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Lithium Ore, Rare Earth Elements, Uranium Ore
- Solar Power: 22.22×
> A nightmare world. Cinderfall orbits so close to Alpha Centauri B that its dayside is a permanent ocean of magma, glowing sullen orange-red visible from orbit. The tenuous atmosphere is rock vapor — sodium, potassium, and silicon monoxide — that condenses on the nightside as a constant rain of microscopic glass and mineral crystals. Tidal forces keep the interior churning, and the planet radiates more heat than it receives. The terminator zone, where magma solidifies into fresh black basalt, is a landscape of constant geological violence: fountains of lava, collapsing crust, and sulfurous venting. Scientifically fascinating. Operationally, stay in orbit.

**Bc — "Tephra"** (Warm rocky world)
- Orbital: 0.40 AU | Ecc: 0.05 | Period: 78 days
- Physical: 1.15 M⊕ | Radius: 1.04 R⊕ | Gravity: 1.06 g | Temp: 395 K
- Atmosphere: 0.35 atm — CO₂ 78%, N₂ 15%, SO₂ 4%, H₂O 2% | Scale height: 9.8 km
- Habitability: 18/100
- Science: 45
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Water (polar permafrost), Atmospheric CO₂, Rare Earth Elements, Uranium Ore
- Solar Power: 3.13×
> Too hot for liquid water on the surface, but not by much. Tephra sits in the Venus zone of Alpha Centauri B — a thick CO₂ atmosphere traps heat, pushing surface temperatures to 420 K. But unlike Phosphor around A, the atmosphere here is thinner and the greenhouse less extreme. Polar regions dip to 370 K. The terrain is dominated by volcanic plateaus and vast lava tube networks, some large enough to shelter pressurized habitats from the surface heat. Sulfur dioxide haze gives the sky a perpetual yellow cast. Underground water ice exists at the poles, locked in permafrost beneath the regolith. A challenging but not impossible outpost.

**Bd — "Glacia"** (Cold rocky world)
- Orbital: 0.70 AU | Ecc: 0.06 | Period: 181 days
- Physical: 0.65 M⊕ | Radius: 0.88 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.84 g | Temp: 238 K
- Atmosphere: 0.08 atm — CO₂ 60%, N₂ 30%, Ar 8% | Scale height: 9.1 km
- Habitability: 32/100
- Science: 45
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Water (polar ice caps), Atmospheric CO₂, Rare Earth Elements
- Solar Power: 1.02×
> On the outer edge of B's habitable zone, Glacia is a cold, dry world with a thin CO₂ atmosphere and extensive polar ice caps covering 35% of the surface. Mean temperature hovers around 238 K, but equatorial noon reaches 260 K — cold but workable. The landscape is ancient: heavily cratered highlands and smooth frozen plains. Glacia's main attraction is stability. Enough atmosphere for aerobraking, enough ice for water, enough gravity for long-term habitation, and a location far enough from B that Alpha Centauri A appears as a brilliant second sun, providing measurably extra warmth and light during parts of its 80-year binary orbit. When A swings closest, Glacia's equatorial temperatures briefly touch 270 K — almost warm enough for transient liquid water.

#### Planets of Proxima Centauri

**Proxima d — "Cinder"** (Hot sub-Earth)
- Orbital: 0.029 AU | Ecc: 0.04 | Period: 5.1 days
- Physical: 0.36 M⊕ | Radius: 0.73 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.68 g | Temp: 360 K
- Atmosphere: None (stripped by stellar activity)
- Habitability: 5/100
- Science: 25
- Resources: Regolith
- Solar Power: 2.02×
> The innermost known world of Proxima Centauri. Tidally locked, with one face eternally staring into the angry red eye of its star. Proxima is a violent flare star, and Cinder bears the scars: its surface is irradiated, its atmosphere long since blasted away by coronal mass ejections. The dayside is a dark, radiation-baked plain. The nightside is perpetually frozen. Only the terminator ring offers any respite — a narrow band where temperatures hover around 280 K, but without atmospheric protection, radiation levels are lethal within hours. A small, harsh world useful primarily as a gravity anchor for orbital infrastructure.

**Proxima b — "Ferrus"** (Temperate rocky — LIFE WORLD)
- Orbital: 0.049 AU | Ecc: 0.02 | Period: 11.2 days
- Physical: 1.07 M⊕ (minimum; likely ~1.4 M⊕) | Radius: ~1.08 R⊕ | Gravity: ~1.07 g | Temp: 234 K (equilibrium); surface varies 180-310 K
- Atmosphere: 0.7 atm — N₂ 68%, CO₂ 22%, H₂O 5%, Ar 3%, H₂ 0.12%, trace CH₄ | Scale height: 7.6 km
- Habitability: 35/100
- Science: 300
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Water (subsurface aquifers), Atmospheric CO₂, Rare Earth Elements, Uranium Ore
- Solar Power: 0.71×
> The closest exoplanet to Sol, and the site of the most consequential discovery in human history: alien life.
>
> Ferrus is tidally locked to Proxima Centauri, presenting one hemisphere to its red dwarf star in perpetuity. The substellar point is a warm, windy region where temperatures reach 310 K and liquid water pools in shallow, iron-stained basins. The antistellar hemisphere is locked in permanent night, a frozen wasteland at 180 K. Between them lies the terminator — a ring of twilight where the real action happens.
>
> The atmosphere is thick enough to redistribute some heat, driving powerful Hadley-cell winds from the dayside to the nightside, but not thick enough to fully equilibrate temperatures. The 0.12% molecular hydrogen concentration was the first clue. No known geological process on a world this size produces persistent atmospheric H₂ at that concentration — it should be lost to space within millennia. Something is replenishing it.
>
> That something is life.
>
> **Ferric Lithotrophs** — single-celled organisms roughly 2 micrometers in diameter, coated in iron oxide crusts that give them a rust-red coloration. They thrive in subsurface aquifers where geothermally heated water percolates through basaltic rock. Their metabolism is chemolithoautotrophic: they oxidize dissolved ferrous iron (Fe2+) to ferric iron (Fe3+), extracting energy through iron-sulfur cluster complexes that serve the role ATP plays in terrestrial biochemistry. They require no organic carbon, no sunlight, no oxygen. Their metabolic byproduct is molecular hydrogen, which seeps upward through fractured rock and enters the atmosphere — creating the anomalous H₂ signal.
>
> On the surface, their presence is visible. Near geothermal vents along the terminator, where warm water seeps through cracks in the basalt, vivid rust-red biofilms coat the rock faces. These mats of iron-encrusted cells can extend for hundreds of meters along water seepage lines, painting the dark basalt in streaks of ochre and vermillion. They are most concentrated in a region informally called the Rust Vents — a 200-km stretch of the southern terminator where tectonic fractures allow particularly vigorous water flow.
>
> Ferrus is not habitable in the human sense. The atmosphere is unbreathable (22% CO₂ is rapidly lethal), radiation from Proxima's flares is a constant hazard on the dayside, and the terminator weather is violent — 80 m/s winds are common where the temperature gradient is steepest. But a pressurized colony on the terminator, shielded from flares by terrain or magnetic deflectors, would have access to liquid water, moderate gravity, and the most extraordinary biology in the known universe. This is where xenobiology begins.

**Proxima c** (Cold mini-Neptune, unconfirmed)
- Orbital: 1.49 AU | Ecc: 0.04 | Period: 1,928 days
- Physical: ~7 M⊕ | Radius: ~2.4 R⊕ | Temp: 30 K
- Atmosphere: Hydrogen-helium envelope over ice mantle | Scale height: ~35 km
- Habitability: 0/100 (no solid surface accessible)
- Science: 0
- Resources: Gas Giant Atmosphere
- Solar Power: 0.00×
> A cold, distant world at the outer edge of Proxima's system. If it exists — and the evidence remains disputed — Proxima c is a mini-Neptune: a rocky-icy core wrapped in a puffy hydrogen-helium atmosphere. At 1.49 AU from a star producing only 0.17% of Sol's luminosity, this world receives almost no heat. Cloud-top temperatures hover near 30 K. It is a dark, frigid gas ball, barely illuminated by its dim red parent star. Its primary value is as a gravitational signpost — its orbit constrains the dynamics of the inner system — and as a potential source of volatiles for deep-space refueling.

**Proxima e — "Niflheim"** (Outer icy dwarf)
- Orbital: 3.8 AU | Ecc: 0.15 | Period: 12,400 days
- Physical: 0.08 M⊕ | Radius: 0.45 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.39 g | Temp: 18 K
- Atmosphere: None (trace N₂ frost sublimation)
- Habitability: 1/100
- Science: 20
- Resources: Regolith, Water (water ice)
- Solar Power: 0.00×
> A Pluto-class ice dwarf in the frozen outer reaches of the Proxima system. Niflheim is a ball of water ice, nitrogen ice, and silicate rock, its surface a cracked and cratered expanse of dirty gray ice at 18 K. At this distance from Proxima, the star appears as little more than a bright red point — barely brighter than Alpha Centauri A and B appear from here as a close, brilliant double star. Niflheim is geologically dead, but its ice is useful: water for propellant, nitrogen for atmosphere. A waypoint, not a destination.

### 2. Barnard's Star
- **Distance:** 5.96 ly
- **Galactic Position:** (26.9, -20987.0) ly
- **Galactic Velocity:** (269.0, -3.4) km/s
- **Galactic Orbit:** SMA: 26,157.4 ly | Ecc: 0.198 | ω: 274.9° | M₀: 356.9° | Period: 223.8 Myr
- **Spectral Type:** M4V
- **Stars:**
  - Barnard's Star: Mass 0.16 M☉ | Radius 0.19 R☉ | Luminosity 0.0035 L☉

The second nearest star system. An ancient red dwarf (10+ billion years old) with the highest proper motion of any known star — it visibly shifts against the background stars over a human lifetime. Its planetary system is remarkably compact: four confirmed sub-Earths huddle within 0.05 AU, all too hot for liquid water.

#### Planets

**b — "Ashling"** (Hot sub-Earth)
- Orbital: 0.0229 AU | Ecc: 0.03 | Period: 2.34 days
- Physical: 0.32 M⊕ | Radius: 0.70 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.65 g | Temp: 475 K
- Atmosphere: None (stripped)
- Habitability: 3/100
- Science: 25
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Rare Earth Elements
- Solar Power: 6.67×
> The innermost of Barnard's compact quartet. Tidally locked, airless, and baked. The dayside glows faintly in thermal infrared — dark basaltic plains heated to forge-like temperatures. The nightside is a frozen vacuum. Ashling completes an orbit every 56 hours, so close to its star that tidal forces have circularized its orbit to near-perfection. Mineral surveys show elevated concentrations of rare earth elements in the crust, making it a potential mining target for automated extraction — no human would want to stay long.

**c** (Hot sub-Earth)
- Orbital: 0.0296 AU | Ecc: 0.02 | Period: 3.15 days
- Physical: 0.19 M⊕ | Radius: 0.60 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.53 g | Temp: 430 K
- Atmosphere: None
- Habitability: 2/100
- Science: 25
- Resources: Regolith
- Solar Power: 3.99×
> The smallest of the four, barely twice the mass of the Moon. A dark, cratered world with no geological activity and no atmosphere. Its low gravity makes landing and departure trivially easy, but there is little reason to visit — Barnard c is a dead rock baking in the dim heat of its ancient star.

**d** (Warm sub-Earth)
- Orbital: 0.0382 AU | Ecc: 0.04 | Period: 4.12 days
- Physical: 0.24 M⊕ | Radius: 0.64 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.59 g | Temp: 380 K
- Atmosphere: None
- Habitability: 3/100
- Science: 35
- Resources: Regolith, Water (nightside crater ice)
- Solar Power: 2.40×
> Slightly cooler than its inner siblings but still well above water's boiling point. Barnard d shows evidence of ancient volcanism — lava tubes and collapsed calderas pockmark its equatorial region. The nightside hosts deposits of water ice in permanently shadowed craters, a resource worth tapping for any vessel passing through.

**e** (Warm sub-Earth)
- Orbital: 0.0476 AU | Ecc: 0.03 | Period: 6.74 days
- Physical: 0.34 M⊕ | Radius: 0.72 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.66 g | Temp: 340 K
- Atmosphere: Trace — 0.001 atm, CO₂ and SO₂
- Habitability: 6/100
- Science: 35
- Resources: Regolith
- Solar Power: 1.54×
> The outermost and most interesting of the confirmed quartet. Barnard e retains a wisp of atmosphere — not enough to matter for habitation, but enough to generate faint dust storms that redistribute surface material. Temperature at the substellar point is 340 K (67 degrees C), uncomfortably hot but not hellish. The terminator band averages 290 K — tantalizingly close to room temperature. If this world were just slightly farther out, or slightly more massive, it might have held onto a real atmosphere. As it stands, it is the best of a disappointing lot.

**f — "Hrimfaxi"** (Cold mini-Neptune)
- Orbital: 0.17 AU | Ecc: 0.10 | Period: 45 days
- Physical: 5.8 M⊕ | Radius: 2.1 R⊕ | Temp: 95 K (cloud-top)
- Atmosphere: Hydrogen-helium with methane and ammonia ice clouds | Scale height: 22 km
- Habitability: 0/100 (no solid surface)
- Science: 0
- Resources: Gas Giant Atmosphere
- Solar Power: 0.12×
> Beyond the compact inner system, a gap, and then Hrimfaxi — a cold mini-Neptune that likely migrated inward from a more distant formation zone. Its atmosphere is a murky mix of hydrogen, helium, and hydrocarbon hazes, giving it a dull brownish-gray appearance. Cloud-top temperatures of 95 K mean methane condenses into wispy cirrus clouds. Hrimfaxi is useful for atmospheric harvesting: hydrogen and helium-3 for fusion fuel.

**g — "Isa"** (Ice dwarf)
- Orbital: 0.45 AU | Ecc: 0.12 | Period: 198 days
- Physical: 0.06 M⊕ | Radius: 0.40 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.38 g | Temp: 42 K
- Atmosphere: None
- Habitability: 1/100
- Science: 20
- Resources: Regolith, Water (water ice)
- Solar Power: 0.02×
> A frozen pebble at the outer edge of Barnard's system. Isa is a Ceres-class body: water ice, silicate rock, and perpetual cold. Its surface is ancient and heavily cratered, dusted with dark carbonaceous material. At 42 K, even CO₂ is frozen solid. A fuel depot at best, a navigation waypoint at worst.

### 3. Wolf 359
- **Distance:** 7.86 ly
- **Galactic Position:** (16.7, -20995.5) ly
- **Galactic Velocity:** (209.4, 5.0) km/s
- **Galactic Orbit:** SMA: 20,129.9 ly | Ecc: 0.049 | ω: 116.8° | M₀: 150.7° | Period: 173.4 Myr
- **Spectral Type:** M6.5Ve (extreme flare star)
- **Stars:**
  - Wolf 359: Mass 0.09 M☉ | Radius 0.11 R☉ | Luminosity 0.0014 L☉

One of the dimmest and smallest stars near Sol — and one of the most violent. Wolf 359 is an extreme flare star that can double its brightness in minutes during outbursts. Superflares occur roughly monthly, bathing the entire inner system in hard UV and X-rays. Any planet here is a radiation-blasted wasteland.

#### Planets

**b — "Flashburn"** (Hot tidally locked rocky)
- Orbital: 0.02 AU | Ecc: 0.01 | Period: 2.8 days
- Physical: 0.55 M⊕ | Radius: 0.84 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.78 g | Temp: 415 K
- Atmosphere: None (repeatedly stripped by flares)
- Habitability: 2/100
- Science: 30
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Rare Earth Elements
- Solar Power: 3.50×
> Flashburn exists in a state of perpetual ablation. Every major flare from Wolf 359 scours the dayside with energetic protons, sputtering surface atoms into space. Over billions of years, this has stripped any atmosphere and eroded the upper crust. The surface is a glassy, radiation-darkened wasteland — partly melted and re-solidified with each major flare event. Magnetic field measurements suggest a molten iron core still churns, but no magnetosphere could withstand flares this powerful. Flashburn is a laboratory for studying extreme space weathering, nothing more.

**c — "Penumbra"** (Cold rocky)
- Orbital: 0.06 AU | Ecc: 0.05 | Period: 14.5 days
- Physical: 0.72 M⊕ | Radius: 0.91 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.87 g | Temp: 210 K
- Atmosphere: 0.004 atm — CO₂, trace SO₂ | Scale height: 11.5 km
- Habitability: 10/100
- Science: 35
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Water (polar ice), Rare Earth Elements, Uranium Ore
- Solar Power: 0.39×
> Far enough from Wolf 359 to be cold, but not far enough to escape the flares. Penumbra retains a wisp of atmosphere that is periodically blown off during superflares, then slowly rebuilt by volcanic outgassing. This cycle of destruction and renewal has left the surface covered in layers of volcanic ash alternating with radiation-glazed rock — a geological record of the star's tantrums. Subsurface lava tubes offer radiation shelter, and ice exists at the poles. The most viable base in the system, though "viable" is generous.

**d — "Rimescar"** (Ice dwarf)
- Orbital: 0.15 AU | Ecc: 0.08 | Period: 57 days
- Physical: 0.12 M⊕ | Radius: 0.51 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.46 g | Temp: 108 K
- Atmosphere: None
- Habitability: 1/100
- Science: 20
- Resources: Regolith, Water (water ice)
- Solar Power: 0.06×
> A small, frozen body at the outer edge of Wolf 359's influence. Rimescar is mostly water ice and rock, its surface cratered and ancient. Flare radiation still reaches here, though attenuated — surface radiation levels are merely dangerous rather than instantly lethal. The ice is useful for water and propellant, but there are easier places to get it.

### 4. Lalande 21185
- **Distance:** 8.31 ly
- **Galactic Position:** (22.7, -21000.3) ly
- **Galactic Velocity:** (225.0, 17.6) km/s
- **Galactic Orbit:** SMA: 21,628.2 ly | Ecc: 0.082 | ω: 196.2° | M₀: 64.9° | Period: 185.9 Myr
- **Spectral Type:** M2V
- **Stars:**
  - Lalande 21185: Mass 0.39 M☉ | Radius 0.39 R☉ | Luminosity 0.021 L☉

A relatively bright M-dwarf — calm compared to Wolf 359 or Proxima, with infrequent flares and a stable luminosity. One confirmed super-Earth orbits close in, with room for more planets in the habitable zone.

#### Planets

**b — "Crucible"** (Hot super-Earth, confirmed)
- Orbital: 0.079 AU | Ecc: 0.03 | Period: 12.9 days
- Physical: 2.7 M⊕ | Radius: 1.28 R⊕ | Gravity: 1.65 g | Temp: 580 K
- Atmosphere: 0.15 atm — CO₂ 85%, SO₂ 8%, trace metals | Scale height: 8.2 km
- Habitability: 6/100
- Science: 35
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Lithium Ore, Rare Earth Elements, Uranium Ore
- Solar Power: 3.36×
> A dense, hot super-Earth baking in the inner system. Crucible's surface is a volcanic hellscape — the high gravity and proximity to its star drive intense tidal heating that keeps the mantle partially molten. The thin atmosphere is a toxic soup of carbon dioxide and sulfur dioxide, maintained by relentless volcanic outgassing. Surface features are dominated by vast shield volcanoes and lava plains. Despite the hostility, Crucible is mineral-rich: concentrated deposits of platinum-group metals and rare earths in volcanic intrusions make it a valuable automated mining target.

**c — "Thalassa"** (Rocky world in habitable zone)
- Orbital: 0.13 AU | Ecc: 0.05 | Period: 28.6 days
- Physical: 1.6 M⊕ | Radius: 1.13 R⊕ | Gravity: 1.25 g | Temp: 285 K
- Atmosphere: 0.45 atm — N₂ 58%, CO₂ 32%, H₂O 7%, Ar 2% | Scale height: 7.8 km
- Habitability: 48/100
- Science: 120
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Water (equatorial basins), Atmospheric CO₂, Rare Earth Elements, Uranium Ore
- Solar Power: 1.24×
> The most promising world in the Lalande system. Thalassa sits near the inner edge of the habitable zone, and conditions are borderline: surface temperature averages 295 K with greenhouse warming, and liquid water exists in equatorial basins. But that 32% CO₂ makes the atmosphere unbreathable, and the heavy carbon dioxide load creates acid rain that etches exposed rock. No life has been detected. The oceans — such as they are — are shallow, warm, and loaded with dissolved minerals, giving them a greenish-brown hue. With atmospheric processing, Thalassa could become habitable within decades. A serious colonization target.

**d — "Mistral"** (Mini ice giant)
- Orbital: 0.50 AU | Ecc: 0.08 | Period: 213 days
- Physical: 8.5 M⊕ | Radius: 2.6 R⊕ | Temp: 112 K (cloud-top)
- Atmosphere: Hydrogen-helium, ammonia ice clouds | Scale height: 19 km
- Habitability: 0/100 (no solid surface)
- Science: 0
- Resources: Gas Giant Atmosphere
- Solar Power: 0.08×
> A small ice giant in the cold outer system. Mistral is enveloped in pale ammonia clouds, giving it a creamy white appearance streaked with tan bands. Its small size makes atmospheric skimming challenging — the gravity well is shallow, but so is the atmosphere. Useful primarily as a helium-3 source for fusion operations.

**e** (Cold outer body, long-period candidate)
- Orbital: 2.5 AU | Ecc: 0.20 | Period: ~3,650 days
- Physical: ~4 M⊕ | Radius: ~1.5 R⊕ | Temp: 55 K
- Atmosphere: Trace — thin N₂, CH₄ frost
- Habitability: 2/100
- Science: 30
- Resources: Regolith, Hydrocarbons (CH₄ frost)
- Solar Power: 0.00×
> A cold super-Earth or sub-Neptune in the outer system, detected as a long-period radial velocity signal. At 2.5 AU from a dim M-dwarf, this world receives negligible warmth. Its surface — if solid — is locked under nitrogen and methane ices. A deep-freeze world with limited strategic value.

### 5. Sirius
- **Distance:** 8.60 ly
- **Galactic Position:** (17.5, -20997.9) ly
- **Galactic Velocity:** (204.4, -11.0) km/s
- **Galactic Orbit:** SMA: 19,713.2 ly | Ecc: 0.085 | ω: 53.2° | M₀: 223.0° | Period: 170.0 Myr
- **Spectral Type:** A1V + DA2 (white dwarf)
- **Stars:**
  - Sirius A: Mass 2.06 M☉ | Radius 1.71 R☉ | Luminosity 25.4 L☉
  - Sirius B: Mass 1.02 M☉ | Radius 0.008 R☉ (white dwarf, ~25,200 K surface)

The brightest star in Earth's sky, and a system of extremes. Sirius A is a hot, luminous A-type star that pushes the habitable zone out beyond 4 AU. Sirius B is a white dwarf — the collapsed remnant of a star that was once even more massive — orbiting at 8-31 AU. The white dwarf's past as a red giant likely sterilized the inner system billions of years ago, stripping atmospheres and rearranging orbits. What remains is a stark, radiation-intense environment.

#### Planets

**b — "Pyrrhus"** (Hot gas giant)
- Orbital: 2.0 AU | Ecc: 0.15 | Period: 520 days
- Physical: 1.8 M_J (572 M⊕) | Radius: 1.25 R_J | Temp: 520 K (cloud-top)
- Atmosphere: Hydrogen-helium, silicate clouds, Na/K absorption | Scale height: 45 km
- Habitability: 0/100 (no solid surface)
- Science: 0
- Resources: Gas Giant Atmosphere
- Solar Power: 6.35×
> A hot Jupiter bathed in the blue-white glare of Sirius A. Pyrrhus orbits where Mars would be in our system, but Sirius A's ferocious luminosity heats its cloud tops to 520 K. The upper atmosphere is laced with silicate condensates — clouds of vaporized rock — giving the planet a bronze-gold sheen. Sodium and potassium absorption darkens the visible spectrum. No stable moons survive this close to Sirius A; the intense radiation pressure and the gravitational perturbations of Sirius B's elliptical orbit have cleared them. Pyrrhus is a fuel reservoir: vast quantities of hydrogen and helium, but the radiation environment makes skimming operations punishing on equipment.

**c — "Scoria"** (Massive atmosphereless rocky world)
- Orbital: 5.0 AU | Ecc: 0.10 | Period: 2,050 days
- Physical: 8.2 M⊕ | Radius: 1.75 R⊕ | Gravity: 2.68 g | Temp: 280 K
- Atmosphere: None (stripped by Sirius B's red giant phase and A's UV flux)
- Habitability: 8/100
- Science: 30
- Resources: Metal Ore (rich, nickel-iron), Regolith, Rare Earth Elements
- Solar Power: 1.02×
> An anomaly: a massive rocky planet with no atmosphere whatsoever. Scoria was likely a super-Earth with a substantial envelope before Sirius B's red giant phase boiled it away billions of years ago. What remains is a dense, iron-rich core world with crushing gravity — 2.68 g at the surface. The landscape is extraordinary: vast metallic plains of exposed nickel-iron interspersed with silicate mountain ranges, the whole surface polished by eons of micrometeorite bombardment into a dull mirror sheen. Under Sirius A's brilliant blue-white light, the surface gleams. Standing on Scoria, you would weigh 2.7 times your Earth weight and look up at the brightest star you have ever seen, with the white-hot pinpoint of Sirius B visible as a second star. Mining here yields the highest-grade iron and nickel in the zone — but the gravity makes getting it off-world expensive.

**d — "Hyperion"** (Cold ice giant)
- Orbital: 12.0 AU | Ecc: 0.08 | Period: 7,650 days
- Physical: 22 M⊕ (0.069 M_J) | Radius: 3.8 R⊕ | Temp: 145 K (cloud-top)
- Atmosphere: Hydrogen-helium, methane, ammonia | Scale height: 30 km
- Habitability: 0/100 (no solid surface)
- Science: 0
- Resources: Gas Giant Atmosphere
- Solar Power: 0.18×
> A blue-tinted ice giant in the outer reaches of the Sirius system, occupying the dynamically stable zone beyond Sirius B's maximum orbital distance. Hyperion's orbit is mildly perturbed by the white dwarf's gravitational influence, causing slow oscillations in eccentricity over millennia. The atmosphere shows pronounced methane absorption, giving it a deeper blue than Uranus. Wind speeds in the equatorial jet exceed 500 m/s. A standard helium-3 harvesting target, with the bonus of relative calm compared to the radiation-blasted inner system.

> **Moon: "Frostcap"** (Tiny captured irregular, of Hyperion)
> - Orbital: 300,000 km from Hyperion | Period: 3.0 days
> - Physical: 0.001 M⊕ | Radius: 0.08 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.16 g | Temp: 80 K
> - Atmosphere: None
> - Habitability: 0/100
> - Science: 15
> - Resources: Regolith
> - Solar Power: 0.18×
> > A small, dark body on an inclined orbit — almost certainly captured from the Sirius system's depleted outer debris population. Frostcap's surface is matte charcoal: carbonaceous material with a geometric albedo below 0.04, making it nearly invisible against space. The shape is irregular, roughly 500 km along its longest axis, with a 60 km impact basin on the trailing hemisphere that exposes brighter subsurface ice beneath the carbon-rich regolith. A fossil remnant of whatever Kuiper Belt analog Sirius once possessed before the white dwarf's progenitor blew most of it away.

### 6. Luyten 726-8 / UV Ceti
- **Distance:** 8.73 ly
- **Galactic Position:** (26.9, -21000.2) ly
- **Galactic Velocity:** (220.0, -3.5) km/s
- **Galactic Orbit:** SMA: 21,098.9 ly | Ecc: 0.018 | ω: 345.8° | M₀: 286.2° | Period: 181.5 Myr
- **Spectral Type:** M5.5Ve + M6Ve (both flare stars)
- **Stars:**
  - BL Ceti (A): Mass 0.10 M☉ | Radius 0.14 R☉ | Luminosity 0.0006 L☉
  - UV Ceti (B): Mass 0.10 M☉ | Radius 0.14 R☉ | Luminosity 0.0004 L☉

A close binary of two extreme flare stars separated by roughly 5 AU. UV Ceti (the B component) is the archetype of its class — capable of brightening by a factor of 75 in seconds during superflares. Together, these two tiny red dwarfs create a radiation environment that makes Wolf 359 look gentle.

#### Planets

**Ab — "Stormwatch"** (Hot irradiated rocky)
- Orbital: 0.02 AU from A | Ecc: 0.02 | Period: 3.1 days
- Physical: 0.40 M⊕ | Radius: 0.76 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.69 g | Temp: 350 K
- Atmosphere: None (flare-stripped)
- Habitability: 1/100
- Science: 30
- Resources: Regolith
- Solar Power: 1.50×
> A small, tidally locked rock orbiting BL Ceti so closely that UV Ceti's flares are visible as brilliant flashes lighting up the nightside sky. The surface is radiation-darkened to near-black, with a glassy texture from repeated flash-melting during superflares. The magnetic field is measurable but pathetically weak — not enough to deflect the constant barrage of energetic particles. Stormwatch is primarily a scientific curiosity: a laboratory for studying stellar-planetary magnetic interactions at extreme close range.

**Ac — "Vestige"** (Cold dwarf planet)
- Orbital: 0.30 AU from A | Ecc: 0.10 | Period: 93 days
- Physical: 0.05 M⊕ | Radius: 0.38 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.35 g | Temp: 55 K
- Atmosphere: None
- Habitability: 1/100
- Science: 20
- Resources: Regolith, Water (ice)
- Solar Power: 0.01×
> A tiny, frozen world at the edge of dynamical stability for the A component. At 0.3 AU, Vestige is perturbed by B's gravity during close passages of the binary orbit, giving it a slowly precessing, mildly eccentric path. The surface is ancient cratered ice and rock, unremarkable in every way. The only noteworthy feature is the view: two red dwarf stars, the nearer one occasionally flaring to rival the combined light of all the stars in the sky, with the farther one a steady red beacon. Vestige exists as proof that even the most inhospitable systems can hold onto small worlds.

### 7. Ross 154
- **Distance:** 9.69 ly
- **Galactic Position:** (25.4, -20982.5) ly
- **Galactic Velocity:** (197.9, -0.9) km/s
- **Galactic Orbit:** SMA: 19,123.7 ly | Ecc: 0.097 | ω: 86.9° | M₀: 183.8° | Period: 165.1 Myr
- **Spectral Type:** M3.5Ve
- **Stars:**
  - Ross 154: Mass 0.17 M☉ | Radius 0.20 R☉ | Luminosity 0.0038 L☉

A flare star, though less extreme than Wolf 359 or UV Ceti. Flares occur frequently but rarely reach the catastrophic intensities of the UV Ceti class. The system has no confirmed planets, but radial velocity surveys suggest the presence of small rocky worlds.

#### Planets

**b — "Kindling"** (Hot rocky)
- Orbital: 0.025 AU | Ecc: 0.02 | Period: 3.4 days
- Physical: 0.70 M⊕ | Radius: 0.90 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.86 g | Temp: 500 K
- Atmosphere: None
- Habitability: 3/100
- Science: 30
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Rare Earth Elements
- Solar Power: 6.08×
> Another tidally locked world baking in the inner zone of a flare star. Kindling is slightly more massive than its counterparts in other M-dwarf systems, and shows evidence of past volcanism: extinct shield volcanoes and collapsed lava tubes scar the equatorial region. The dayside is too hot for anything except radiation-hardened equipment. The nightside hosts frozen volatiles in crater shadows.

**c — "Thornfield"** (Warm super-Earth)
- Orbital: 0.05 AU | Ecc: 0.04 | Period: 9.6 days
- Physical: 2.1 M⊕ | Radius: 1.22 R⊕ | Gravity: 1.41 g | Temp: 340 K
- Atmosphere: 0.25 atm — CO₂ 70%, N₂ 20%, SO₂ 6%, H₂O 3% | Scale height: 7.4 km
- Habitability: 15/100
- Science: 50
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Atmospheric CO₂, Lithium Ore, Rare Earth Elements, Uranium Ore
- Solar Power: 1.52×
> The most substantial planet in the Ross 154 system. Thornfield has managed to retain a thin, toxic atmosphere despite the flare activity — its higher mass and moderate distance from the star give it just enough gravitational grip. The surface is warm, volcanic, and shrouded in a permanent sulfurous haze. Flares periodically strip the upper atmosphere, which rebuilds over months from volcanic outgassing in a perpetual cycle. The equatorial region shows active lava flows. Not a place to build a colony, but the CO₂-rich atmosphere and volcanic minerals could support a small, hardened industrial outpost.

**d — "Hollowstone"** (Cold rocky)
- Orbital: 0.12 AU | Ecc: 0.06 | Period: 35 days
- Physical: 0.45 M⊕ | Radius: 0.80 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.70 g | Temp: 178 K
- Atmosphere: 0.002 atm — CO₂ | Scale height: 11 km
- Habitability: 8/100
- Science: 30
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Water (polar ice), Rare Earth Elements
- Solar Power: 0.26×
> A cold, nearly airless world at the outer edge of the system. Hollowstone's thin CO₂ atmosphere barely qualifies as one — surface pressure is comparable to Mars. Water ice exists in the polar regions and in permanently shadowed craters. The surface is ancient and heavily cratered, with a curious feature: an extensive network of subsurface voids detected by seismic surveys, possibly drained magma chambers from a more volcanically active past. These natural caverns could shelter a subsurface habitat from flare radiation.

  - Ross 248: Mass 0.12 M☉ | Radius 0.16 R☉ | Luminosity 0.0018 L☉

A faint red dwarf notable for its high velocity — in approximately 33,000 years, Ross 248 will briefly become the closest star to Sol, passing within 3.0 light-years before receding again. A transient neighbor, visiting from the deep galaxy.

#### Planets

**b — "Lantern"** (Small hot rocky, tidally locked)
- Orbital: 0.02 AU | Ecc: 0.01 | Period: 2.6 days
- Physical: 0.30 M⊕ | Radius: 0.69 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.63 g | Temp: 420 K
- Atmosphere: None
- Habitability: 2/100
- Science: 30

- Solar Power: 4.50×
> A small, tidally locked rock hugging its star. Lantern's dayside reaches temperatures sufficient to soften certain minerals, while the nightside drops below 80 K. The terminator band is narrow and geologically fractured from the extreme thermal stress. An unremarkable world notable only for its parent star's peculiar trajectory through the galaxy — in the deep future, Lantern will be the closest known exoplanet to Sol.

**c — "Driftwood"** (Frozen dwarf)
- Orbital: 0.10 AU | Ecc: 0.08 | Period: 29 days
- Physical: 0.09 M⊕ | Radius: 0.46 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.42 g | Temp: 85 K
- Atmosphere: None
- Habitability: 1/100
- Science: 20

- Solar Power: 0.18×
> A frozen Ceres-class body in the outer system. Driftwood is a ball of water ice and carbonaceous rock, its surface dark and old. It is moving with Ross 248 through the galaxy at considerable velocity relative to Sol — this entire system is a ship passing in the cosmic night, and Driftwood is a cold passenger.

### 9. Epsilon Eridani (Ran)
- **Distance:** 10.50 ly
- **Galactic Position:** (20.9, -21002.2) ly
- **Galactic Velocity:** (199.6, 6.4) km/s
- **Galactic Orbit:** SMA: 19,294.0 ly | Ecc: 0.094 | ω: 107.6° | M₀: 159.0° | Period: 166.5 Myr
- **Spectral Type:** K2V
- **Stars:**
  - Epsilon Eridani (Ran): Mass 0.82 M☉ | Radius 0.74 R☉ | Luminosity 0.34 L☉

A young star — only 800 million years old — and it shows. Epsilon Eridani is surrounded by a prominent debris disk analogous to a young Kuiper Belt, with warm dust between 3-20 AU and a cold outer belt at 70 AU. This is a solar system still in its adolescence: planets are geologically hyperactive, surfaces are fresh, and everything is still settling down. The confirmed gas giant AEgir dominates the outer system, while inner rocky worlds seethe with volcanic fury.

#### Planets

**b — "Surtsey"** (Hot rocky)
- Orbital: 0.15 AU | Ecc: 0.05 | Period: 23 days
- Physical: 1.1 M⊕ | Radius: 1.03 R⊕ | Gravity: 1.04 g | Temp: 510 K
- Atmosphere: 0.06 atm — CO₂ 55%, SO₂ 25%, SiO 8%, trace metals | Scale height: 10.5 km
- Habitability: 4/100
- Science: 30
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Atmospheric CO₂, Lithium Ore, Rare Earth Elements, Uranium Ore
- Solar Power: 15.11×
> Named for Earth's volcanic island, Surtsey is a world in geological fury. At 800 million years old, its interior is still differentiating, its crust thin and constantly recycled by vigorous plate tectonics. The surface is 60% active lava fields. The atmosphere is volcanic exhaust — sulfur dioxide, carbon dioxide, and vaporized silicates — replenished faster than the star's UV can strip it. Landing here means landing on fresh rock that may not stay solid. From orbit, the nightside glows with a web of orange-red fracture lines — a world still being born.

**c — "Vulcanis"** (Volcanically active super-Earth)
- Orbital: 0.40 AU | Ecc: 0.06 | Period: 99 days
- Physical: 3.5 M⊕ | Radius: 1.42 R⊕ | Gravity: 1.74 g | Temp: 340 K
- Atmosphere: 2.2 atm — CO₂ 60%, N₂ 22%, H₂O 12%, SO₂ 4%, H₂S 1% | Scale height: 7.1 km
- Habitability: 14/100
- Science: 50
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Water (acidic liquid), Atmospheric CO₂, Lithium Ore, Rare Earth Elements, Uranium Ore
- Solar Power: 2.13×
> The most massive rocky world in the Ran system, and arguably the most geologically active planet in Zone 1. Vulcanis is wracked by continuous eruptions: its young, hot interior drives a convective mantle that recycles the crust every few hundred million years. The thick, steamy atmosphere is a pressure cooker of greenhouse gases, pushing surface temperatures well above the equilibrium value. Liquid water exists — but it is acidic, superheated under the crushing atmosphere, and laced with dissolved sulfur compounds. The landscape is a Hadean nightmare of lava lakes, sulfur-crusted fumaroles, and towering volcanic peaks jutting through the cloud layer. Vulcanis is what Earth looked like 4 billion years ago, preserved in amber. For geologists, it is the discovery of a lifetime.

**d — "Greenmantle"** (Mars-like, near habitable zone)
- Orbital: 0.60 AU | Ecc: 0.04 | Period: 182 days
- Physical: 0.75 M⊕ | Radius: 0.93 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.87 g | Temp: 264 K
- Atmosphere: 0.08 atm — CO₂ 65%, N₂ 25%, H₂O 5%, Ar 3% | Scale height: 9.0 km
- Habitability: 25/100
- Science: 70
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Water (intermittent briny pools), Atmospheric CO₂, Rare Earth Elements, Uranium Ore
- Solar Power: 0.94×
> Sitting in the habitable zone of a young K-type star, Greenmantle is what Mars might have been if it had kept its atmosphere a bit longer. The planet is cold but not frozen, with surface temperatures ranging from 230-290 K depending on latitude and season. Liquid water exists intermittently — briny pools form in equatorial lowlands during summer, fed by sublimating CO₂/water ice caps. The atmosphere is thin but real, enough for aerobraking and marginal wind erosion. The surface is young, with few impact craters — volcanic resurfacing dominates. In 800 million years, this world has not yet had time to develop the geological complexity of an older planet, but its trajectory is promising. Given another billion years and a bit more mass, Greenmantle could become genuinely habitable. For now, it is a tantalizing almost — a world on the cusp.

**e / AEgir** (Confirmed gas giant)
- Orbital: 3.53 AU | Ecc: 0.06 | Period: 2,688 days (7.4 years)
- Physical: 0.98 M_J (311 M⊕) | Radius: ~1.0 R_J | Temp: 110 K (cloud-top)
- Atmosphere: Hydrogen-helium, ammonia/water clouds, young and turbulent | Scale height: 27 km
- Habitability: 0/100 (no solid surface)
- Science: 0
- Resources: Gas Giant Atmosphere
- Solar Power: 0.03×
> The confirmed Jupiter-analog of the Ran system, and the gravitational anchor around which the outer system organizes. AEgir is a near-twin of Jupiter in mass, but younger and more turbulent: its cloud bands are broader and more chaotic, with enormous storm systems that form and dissipate over months rather than centuries. The Great Blue Spot — a vast anticyclonic storm in the southern hemisphere — has been observed for decades and shows no sign of abating. The atmosphere is slightly more metal-rich than Jupiter's, reflecting the system's youth and the debris disk that still feeds the outer system with dust and planetesimals.

> **Moon: "Surtur"** (Volcanic Io-analog, of AEgir)
> - Orbital: 421,000 km from AEgir | Period: 1.8 days
> - Physical: 0.015 M⊕ | Radius: 0.29 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.18 g | Temp: 130 K (surface avg; eruption sites 1,800+ K)
> - Atmosphere: 0.00001 atm — SO₂ frost sublimation
> - Habitability: 2/100
> - Science: 50
> - Resources: Regolith, Uranium Ore
> - Solar Power: 0.03×
> > If Io is volcanically active, Surtur is volcanically deranged. Tidal forces from AEgir, amplified by the young system's still-settling orbital resonances, pump enormous energy into Surtur's interior. The surface is a patchwork of sulfur flows, silicate lava lakes, and explosive volcanic plumes that fountain material hundreds of kilometers into space. Eruption temperatures exceed 1,800 K — hotter than anything on Io. The moon's surface is recoated every few thousand years. Sulfur dioxide frost sublimates on the dayside, creating a tenuous, transient atmosphere that freezes out on the nightside. Surtur contributes a plasma torus to AEgir's magnetosphere, a ring of ionized sulfur and oxygen that glows faintly in ultraviolet. Landing here is technically possible in the volcanic dead zones, but the ground may not stay dead for long.

> **Moon: "Ymir"** (Icy Ganymede-analog, of AEgir)
> - Orbital: 1,070,000 km from AEgir | Period: 7.2 days
> - Physical: 0.025 M⊕ | Radius: 0.41 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.15 g | Temp: 95 K
> - Atmosphere: 0.0001 atm — O₂ from ice radiolysis, trace H₂O | Scale height: 18 km
> - Habitability: 10/100
> - Science: 55
> - Resources: Regolith, Water (subsurface ocean)
> - Solar Power: 0.03×
> > A large icy moon with a differentiated interior: silicate core, ice mantle, and a suspected subsurface ocean at 80-150 km depth, maintained by tidal heating (less extreme than Surtur's, but steady). The surface is a mixture of ancient, heavily cratered dark terrain and younger, grooved bright terrain where internal geological processes have resurfaced the ice. A tenuous oxygen atmosphere — generated by radiation splitting surface ice — clings to the moon. Ymir is the best candidate for a permanent base in the Ran outer system: low radiation (shielded by AEgir's magnetosphere), accessible water ice, and enough tidal heat to potentially power geothermal systems. The subsurface ocean is a prime target for future exploration, though at 800 million years old, it is likely too young for indigenous life.

> **Moon: "Styx"** (Small captured irregular, of AEgir)
> - Orbital: 2,500,000 km from AEgir | Period: 18.0 days (retrograde)
> - Physical: 0.002 M⊕ | Radius: 0.11 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.17 g | Temp: 85 K
> - Atmosphere: None
> - Habitability: 0/100
> - Science: 15
> - Resources: Regolith
> - Solar Power: 0.03×
> > A small, dark, heavily battered body in a distant retrograde orbit — the signature of gravitational capture rather than co-formation. Styx was almost certainly a planetesimal from Ran's debris disk that wandered too close to AEgir and lost enough energy in a three-body interaction to become permanently bound. The surface is ancient and saturated with craters, a record of 800 million years of bombardment from the debris disk. The albedo is extremely low, the composition carbonaceous, and the shape irregular — a lumpy, roughly 700 km body rotating every 11 hours. Styx occupies a lonely region far beyond Ymir, where the boundary between moon and temporary capture blurs.

**f — "Fimbul"** (Cold ice giant)
- Orbital: 8.0 AU | Ecc: 0.10 | Period: 8,750 days
- Physical: 18 M⊕ (0.057 M_J) | Radius: 3.6 R⊕ | Temp: 72 K (cloud-top)
- Atmosphere: Hydrogen-helium, methane ice clouds | Scale height: 26 km
- Habitability: 0/100 (no solid surface)
- Science: 0
- Resources: Gas Giant Atmosphere
- Solar Power: 0.01×
> A Uranus-class ice giant in the cold outer system, orbiting within the warm dust belt of Ran's debris disk. Fimbul is slightly tilted (35 degree axial tilt) and rotates slowly, giving it a banded but asymmetric appearance. Methane absorption gives it a deep blue color. Occasional impacts from debris disk material produce bright clouds of condensate that persist for weeks before being sheared apart by zonal winds. A helium-3 harvesting target, with the bonus of studying ongoing planetary accretion in the debris disk.

> **Moon: "Rime"** (Young cratered ice moon, of Fimbul)
> - Orbital: 180,000 km from Fimbul | Period: 2.5 days
> - Physical: 0.003 M⊕ | Radius: 0.14 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.15 g | Temp: 60 K
> - Atmosphere: None
> - Habitability: 1/100
> - Science: 20
> - Resources: Regolith, Water (water ice)
> - Solar Power: 0.01×
> > A small, bright ice ball with one of the youngest-looking surfaces in the Ran system. Rime's face is a palimpsest of overlapping impact craters — some fresh and sharp-rimmed with bright ejecta rays, others partially buried by subsequent strikes. The debris disk that surrounds Ran at these distances actively bombards Rime, resurfacing it on timescales of tens of millions of years. Several craters expose clean, blue-white water ice beneath a thin regolith of dust-darkened frost. Geologically dead but paradoxically young, kept fresh by the rain of debris that defines this system's adolescence.

**g — "Frostmantle"** (Outer dwarf)
- Orbital: 18 AU | Ecc: 0.15 | Period: 29,500 days
- Physical: 0.10 M⊕ | Radius: 0.48 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.43 g | Temp: 38 K
- Atmosphere: None (trace N₂ frost)
- Habitability: 1/100
- Science: 20
- Resources: Regolith, Water (water ice), Hydrocarbons (methane ice)
- Solar Power: 0.00×
> A Pluto-class ice dwarf orbiting in the gap between the warm dust belt and the outer debris disk. Frostmantle's surface is ancient nitrogen and methane ice, tinted pink-brown by tholins — organic molecules generated by cosmic ray bombardment. At 38 K, everything is frozen solid. The world is notable primarily as a navigation waypoint and a sampling target for pristine primordial material from Ran's protoplanetary disk.

### 10. Lacaille 9352 (GJ 887)
- **Distance:** 10.74 ly
- **Galactic Position:** (23.8, -20981.3) ly
- **Galactic Velocity:** (207.4, -11.1) km/s
- **Galactic Orbit:** SMA: 19,963.4 ly | Ecc: 0.075 | ω: 46.1° | M₀: 230.1° | Period: 172.0 Myr
- **Spectral Type:** M2V
- **Stars:**
  - Lacaille 9352: Mass 0.49 M☉ | Radius 0.47 R☉ | Luminosity 0.033 L☉

One of the brightest nearby M-dwarfs, and remarkably quiet — very few flares detected. This stability makes its planets among the most hospitable in the M-dwarf category. Two confirmed super-Earths orbit close in.

#### Planets

**b** (Hot super-Earth, confirmed)
- Orbital: 0.068 AU | Ecc: 0.04 | Period: 9.3 days
- Physical: 4.2 M⊕ | Radius: 1.50 R⊕ | Gravity: 1.87 g | Temp: 468 K
- Atmosphere: 0.5 atm — CO₂ 72%, N₂ 18%, H₂O 6%, SO₂ 3% | Scale height: 7.2 km
- Habitability: 8/100
- Science: 35
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Atmospheric CO₂, Rare Earth Elements, Uranium Ore
- Solar Power: 7.14×
> A massive, dense super-Earth baking in the inner system. The high gravity gives it a thick, clinging atmosphere dominated by carbon dioxide, with enough water vapor to generate occasional acidic rain. Surface temperatures are well above water's boiling point at this pressure. The terrain is dominated by high-altitude plateaus and deep rift valleys carved by tectonic forces — the planet's thick lithosphere fractures rather than subducts. Mineral-rich but too hot for comfortable habitation.

**c — "Galena"** (Warm super-Earth, confirmed)
- Orbital: 0.12 AU | Ecc: 0.05 | Period: 21.8 days
- Physical: 7.6 M⊕ | Radius: 1.78 R⊕ | Gravity: 2.40 g | Temp: 330 K
- Atmosphere: 3.5 atm — CO₂ 48%, N₂ 35%, H₂O 10%, Ar 4%, SO₂ 2% | Scale height: 5.8 km
- Habitability: 12/100
- Science: 40
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Water (hot acidic rain), Atmospheric CO₂, Rare Earth Elements, Uranium Ore
- Solar Power: 2.29×
> The larger of the two confirmed planets, and a world that tests the definition of "rocky." At 7.6 Earth masses, Galena is near the boundary between super-Earth and mini-Neptune, but its density confirms a rocky-iron composition with a thick outgassed atmosphere. Surface gravity is crushing — 2.4 g, enough to make walking difficult and construction expensive. The atmosphere is thick and hot, with a powerful greenhouse effect raising surface temperatures to 380 K. Rain falls — sulfuric acid and hot water, eroding the basaltic surface into dramatic canyon systems. The name comes from the planet's unusually high lead sulfide (galena) content in surface rocks, giving exposed cliff faces a metallic gray gleam.

**d — "Halvard"** (Mini-Neptune)
- Orbital: 0.30 AU | Ecc: 0.07 | Period: 86 days
- Physical: 12 M⊕ | Radius: 2.9 R⊕ | Temp: 175 K (cloud-top)
- Atmosphere: Hydrogen-helium over water-ice mantle | Scale height: 20 km
- Habitability: 0/100 (no solid surface accessible)
- Science: 0
- Resources: Gas Giant Atmosphere
- Solar Power: 0.37×
> A mini-Neptune at the outer edge of the system. Halvard has a puffy hydrogen-helium envelope over a dense core of water ice and silicates. The atmosphere is bland — pale gray clouds of water ice over deeper layers of ammonia compounds. Its main value is as a fuel source and a gravitational anchor for outer system operations in the Lacaille system.

### 11. Ross 128
- **Distance:** 11.01 ly
- **Galactic Position:** (26.3, -20981.4) ly
- **Galactic Velocity:** (217.4, -7.8) km/s
- **Galactic Orbit:** SMA: 20,853.4 ly | Ecc: 0.038 | ω: 11.5° | M₀: 262.8° | Period: 179.4 Myr
- **Spectral Type:** M4V
- **Stars:**
  - Ross 128: Mass 0.17 M☉ | Radius 0.20 R☉ | Luminosity 0.0036 L☉

A quiet M-dwarf — one of the least active red dwarfs in the solar neighborhood. Unlike Proxima, Wolf 359, or UV Ceti, Ross 128 produces almost no detectable flares. This calm stellar environment makes its habitable-zone planet one of the best prospects for stable surface conditions in Zone 1 — and, as it turns out, for life.

#### Planets

**b — "Aurelian"** (Temperate rocky — LIFE WORLD)
- Orbital: 0.050 AU | Ecc: 0.12 | Period: 9.9 days
- Physical: 1.4 M⊕ (minimum; likely ~1.8 M⊕) | Radius: ~1.15 R⊕ | Gravity: ~1.18 g | Temp: 280 K (equilibrium); surface 260-310 K
- Atmosphere: 1.1 atm — N₂ 62%, CO₂ 25%, H₂O 8%, Ar 2.5%, H₂S 0.3%, OCS 0.08% | Scale height: 7.2 km
- Habitability: 40/100
- Science: 400
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Water (shallow dayside sea), Atmospheric CO₂, Rare Earth Elements, Uranium Ore
- Solar Power: 1.44×
> The second world in Zone 1 found to harbor life, and the most remarkable biological discovery in the catalog. Where Ferrus (Proxima b) hosts subsurface chemolithotrophs clinging to existence in rock crevices, Aurelian's biosphere is visible from orbit — and visible from the ground as forests.
>
> Aurelian is tidally locked, with one hemisphere permanently facing its quiet red sun. The substellar region is warm and wet — temperatures reach 310 K, and a shallow, mineral-rich sea covers much of the dayside, kept liquid by greenhouse warming. The nightside is frozen, but atmospheric circulation prevents the extremes seen on flare-star worlds: winds carry heat from day to night, maintaining a broad temperate band along the terminator. The moderate eccentricity (0.12) creates slight seasonal variations as the planet's distance from the star oscillates.
>
> The atmospheric anomaly that first drew attention was carbonyl sulfide (OCS) — a trace gas with no known abiotic source at the detected concentration. Hydrogen sulfide was also elevated beyond geological expectations. The source is biological.
>
> **Sulfur Weavers** — organisms with a metabolism built on sulfur chemistry rather than carbon-oxygen biochemistry. At the cellular level, they are rod-shaped, roughly 3-5 micrometers long, with an outer coating of precipitated silicate minerals. Their energy metabolism is chemosynthetic: they oxidize atmospheric H₂S and dissolved sulfides from groundwater, storing energy in polysulfide chain bonds (analogous to ATP's phosphate bonds in terrestrial life) and releasing it by cleaving these chains. Their metabolic waste products — carbonyl sulfide and excess hydrogen sulfide — accumulate in the atmosphere, creating the signature that betrayed their presence. Crucially, Sulfur Weavers do not photosynthesize. Their energy source is chemical, not solar — which means they thrive on the nightside as readily as the dayside.
>
> Their genetic material is unlike anything terrestrial: the informational polymers use sulfur-containing base analogs instead of nitrogen-containing nucleobases, with a sugar-phosphate backbone replaced by a sulfur-silicon framework. This is not merely different biochemistry — it is a fundamentally alien molecular architecture, suggesting an independent origin of life.
>
> But the most extraordinary feature is what these organisms build. Sulfur Weavers are not merely colonial — they are truly multicellular, with differentiated cell types: structural silica-depositing cells that construct the mineral framework, metabolic sulfur-oxidizing cells that power the organism, and reproductive spore cells for dispersal. From these three cell types, Aurelian's biosphere has produced the first confirmed macroscopic alien land organisms ever encountered by humanity.
>
> **Spire-trees** — the signature organism. Near geothermal vents, where warm sulfide-laden water seeps to the surface, Sulfur Weavers build hollow biogenic silica lattice trunks 2-5 meters tall, topped with branching crowns of flattened crystal plates for atmospheric H₂S gas exchange. The silica framework is strong in compression and lightweight, with translucent amber walls enclosing internal void spaces that house the living tissue. Individual spire-trees grow slowly — centimeters per year — but can live for thousands of years, their lattices thickening and branching with age.
>
> **Ground lattices** spread between vent sites, following underground sulfide seeps. These are horizontal crystalline networks, low (10-30 cm) and web-like, covering terrain in amber crystalline carpets that crunch faintly underfoot.
>
> At the richest vent fields, spire-trees develop dense overlapping crowns — **canopy formations** where crystal plates layer above one another, filtering the dim red sunlight into warm amber tones beneath. Walking through these formations is like entering an alien cathedral: translucent amber spires rising overhead, light diffusing through crystal lattice walls, the air warm and sulfurous.
>
> The forests thin with distance from geothermal activity — dense spire-tree groves near the vents, transitioning to low ground lattice mats, then bare rock. But because the organisms are chemosynthetic, they are not confined to the sunlit hemisphere. Nightside vent sites host their own forests: smaller, hardier spire-trees clustered around isolated geothermal sources in the frozen dark, glowing faintly amber when illuminated by artificial light.
>
> Reproduction is by fragmentation and waterborne spore dispersal. Spore cells released from mature spire-trees are carried by groundwater flow to new vent sites, where they establish fresh colonies that slowly build into new lattice structures over centuries.
>
> Aurelian's atmosphere is unbreathable (25% CO₂), and the hydrogen sulfide concentrations in the geothermal areas are toxic to humans. But the quiet stellar environment, moderate gravity, and stable temperatures make this world far more accessible than Ferrus. A pressurized research colony on the terminator would be straightforward to establish. The crystal forests are fragile — silica lattice shatters under impact — and any human activity near vent sites must contend with the tension between scientific preservation and the simple desire to walk among the spires. At only 11 light-years from Earth, Aurelian will be among the first life-bearing worlds humanity can reach. The spire-trees will be waiting, as they have waited for millennia, growing one crystalline layer at a time.

**c — "Shale"** (Cold rocky)
- Orbital: 0.10 AU | Ecc: 0.06 | Period: 28 days
- Physical: 0.85 M⊕ | Radius: 0.95 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.94 g | Temp: 170 K
- Atmosphere: 0.015 atm — CO₂ 80%, N₂ 15% | Scale height: 8.8 km
- Habitability: 12/100
- Science: 35
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Water (polar ice, subsurface permafrost), Atmospheric CO₂, Rare Earth Elements
- Solar Power: 0.36×
> A cold, nearly airless world just beyond the habitable zone. Shale's thin atmosphere provides minimal greenhouse warming, leaving the surface locked in perpetual frost. Water ice exists extensively, both at the poles and as subsurface permafrost. The surface is geologically quiet — older and more cratered than Aurelian, with exposed sedimentary-like layering in canyon walls (hence the name). No biological activity has been detected here; the cold and thin atmosphere preclude liquid water. Shale serves as a staging base for Aurelian research operations, offering stable ground, accessible water ice, and a safe distance from the biological contamination concerns of the life-bearing inner world.

**d — "Ashglaze"** (Ice dwarf)
- Orbital: 0.25 AU | Ecc: 0.10 | Period: 110 days
- Physical: 0.04 M⊕ | Radius: 0.35 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.33 g | Temp: 90 K
- Atmosphere: None
- Habitability: 1/100
- Science: 20
- Resources: Regolith, Water (water ice)
- Solar Power: 0.06×
> A small, frozen body in the outer system. Ashglaze is a Ceres-class dwarf planet composed of water ice, rock, and dark carbonaceous material. Its surface is ancient and uniform — no geological activity, no atmosphere, no features of note. A fuel and water depot for ships transiting the Ross 128 system.

- **Distance:** 11.27 ly
- **Galactic Position:** (28.1, -20981.7) ly
- **Galactic Velocity:** (202.9, -13.5) km/s
- **Galactic Orbit:** SMA: 19,589.0 ly | Ecc: 0.098 | ω: 50.3° | M₀: 227.4° | Period: 169.0 Myr
- **Spectral Type:** M5Ve + M5Ve + M5Ve (triple)
- **Stars:**
  - EZ Aquarii A: Mass 0.10 M☉ | Radius 0.14 R☉ | Luminosity 0.0006 L☉
  - EZ Aquarii B: Mass 0.10 M☉ | Radius 0.14 R☉ | Luminosity 0.0005 L☉
  - EZ Aquarii C: Mass 0.10 M☉ | Radius 0.13 R☉ | Luminosity 0.0004 L☉

A hierarchical triple system: A and B form a close binary (separation ~0.03 AU), with C orbiting the pair at roughly 5 AU. All three are flare stars. The gravitational dynamics severely limit stable planetary orbits.

#### Planets

**ABb — "Triplepoint"** (Hot irradiated rocky)
- Orbital: 0.015 AU from A-B barycenter | Ecc: 0.03 | Period: 1.9 days
- Physical: 0.25 M⊕ | Radius: 0.65 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.59 g | Temp: 520 K
- Atmosphere: None (flare-stripped)
- Habitability: 1/100
- Science: 30
- Resources: Regolith
- Solar Power: 4.89×
> Orbiting the close A-B pair at a distance less than Mercury's from the Sun, Triplepoint endures a double dose of flare radiation from two stars simultaneously. The gravitational field oscillates as the binary rotates, creating a complex tidal environment that keeps the planet's interior warm despite its tiny mass. The surface is radiation-darkened and thermally stressed, with networks of fractures from the oscillating tidal forces. From the surface, the two primary stars would appear as close, brilliant red points — sometimes nearly touching, sometimes separated — with the third star, C, visible as a fainter red dot wandering across the sky. A novelty destination at best.

### 13. Procyon (Alpha Canis Minoris)
- **Distance:** 11.46 ly
- **Galactic Position:** (17.4, -21001.6) ly
- **Galactic Velocity:** (205.9, 1.0) km/s
- **Galactic Orbit:** SMA: 19,822.4 ly | Ecc: 0.060 | ω: 93.8° | M₀: 175.8° | Period: 170.9 Myr
- **Spectral Type:** F5IV-V + DA (white dwarf)
- **Stars:**
  - Procyon A: Mass 1.50 M☉ | Radius 2.05 R☉ | Luminosity 6.93 L☉
  - Procyon B: Mass 0.60 M☉ | Radius 0.012 R☉ (white dwarf, ~7,740 K surface, ~16 AU separation)

The eighth brightest star in Earth's sky. Procyon A is an F-type subgiant — brighter and more massive than Sol, beginning to evolve off the main sequence. Procyon B is a dim white dwarf. Like the Sirius system, the white dwarf's past as a red giant may have disrupted any primordial planetary system, but planets could have formed or migrated afterward.

#### Planets

**b — "Zephyr"** (Hot Neptune)
- Orbital: 0.5 AU | Ecc: 0.08 | Period: 88 days
- Physical: 15 M⊕ (0.047 M_J) | Radius: 3.5 R⊕ | Temp: 530 K (cloud-top)
- Atmosphere: Hydrogen-helium, silicate haze, water vapor | Scale height: 38 km
- Habitability: 0/100 (no solid surface)
- Science: 0
- Resources: Gas Giant Atmosphere
- Solar Power: 27.72×
> A hot Neptune sweltering under Procyon A's considerable luminosity. Zephyr's atmosphere is inflated by the intense radiation, giving it a puffy, low-density profile. The upper atmosphere shows silicate haze layers and water vapor absorption. Cloud-top temperatures exceed 500 K. High-altitude winds circulate heat from the permanent dayside to the nightside at speeds exceeding 2 km/s, creating a superrotating equatorial jet. Zephyr is visually striking from a distance: a pale gold sphere against the brilliant yellow-white glare of Procyon A.

**c — "Archon"** (Warm gas giant)
- Orbital: 2.0 AU | Ecc: 0.12 | Period: 730 days
- Physical: 0.85 M_J (270 M⊕) | Radius: 1.1 R_J | Temp: 290 K (cloud-top)
- Atmosphere: Hydrogen-helium, water clouds, ammonia | Scale height: 32 km
- Habitability: 0/100 (no solid surface)
- Science: 0
- Resources: Gas Giant Atmosphere
- Solar Power: 1.73×
> A warm Jupiter orbiting in what would be Procyon A's habitable zone — if it had a solid surface. Cloud-top temperatures are near room temperature, and the upper atmosphere contains extensive water cloud decks that give Archon a brilliant white appearance, making it one of the most reflective planets in Zone 1. Deep in the atmosphere, temperatures and pressures rise rapidly. A faint ring system of water ice and silicate dust encircles the planet, sculpted by the gravity of a single small embedded moon.

> **Moon: "Diadem"** (Ring-embedded gap moon, of Archon)
> - Orbital: 250,000 km from Archon | Period: 1.5 days
> - Physical: 0.001 M⊕ | Radius: 0.08 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.16 g | Temp: 260 K
> - Atmosphere: None
> - Habitability: 0/100
> - Science: 15
> - Resources: Regolith, Water (water ice coating)
> - Solar Power: 1.73×
> > A tiny, bright shepherd moon embedded at the outer edge of Archon's ring system, responsible for the sharp gap that divides the main ring from its diffuse outer component. Diadem's surface is coated in fresh water-ice grains swept up from the ring, giving it an albedo above 0.9 — one of the most reflective solid bodies in Zone 1. The shape is roughly spheroidal but slightly oblate, with equatorial ridges of accreted ring material. At only 500 km across, Diadem lacks the gravity to retain any atmosphere. The scientific interest is in the ring dynamics: Diadem's orbital resonances create wakes and density waves visible from orbit, a textbook demonstration of gravitational sculpting.

**d — "Hesperon"** (Cold gas giant)
- Orbital: 5.0 AU | Ecc: 0.10 | Period: 2,900 days
- Physical: 2.5 M_J (794 M⊕) | Radius: 1.15 R_J | Temp: 175 K (cloud-top)
- Atmosphere: Hydrogen-helium, ammonia clouds, methane absorption | Scale height: 30 km
- Habitability: 0/100 (no solid surface)
- Science: 0
- Resources: Gas Giant Atmosphere
- Solar Power: 0.28×
> The largest planet in the Procyon system, a cold gas giant orbiting well inside Procyon B's 16 AU orbital distance. Hesperon's atmosphere shows the banded structure typical of rapidly rotating gas giants, with ammonia cirrus clouds and deeper water-cloud layers. The planet's orbit is mildly perturbed by Procyon B, causing slow precession over millennia. Hesperon's main utility is as a fuel depot — its deep gravity well and extensive hydrogen atmosphere make atmospheric skimming efficient.

> **Moon: "Cobalt"** (Small rocky inner moon, of Hesperon)
> - Orbital: 220,000 km from Hesperon | Period: 1.3 days
> - Physical: 0.004 M⊕ | Radius: 0.14 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.20 g | Temp: 155 K
> - Atmosphere: None
> - Habitability: 1/100
> - Science: 20
> - Resources: Metal Ore (rich, iron-nickel core remnant), Regolith
> - Solar Power: 0.28×
> > A dense, iron-rich little world on a tight orbit inside Hesperon's radiation belts. Cobalt's surface is a dark blue-gray — magnetite and pyroxene, with metallic flecks that catch the distant light of Procyon A. The iron content is roughly 60% by mass, suggesting Cobalt is the stripped core of a larger differentiated body that lost its mantle in a giant impact. The surface is smooth where ancient iron-rich lavas pooled and solidified, broken by sharp scarps where the body contracted as it cooled. A mining prospect with extraordinary iron and nickel concentrations, though the radiation environment from Hesperon's magnetosphere makes long-duration surface operations hazardous.

> **Moon: "Zephyrine"** (Large icy moon with thin atmosphere, of Hesperon)
> - Orbital: 500,000 km from Hesperon | Period: 3.5 days
> - Physical: 0.020 M⊕ | Radius: 0.35 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.16 g | Temp: 120 K (surface); ~260 K (subsurface)
> - Atmosphere: 0.0005 atm — N₂ 90%, CH₄ 8%, trace CO | Scale height: 20 km
> - Habitability: 12/100
> - Science: 55
> - Resources: Regolith, Water (subsurface ocean), Hydrocarbons (CH₄ atmosphere)
> - Solar Power: 0.28×
> > The largest moon of Hesperon and the most scientifically compelling body in the Procyon outer system. Zephyrine possesses a thin but detectable nitrogen atmosphere, likely outgassed from a warm interior maintained by tidal interaction with Hesperon and orbital resonance with Cobalt. The surface is a mosaic of ancient, dark, cratered highlands and younger, smooth cryolava plains where ammonia-water mixtures have erupted and pooled. Cryovolcanic plumes vent nitrogen and methane to altitudes of 50 km. A subsurface ocean is strongly suspected at 60-100 km depth. Zephyrine is the prime candidate for a permanent outpost in the Procyon system.

> **Moon: "Wrack"** (Tiny captured irregular, of Hesperon)
> - Orbital: 1,800,000 km from Hesperon | Period: 15.0 days (retrograde)
> - Physical: 0.0005 M⊕ | Radius: 0.06 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.14 g | Temp: 140 K
> - Atmosphere: None
> - Habitability: 0/100
> - Science: 15
> - Resources: Regolith
> - Solar Power: 0.28×
> > A battered fragment on a distant retrograde orbit, almost certainly a captured asteroid scattered inward from Procyon's outer debris belt. The surface is uniformly dark and ancient, saturated with craters at every scale. The body is irregularly shaped: roughly 350 km along its longest axis, with a pronounced concavity where a catastrophic impact nearly split it apart. Wrack's orbit precesses measurably over centuries due to perturbations from both Procyon A and the distant influence of Procyon B.

### 14. 61 Cygni
- **Distance:** 11.40 ly
- **Galactic Position:** (35.1, -20990.6) ly
- **Galactic Velocity:** (228.5, -6.3) km/s
- **Galactic Orbit:** SMA: 21,903.0 ly | Ecc: 0.051 | ω: 306.7° | M₀: 326.8° | Period: 188.2 Myr
- **Spectral Type:** K5V + K7V
- **Stars:**
  - 61 Cygni A: Mass 0.70 M☉ | Radius 0.67 R☉ | Luminosity 0.15 L☉
  - 61 Cygni B: Mass 0.63 M☉ | Radius 0.60 R☉ | Luminosity 0.09 L☉

A wide binary of two orange K-type stars separated by roughly 84 AU — far enough apart that each can host its own stable planetary system. The first star system to have its distance measured by parallax (in 1838), 61 Cygni holds a special place in the history of astronomy.

#### Planets of 61 Cygni A

**Ab — "Hearthstone"** (Rocky inner world)
- Orbital: 0.15 AU | Ecc: 0.04 | Period: 24 days
- Physical: 1.3 M⊕ | Radius: 1.08 R⊕ | Gravity: 1.11 g | Temp: 420 K
- Atmosphere: 0.18 atm — CO₂ 75%, N₂ 18%, SO₂ 4% | Scale height: 8.5 km
- Habitability: 9/100
- Science: 30
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Atmospheric CO₂, Rare Earth Elements, Uranium Ore
- Solar Power: 6.67×
> A warm rocky planet too close to A for habitability. Hearthstone has a thin, toxic CO₂ atmosphere and surface temperatures that preclude liquid water. Active volcanism maintains the atmosphere against slow thermal escape. The terrain is rugged — shield volcanoes, rift valleys, and impact basins. A mining target for refractory minerals.

**Ac + Ac-II — "Castor & Pollux"** (Binary planet pair in habitable zone)
- Orbital: 0.40 AU from A | Ecc: 0.03 | Period: 107 days
- Mutual orbit: 48,000 km separation | Mutual period: 4.2 days | Mutually tidally locked
- **Castor (Ac):** 2.4 M⊕ | Radius: 1.26 R⊕ | Gravity: 1.51 g | Temp: 268 K
  - Atmosphere: 0.85 atm — N₂ 65%, CO₂ 24%, H₂O 6%, Ar 3% | Scale height: 7.1 km
  - Habitability: 52/100
  - Science: 150
  - Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Water (shallow CO₂-saturated lakes), Atmospheric CO₂, Rare Earth Elements, Uranium Ore
  - Solar Power: 0.94×
- **Pollux (Ac-II):** 0.9 M⊕ | Radius: 0.97 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.96 g | Temp: 255 K
  - Atmosphere: 0.30 atm — CO₂ 55%, N₂ 35%, Ar 6%, H₂O 3% | Scale height: 8.2 km
  - Habitability: 38/100
  - Science: 120
  - Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Water (polar ice caps), Atmospheric CO₂, Rare Earth Elements
  - Solar Power: 0.94×
> The most extraordinary planetary configuration in Zone 1. Castor and Pollux are a gravitationally bound binary planet pair — two rocky worlds locked in mutual tidal embrace, orbiting their common center of mass every 4.2 days while together circling 61 Cygni A in the habitable zone. Both present the same face to the other at all times.
>
> Castor, the larger partner, is a super-Earth with near-Earth gravity and a substantial CO₂-rich atmosphere. Liquid water exists in equatorial lowlands — cold, CO₂-saturated pools and shallow lakes, not true oceans. The atmosphere is unbreathable but manageable: surface pressure is comfortable, and atmospheric processing could eventually yield oxygen. The sky is a pale salmon color, with Pollux hanging motionless at one point in the sky (from the facing hemisphere) as a disc roughly 3 times the apparent diameter of Earth's Moon — a breathtaking sight, its ruddy surface markings clearly visible to the naked eye.
>
> Pollux is smaller, colder, and drier — a Mars-plus world with a thin CO₂ atmosphere and extensive polar ice caps. Surface conditions are harsher, but the lower gravity makes surface operations easier. From Pollux's facing hemisphere, Castor dominates the sky: a massive disc nearly 5 times the Moon's apparent size, showing weather patterns, ice caps, and the dark patches of its liquid water basins. On the far hemispheres of both worlds, the partner planet is never visible — only inferred by the strong tidal effects.
>
> The binary pair creates a unique operational environment. Spacecraft can transfer between the two worlds with minimal delta-v — the mutual orbit velocity is only about 0.3 km/s. A colony could span both planets, with Castor serving as the primary habitation (better atmosphere, liquid water) and Pollux as a mining and industrial base (lower gravity, easier launch). The gravitational interplay between the pair drives tidal heating in both worlds, keeping their interiors warmer than their distance from A would otherwise allow. No life has been detected on either world, but the conditions on Castor are within the range where prebiotic chemistry could be active.

**Ad — "Sleetwall"** (Cold mini-Neptune)
- Orbital: 1.2 AU | Ecc: 0.09 | Period: 557 days
- Physical: 10 M⊕ | Radius: 2.7 R⊕ | Temp: 100 K (cloud-top)
- Atmosphere: Hydrogen-helium, methane, water ice clouds | Scale height: 21 km
- Habitability: 0/100 (no solid surface)
- Science: 0
- Resources: Gas Giant Atmosphere
- Solar Power: 0.10×
> A small ice giant in the outer system of A. Sleetwall's atmosphere is dominated by hydrogen and helium, with methane giving it a blue-gray tint. Water ice clouds form at high altitudes, creating a hazy, washed-out appearance. A fuel harvesting target and navigational landmark.

#### Planets of 61 Cygni B

**Bb — "Ashquiet"** (Cold rocky)
- Orbital: 0.30 AU | Ecc: 0.05 | Period: 87 days
- Physical: 1.05 M⊕ | Radius: 1.02 R⊕ | Gravity: 1.01 g | Temp: 195 K
- Atmosphere: 0.04 atm — CO₂ 70%, N₂ 22%, Ar 5% | Scale height: 9.0 km
- Habitability: 22/100
- Science: 45
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Water (polar ice, subsurface permafrost), Atmospheric CO₂, Rare Earth Elements, Uranium Ore
- Solar Power: 1.00×
> A cold, Mars-like world orbiting 61 Cygni B. Earth-like in size and gravity, but too far from its dim star for liquid water. The thin CO₂ atmosphere provides minimal greenhouse warming. Water ice is abundant at the poles and as subsurface permafrost. The surface is ancient and cratered, with a few shield volcanoes suggesting sporadic geological activity. Ashquiet has the right gravity and the right chemistry — it just needs more heat. A long-term terraforming candidate, if anyone has the patience.
