### 28. Altair (Alpha Aquilae)
- **Distance:** 16.7 ly
- **Galactic Position:** (36.2, -20980.8) ly
- **Galactic Velocity:** (213.1, -26.1) km/s
- **Galactic Orbit:** SMA: 20,588.5 ly | Ecc: 0.125 | ω: 15.9° | M₀: 268.3° | Period: 177.2 Myr
- **Spectral Type:** A7IV-V
- **Stars:**
  - Altair: Mass 1.79 M☉ | Radius 2.03 R☉ (equatorial) / 1.63 R☉ (polar) | Luminosity 10.6 L☉

The twelfth brightest star in Earth's sky, and a world apart from the red dwarfs that dominate Zones 1 and 2. Altair is a hot A-type star spinning at 286 km/s — so fast that it is visibly oblate, with an equatorial radius 25% larger than its polar radius. This rapid rotation drives gravity darkening: the poles are hotter and brighter than the equator, creating a star that is literally brighter at the top and bottom than around the middle. The habitable zone extends to 2.5-4.5 AU, and the intense UV flux means any planetary atmospheres must contend with extreme photochemistry. No planets have been confirmed, but the star is young (~1.2 billion years) and luminous enough to support a wide planetary system.

#### Planets

**b — "Scintilla"** (Hot rocky)
- Orbital: 0.8 AU | Ecc: 0.06 | Period: 179 days
- Physical: 1.60 M⊕ | Radius: 1.13 R⊕ | Gravity: 1.25 g | Temp: 680 K
- Atmosphere: None (UV-stripped)
- Habitability: 2/100
- Science: 30
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Rare Earth Elements
- Solar Power: 16.56×
> A sun-blasted cinder orbiting well inside Altair's scorching inner system. At 0.8 AU from a star producing 10.6 solar luminosities, Scintilla receives 16 times the solar flux Earth does. Any atmosphere has been stripped by the ferocious UV radiation. The surface is a bright, reflective wasteland of anorthosite and plagioclase — pale minerals that give the planet an unusually high albedo. Under Altair's blue-white glare, Scintilla is a blinding white point. The gravity-darkened equatorial band of the star is visible as a subtle dimming across the stellar disc from the planet's surface. Mining value is limited — the UV flux degrades surface equipment rapidly.

**c — "Azurhaven"** (Gas giant in habitable zone)
- Orbital: 3.2 AU | Ecc: 0.08 | Period: 1,515 days
- Physical: 1.4 M_J (445 M⊕) | Radius: 1.15 R_J | Temp: 260 K (cloud-top)
- Atmosphere: Hydrogen-helium, water clouds, ammonia cirrus | Scale height: 35 km
- Habitability: 0/100 (no solid surface)
- Science: 0
- Resources: Gas Giant Atmosphere
- Solar Power: 1.04×
> A Jupiter-class gas giant sitting in the habitable zone of Altair — where an Earth-like world would thrive, a gas giant squats instead. Azurhaven's cloud-top temperature is near room temperature, and the upper atmosphere features extensive water cloud decks that give the planet a striking blue-white appearance reminiscent of a gas-giant Neptune but far larger. Under Altair's blue-white light, the planet appears vividly blue — a sapphire orb. The atmosphere is highly photochemically active: Altair's UV flux drives complex hydrocarbon chemistry in the upper layers, producing hazes that scatter short-wavelength light. Azurhaven is the brightest and most visually dramatic planet in Zone 2.

> **Moon: "Dustmote"** (Tiny rocky inner moon, of Azurhaven)
> - Orbital: 180,000 km from Azurhaven | Period: 0.9 days
> - Physical: 0.001 M⊕ | Radius: 0.08 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.16 g | Temp: 280 K
> - Atmosphere: None
> - Habitability: 0/100
> - Science: 20
> - Resources: Regolith
> - Solar Power: 1.04×
> > The innermost moon, a tiny body baked by Altair's UV flux and Azurhaven's reflected radiation. Dustmote's surface is UV-darkened silicate — once bright, now photochemically altered to a dull gray-brown. Micrometeorite impacts create transient bright spots that fade within decades. A navigation waypoint, nothing more.

> **Moon: "Shardlight"** (Medium icy moon with fractures, of Azurhaven)
> - Orbital: 350,000 km from Azurhaven | Period: 1.8 days
> - Physical: 0.015 M⊕ | Radius: 0.30 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.17 g | Temp: 200 K
> - Atmosphere: 0.0001 atm — O₂ from ice radiolysis | Scale height: 16 km
> - Habitability: 5/100
> - Science: 40
> - Resources: Regolith, Water (subsurface ocean)
> - Solar Power: 1.04×
> > A fractured ice world whose surface tells a story of tidal violence. Shardlight's ice shell is cross-cut by a global network of fractures — some fresh and bright, exposing clean ice; others ancient and darkened by radiation processing. The fracture pattern is geometric, almost crystalline, earning the moon its name: from orbit, the surface resembles a cracked pane of frosted glass with light leaking through the seams. Tidal heating from Azurhaven maintains a warm interior, and a subsurface ocean is probable. Cryovolcanic activity along the major fracture zones feeds a tenuous oxygen atmosphere generated by ice radiolysis.

> **Moon: "Sapphira"** (Large atmosphered moon with liquid water, of Azurhaven)
> - Orbital: 600,000 km from Azurhaven | Period: 3.8 days
> - Physical: 0.15 M⊕ | Radius: 0.55 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.50 g | Temp: 255 K (greenhouse-adjusted)
> - Atmosphere: 0.30 atm — N₂ 55%, CO₂ 30%, H₂O 10%, Ar 3% | Scale height: 8.5 km
> - Habitability: 35/100
> - Science: 150
> - Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Water (equatorial seas), Atmospheric CO₂
> - Solar Power: 1.04×
> > The crown jewel of the Altair system, and one of the most remarkable moons in the entire catalog. Sapphira is large enough to retain a substantial atmosphere, and warm enough — through the combination of Altair's habitable-zone luminosity and tidal heating from Azurhaven — for liquid water to exist on the surface. Equatorial seas cover roughly 15% of the surface, shallow and mineral-rich, fed by cryovolcanic melt and rainfall from a thin but active water cycle. The polar regions are ice-capped, and the mid-latitudes are a barren landscape of volcanic rock and frost. The atmosphere is unbreathable — the CO₂ is far too high — but the pressure is manageable, the temperatures are survivable, and the water is real. From Sapphira's surface, Azurhaven dominates the sky as a brilliant blue-white banded sphere roughly 6 degrees across, and Altair blazes overhead as a flattened blue-white disc — visibly oblate, its gravity-darkened equator a subtle band of cooler light. Sapphira is the best colonization prospect in Zone 2 outside of the dedicated habitable-zone rocky worlds, and the only moon in the catalog with confirmed surface liquid water.

> **Moon: "Outrider"** (Distant captured irregular, of Azurhaven)
> - Orbital: 3,000,000 km from Azurhaven | Period: 25.0 days (retrograde)
> - Physical: 0.002 M⊕ | Radius: 0.11 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.17 g | Temp: 180 K
> - Atmosphere: None
> - Habitability: 0/100
> - Science: 15
> - Resources: Regolith
> - Solar Power: 1.04×
> > A dark, distant body on a retrograde orbit, captured from the debris population that pervades the Altair system. Outrider's surface is carbonaceous and ancient, its albedo less than 0.05. The orbit is inclined 140 degrees to Azurhaven's equatorial plane — strongly retrograde, confirming capture origin. A lonely outpost at the edge of Azurhaven's gravitational influence.

**d — "Stormhewn"** (Cold ice giant)
- Orbital: 8.0 AU | Ecc: 0.12 | Period: 5,370 days
- Physical: 20 M⊕ (0.063 M_J) | Radius: 3.7 R⊕ | Temp: 130 K (cloud-top)
- Atmosphere: Hydrogen-helium, methane ice clouds, photochemical hazes | Scale height: 28 km
- Habitability: 0/100 (no solid surface)
- Science: 0
- Resources: Gas Giant Atmosphere
- Solar Power: 0.17×
> An ice giant in the cold outer system, far enough from Altair to escape the worst of the UV bombardment but still photochemically influenced. Stormhewn has a deep blue-green color from methane absorption, with white methane-ice cirrus clouds and an extensive system of dark spots — persistent anticyclonic storms driven by internal heat. The oblate shape of Altair is clearly visible from this distance, a reminder of the star's violent rotation. A standard helium-3 harvesting target with the bonus of studying photochemistry in an extreme UV environment.

> **Moon: "Crag"** (Tiny dark captured body, of Stormhewn)
> - Orbital: 150,000 km from Stormhewn | Period: 2.0 days
> - Physical: 0.0008 M⊕ | Radius: 0.07 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.16 g | Temp: 120 K
> - Atmosphere: None
> - Habitability: 0/100
> - Science: 15
> - Resources: Regolith
> - Solar Power: 0.17×
> > A small, angular, carbonaceous body — one of the darkest objects in the Altair system. Crag's surface has been photochemically darkened by Altair's intense UV flux, reducing its albedo to nearly zero. The shape is distinctly non-spherical: a blocky, fractured mass roughly 400 km across, likely a collisional fragment that Stormhewn captured from the debris population. Impact craters are shallow and degraded, partially infilled by dark, fine-grained regolith.

### 29. 70 Ophiuchi
- **Distance:** 16.7 ly
- **Galactic Position:** (32.1, -20977.7) ly
- **Galactic Velocity:** (217.0, 1.4) km/s
- **Galactic Orbit:** SMA: 20,797.3 ly | Ecc: 0.010 | ω: 120.0° | M₀: 149.5° | Period: 179.0 Myr
- **Spectral Type:** K0V + K4V
- **Stars:**
  - 70 Ophiuchi A: Mass 0.90 M☉ | Radius 0.84 R☉ | Luminosity 0.43 L☉
  - 70 Ophiuchi B: Mass 0.70 M☉ | Radius 0.65 R☉ | Luminosity 0.12 L☉

A close binary of two orange K-type stars with an 88-year orbital period and a separation ranging from 11.4 to 34.8 AU. The relatively close approach limits stable planetary orbits to within roughly 3-4 AU of either star. 70 Ophiuchi was the subject of one of the most famous false planet claims in history — in 1855, a supposed Jupiter-mass planet was announced, then debunked. No planets have been confirmed by modern surveys, but the system's stability zone allows for interesting worlds around the brighter A component.

#### Planets of 70 Ophiuchi A

**Ab — "Kindlegate"** (Warm rocky)
- Orbital: 0.30 AU | Ecc: 0.04 | Period: 58 days
- Physical: 1.70 M⊕ | Radius: 1.14 R⊕ | Gravity: 1.31 g | Temp: 418 K
- Atmosphere: 0.15 atm — CO₂ 70%, N₂ 22%, H₂O 4%, SO₂ 3% | Scale height: 8.5 km
- Habitability: 10/100
- Science: 40
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Rare Earth Elements, Uranium Ore, Atmospheric CO₂
- Solar Power: 4.78×
> A warm, dense rocky world in the inner system. Kindlegate is too hot for liquid water but retains a thin CO₂ atmosphere through volcanic activity. The terrain is dominated by massive basaltic plateaus and deep rift valleys. From the surface, 70 Ophiuchi B is visible as a brilliant orange star — at closest binary approach, B appears as a disc roughly 1/10 the apparent size of Earth's Sun, bright enough to cast shadows and noticeably warm the nightside.

**Ac — "Thornmark"** (Rocky world in habitable zone)
- Orbital: 0.62 AU | Ecc: 0.05 | Period: 176 days
- Physical: 2.50 M⊕ | Radius: 1.28 R⊕ | Gravity: 1.53 g | Temp: 268 K (equilibrium); surface 272-295 K

- Habitability: 48/100

- Solar Power: 1.12×
> The habitable zone world of 70 Ophiuchi A, and the system's primary colonization target. Thornmark sits comfortably in the habitable zone, with surface temperatures averaging 280 K with greenhouse warming. Liquid water exists in equatorial lowlands — cold, mineral-rich lakes that cover roughly 15% of the surface. The atmosphere is substantial but CO₂-heavy, requiring breathing equipment. The planet is not tidally locked (the habitable zone is far enough from the K0V star to avoid this), giving it a proper day-night cycle of approximately 28 hours. This alone makes Thornmark more psychologically habitable than the eternal-twilight worlds of M-dwarf systems. The gravity is moderately heavy (1.53 g). The sky is a warm orange under A's light, with B appearing as a brilliant companion star that migrates across the sky over the 88-year binary period.

**Ad — "Dimwall"** (Cold mini-Neptune)

- Science: 0
- Resources: Gas Giant Atmosphere
- Solar Power: 0.19×
> A small ice giant at the outer edge of A's stable planetary zone. Beyond Dimwall, the gravitational influence of 70 Ophiuchi B during close binary passages begins to destabilize orbits. The atmosphere is bland — pale gray, with water ice clouds and faint ammonia haze. A helium-3 source and little more.

### 30. 40 Eridani (Keid)
- **Distance:** 16.3 ly
- **Galactic Position:** (18.0, -21007.4) ly
- **Galactic Velocity:** (237.1, -6.1) km/s
- **Galactic Orbit:** SMA: 22,766.4 ly | Ecc: 0.082 | ω: 290.5° | M₀: 342.7° | Period: 195.4 Myr
- **Spectral Type:** K1V + DA4 + M4.5Ve (triple)
- **Stars:**
  - 40 Eridani A (Keid): Mass 0.84 M☉ | Radius 0.81 R☉ | Luminosity 0.46 L☉
  - 40 Eridani B: Mass 0.57 M☉ | Radius 0.014 R☉ (DA4 white dwarf, ~16,500 K surface)
  - 40 Eridani C: Mass 0.20 M☉ | Radius 0.23 R☉ (M4.5Ve flare star)

A hierarchical triple system: the bright K-dwarf primary orbits at ~400 AU from the B-C pair (a white dwarf and red dwarf in a tight 250-year mutual orbit). The vast separation means A's planetary system is completely unperturbed by B and C. 40 Eridani A is famous as the canonical location of the planet Vulcan in Star Trek — and while the real system lacks pointed-eared logicians, it does have a stable, Sun-like star with a habitable zone at a comfortable distance. One candidate planet has been reported but remains unconfirmed.

#### Planets of 40 Eridani A

> A hot, dense rocky world baking in the inner system. Forge's thin atmosphere is maintained by active volcanism — the surface shows extensive lava plains and calderas. Too hot for any form of habitation, but the elevated concentrations of tungsten and molybdenum in surface rocks make it a notable resource target. From the surface, the white dwarf 40 Eridani B is visible as an intensely bright blue-white point (visual magnitude -8 at closest approach) — brighter than Venus from Earth, and hot enough to contribute measurable UV flux.

**Ac — "Surak's Landing"** (Temperate super-Earth, habitable zone)

> The habitable zone planet, and one of the best colonization targets in Zone 2. Surak's Landing orbits a stable K1V star at a comfortable distance — far enough to avoid tidal locking, giving it a day-night cycle of approximately 32 hours. Surface temperatures are near-Earth: 275-298 K in the temperate zone, with polar ice caps and equatorial warmth. Liquid water covers 35% of the surface in shallow, cold seas and interconnected lake systems. The atmosphere is thick, with a modest greenhouse effect, and the trace oxygen is photochemical. The gravity is heavy (1.68 g) but tolerable for long-term habitation. The sky is a pale amber-orange under the K1V star, with the white dwarf and red dwarf companions visible as distinctive points of light. Named with a nod to the system's fictional heritage — and because it genuinely is the kind of world where a colony could take root and endure.

The lower gravity and accessible ice make it a natural staging base. From the surface, the triple star system puts on a show: the K-dwarf primary dominates the daytime sky, while on clear nights the white dwarf and its red dwarf companion are visible as a close, contrasting pair — one blue-white and brilliant, the other ruddy and dim.

Sentinel's thick hydrogen-helium envelope over a dense core gives it a low bulk density and a pale, featureless appearance. It serves as a gravitational anchor for outer system dynamics and a modest fuel source.

### 31. Sigma Draconis (Alsafi)
- **Distance:** 18.8 ly
- **Galactic Position:** (29.8, -20974.3) ly
- **Galactic Velocity:** (198.0, -32.1) km/s
- **Galactic Orbit:** SMA: 19,343.4 ly | Ecc: 0.182 | ω: 36.6° | M₀: 251.6° | Period: 166.9 Myr
- **Spectral Type:** G9V
- **Stars:**
  - Sigma Draconis: Mass 0.87 M☉ | Radius 0.78 R☉ | Luminosity 0.41 L☉

A quiet, stable G/K-borderline star — nearly as Sun-like as Tau Ceti but slightly cooler and dimmer. Sigma Draconis has very low chromospheric activity, a slow rotation period, and a mature age (~5.4 billion years). It is one of the most boring stars in the solar neighborhood — and in stellar astrophysics, boring is excellent. At least one planet has been reported, though confirmation remains uncertain. The system modeled here assumes a modest planetary family around a calm, stable host.

A mining target for refractory metals.

Windhollow orbits a quiet G9V star at a distance that provides Earth-like insolation scaled for the star's lower luminosity. Not tidally locked — the day-night cycle is approximately 26 hours, and the axial tilt of 18 degrees creates mild seasons. Liquid water covers 28% of the surface in cold, clear lakes and shallow seas. The atmosphere is thick, with a mild greenhouse effect. The CO₂ is too high for unaided breathing, but the trace oxygen and the stable, quiet stellar environment make this one of the most Earth-like worlds in Zone 2. The name comes from the persistent, gentle trade winds that circle the equatorial regions — driven by differential heating, they produce a soft, constant sighing audible at the surface. A premier colonization target.

### 32. Eta Cassiopeiae (Achird)
- **Distance:** 19.4 ly
- **Galactic Position:** (41.1, -21001.0) ly
- **Galactic Velocity:** (230.6, 15.3) km/s
- **Galactic Orbit:** SMA: 22,156.0 ly | Ecc: 0.083 | ω: 215.5° | M₀: 47.2° | Period: 190.3 Myr
- **Spectral Type:** G0V + K7V
- **Stars:**
  - Eta Cassiopeiae A: Mass 1.00 M☉ | Radius 1.04 R☉ | Luminosity 1.23 L☉
  - Eta Cassiopeiae B: Mass 0.57 M☉ | Radius 0.55 R☉ | Luminosity 0.06 L☉

The nearest near-solar twin. Eta Cassiopeiae A has almost exactly one solar mass and slightly higher luminosity, making its habitable zone essentially identical to Sol's — centered around 1.1 AU. The K7V companion orbits at a wide separation (roughly 12-36 AU in a 480-year period), close enough to provide spectacular sky views from any planet but far enough to permit stable orbits throughout A's habitable zone. No planets have been confirmed, but the solar-twin nature of A makes this a high-priority target for terrestrial planet surveys.

#### Planets of Eta Cassiopeiae A

**Ab — "Vitrine"** (Venus-analog)
- Orbital: 0.50 AU | Ecc: 0.04 | Period: 117 days
- Physical: 1.95 M⊕ | Radius: 1.19 R⊕ | Gravity: 1.38 g | Temp: 540 K (equilibrium); surface ~700 K with greenhouse
- Atmosphere: 68 atm — CO₂ 94%, N₂ 4.5%, SO₂ 0.8%, trace HCl | Scale height: 14.5 km
- Habitability: 3/100
- Science: 75
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Rare Earth Elements, Atmospheric CO₂
- Solar Power: 4.92×
> Achird's Venus — a crushing CO₂ atmosphere, sulfuric acid clouds, and surface temperatures sufficient to melt zinc. Slightly more massive than Venus, with correspondingly thicker atmosphere and stronger gravity. The surface, mapped by radar, shows continent-scale volcanic plateaus, vast lava plains, and a rift system that encircles the equator. Cloud-top habitats at 55 km altitude would experience near-Earth conditions (0.5 atm, 305 K), but the surface is a furnace. A Venus study analog, not a colony site.

**Ac — "Goldenvale"** (Temperate super-Earth, habitable zone — LIFE)
- Orbital: 1.05 AU | Ecc: 0.03 | Period: 380 days
- Physical: 2.60 M⊕ | Radius: 1.29 R⊕ | Gravity: 1.56 g | Temp: 276 K (equilibrium); surface 278-305 K
- Atmosphere: 1.08 atm — N₂ 74%, O₂ 21%, H₂O 2.5%, Ar 1.8%, CO₂ 0.04% (trace) | Scale height: 7.2 km
- Habitability: 100/100
- Science: 550
- Life: **Aureophytes** — golden-green photosynthetic biosphere (microbial mats, ground cover, pad-plants, shrub-analogs; chitin-analog structure)
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Water (oceans), Rare Earth Elements, Uranium Ore
- Solar Power: 1.12×
> The crown jewel of the Eta Cassiopeiae system, and the highest habitability score in Zone 2. Goldenvale orbits a near-solar twin at almost exactly Earth's orbital distance. It has a proper day-night cycle (22 hours), an axial tilt of 24 degrees that creates Earth-like seasons, and surface temperatures that range from 278 K at the poles to 305 K at the equator. Liquid water covers 42% of the surface — genuine oceans, warm and mineral-rich. The atmosphere is breathable: 21% oxygen, produced over more than three billion years by Aureophytes, the world's dominant photosynthetic biosphere — and the only alien world where multicellular land plants cover the continents.
>
> The foundation is microbial. Aureophyte single-celled organisms bear a chlorophyll-analog pigment that absorbs blue and red light with an extension into the near-UV — an adaptation to Eta Cassiopeiae A's slightly hotter spectrum. They carpet land surfaces in golden-green microbial mats and form floating meadows in shallow coastal waters. In deeper waters, photosynthetic activity drops off rapidly; the open ocean is clear and blue. Chemolithotrophs inhabit deep rock, and a cycling of carbon, nitrogen, and oxygen has maintained atmospheric stability for billions of years.
>
> But Goldenvale's biosphere has crossed a threshold no other alien world has matched: true multicellular land plants have evolved from the microbial mat ancestors. The Aureophyte plants share the golden-green pigment of their single-celled relatives but have developed a structural polymer — a **chitin-analog polysaccharide** — that is harder and slightly glassy in texture, rigid but brittle under impact. This is not cellulose; it is something entirely alien, closer in feel to thin shell than wood. Their root systems are **rhizoid networks**: hair-fine horizontal filaments that spread through the upper soil layer, an adaptation to spreading in gravity-compacted soil at 1.56 g rather than pushing deep taproots down against it. They have a **xylem-analog** vascular system for drawing water and minerals upward, but no phloem equivalent — no sugar transport. Every part of the plant must photosynthesize locally, which forces flat, pad-like growth forms and strictly limits height. Reproduction is by spores only — no flowers, no seeds, no fruit.
>
> **Ground cover** (1-5 cm) is the dominant land surface: dense golden-green mats covering most exposed terrain, spongy and slightly crusty up close, looking like a golden-green lawn from a distance. This is what gives the continents their burnished, amber-tinged appearance from orbit.
>
> **Pad-plants** (10-30 cm) grow from the mat surface — multicellular rosettes of thick, fleshy photosynthetic pads, like stonecrops or sedum but golden-green and glassy-textured. Low and spreading, they dot the ground cover across most temperate latitudes.
>
> **Shrub-analogs** (30-80 cm) are the most advanced plants on Goldenvale. Short rigid chitin-analog stems support clusters of thick photosynthetic pads. Found in sheltered valleys with good moisture, these are the tallest organisms on the planet — 1.56 g combined with no true wood and no phloem means nothing grows taller. They are striking up close: translucent golden-green pads catching the light on dark, glassy stems.
>
> No animals exist. The biosphere is in early multicellular stages, comparable to Earth's Ediacaran period. The chitin-analog structural polymer is highly resistant to enzymatic breakdown, starving any potential herbivore niche before it can establish — nothing has evolved to eat these plants. Decomposition is handled entirely by specialized microbial Aureophytes that slowly dissolve dead plant matter over months.
>
> The soil is the key detail for colonization. Three billion years of microbial activity have produced genuine soil across the continents — weathered rock mixed with organic matter from dead Aureophytes. The physical properties are excellent: it holds water, drains properly, has texture and structure. But Earth crops planted directly in Goldenvale soil germinate and then struggle — stunted growth, yellowing leaves, nutrient deficiency. The problems are specific and solvable: alien nitrogen compounds that Earth plants cannot directly metabolize (Aureophyte nitrogen-fixers use a different enzymatic pathway), slightly alkaline pH (8.5-9.0) from carbonate-heavy bedrock, and a wrong micronutrient profile — excess copper and manganese, deficient in molybdenum and zinc. With amendments (pH correction, trace mineral supplementation, inoculation with Earth nitrogen-fixing bacteria), the soil becomes productive. The physical foundation is there; the biology needs bridging. Goldenvale is not farmable out of the box, but it is the closest thing to farmable that any alien world offers.
>
> Twice per local year, synchronized with the axial tilt's seasonal cycle, all Aureophytes — microbial and multicellular alike — enter a reproductive bloom. Dense clouds of windborne spores fill the lower atmosphere for weeks, turning the air hazy gold and triggering severe allergic reactions in most humans — coughing, eye irritation, and respiratory inflammation. The spores are not toxic, but the immune response is intense and universal among unacclimatized populations. Sealed habitats with filtered air circulation are essential during bloom season.
>
> The gravity is the other challenge. At 1.56 g, a 70 kg human weighs 109 kg. Walking is exhausting; running is punishing; falls are dangerous. Long-term residents face cardiovascular strain, joint degradation, and chronic fatigue. Children born here would develop denser bones and stronger muscles over generations, but the first colonists will struggle. Despite this, Goldenvale remains the most Earth-like world in Zone 2: breathable air, liquid oceans, warm climate, real seasons. Eta Cassiopeiae B appears in the sky as a brilliant orange star, bright enough to read by at closest approach. Stand on a hillside between bloom seasons, breathe the warm air, and the golden-green landscape stretching to the horizon looks almost like home — low shrubs and pad-plants on a carpet of living gold, under a sky the right shade of blue. Home, if home were heavier, and the plants were made of glass.

**Ad — "Coldharbour"** (Mars-analog)
- Orbital: 1.8 AU | Ecc: 0.07 | Period: 854 days
- Physical: 0.58 M⊕ | Radius: 0.86 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.78 g | Temp: 195 K
- Atmosphere: 0.02 atm — CO₂ 85%, N₂ 10%, Ar 3% | Scale height: 9.5 km
- Habitability: 28/100
- Science: 70
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Water (polar ice), Rare Earth Elements, Atmospheric CO₂
- Solar Power: 0.38×
> A cold, dry Mars-analog with a thin atmosphere and polar ice caps. Coldharbour's surface is ancient and heavily cratered, with dry riverbeds and sedimentary formations hinting at a warmer, wetter past. The lower gravity makes surface operations and departure easy. A natural staging base between the habitable zone and the outer system.

**Ae — "Farsight"** (Cold gas giant)
- Orbital: 5.0 AU | Ecc: 0.10 | Period: 3,950 days
- Physical: 0.80 M_J (254 M⊕) | Radius: 1.05 R_J | Temp: 125 K (cloud-top)
- Atmosphere: Hydrogen-helium, ammonia clouds, water clouds | Scale height: 27 km
- Habitability: 0/100 (no solid surface)
- Science: 0
- Resources: Gas Giant Atmosphere
- Solar Power: 0.05×
> A Saturn-class gas giant in the outer system, orbiting within the binary's stability limit. Farsight has a banded atmosphere of ammonia clouds and deeper water-cloud layers, with a faint ring system — a sparse collection of icy particles likely sourced from disrupted moons. The planet's orbit is mildly perturbed by Eta Cassiopeiae B during close binary passages, but remains stable over billions of years. A fuel depot and a gravitational anchor for the outer system.

> **Moon: "Frostpeak"** (Icy moon with cryovolcanism, of Farsight)
> - Orbital: 400,000 km from Farsight | Period: 3.0 days
> - Physical: 0.010 M⊕ | Radius: 0.25 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.16 g | Temp: 110 K
> - Atmosphere: 0.0002 atm — N₂, trace CO₂ | Scale height: 18 km
> - Habitability: 5/100
> - Science: 40
> - Resources: Regolith, Water (subsurface brine, cryovolcanic ice)
> - Solar Power: 0.05×
> > An Enceladus-class icy moon with active cryovolcanic features. Frostpeak's south polar region hosts a network of parallel fractures — "tiger stripes" — that vent nitrogen gas and water-ice particles in plumes reaching 80 km altitude. The surface around the vents is young and smooth, resurfaced by cryolava flows; the northern hemisphere is ancient, dark, and cratered. Tidal heating from Farsight maintains a subsurface brine reservoir that feeds the eruptions. From the surface, Farsight's banded disc fills a modest arc of the sky, and on clear occasions, the ring system — that sparse collection of icy particles — is visible as a faint, ethereal band.

### 33. Delta Pavonis
- **Distance:** 19.9 ly
- **Galactic Position:** (13.8, -20974.9) ly
- **Galactic Velocity:** (201.7, -9.2) km/s
- **Galactic Orbit:** SMA: 19,453.6 ly | Ecc: 0.091 | ω: 62.1° | M₀: 213.1° | Period: 167.8 Myr
- **Spectral Type:** G8IV (subgiant)
- **Stars:**
  - Delta Pavonis: Mass 1.05 M☉ | Radius 1.22 R☉ | Luminosity 1.22 L☉

One of the most scrutinized stars in SETI history. Delta Pavonis is a G8 subgiant — slightly evolved off the main sequence, brighter and larger than the Sun, with an estimated age of 6.6-6.9 billion years. It is metal-rich, stable, and surrounded by a habitable zone almost identical to Sol's. For decades, Delta Pavonis has topped lists of "best SETI targets" — a Sun-like star old enough for complex life, close enough for radio detection, and quiet enough for stable planetary conditions. One suspected planetary signal has been reported but never confirmed.

#### Planets

**b — "Incandros"** (Hot rocky)
- Orbital: 0.18 AU | Ecc: 0.05 | Period: 28 days
- Physical: 2.10 M⊕ | Radius: 1.21 R⊕ | Gravity: 1.44 g | Temp: 610 K
- Atmosphere: 0.05 atm — CO₂ 85%, Na 6%, K 3%, SiO 4% | Scale height: 11.5 km
- Habitability: 3/100
- Science: 30
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Rare Earth Elements, Lithium Ore, Uranium Ore, Atmospheric CO₂
- Solar Power: 37.65×
> A scorched super-Earth in the inner system. Incandros orbits too close for anything except rock and metal. The thin atmosphere is exotic — sodium, potassium, and silicon monoxide vapor, liberated by the intense heating of the dayside surface. At night, this vapor condenses as a rain of metallic and silicate crystals. The dayside is partially molten. A mineralogical curiosity, not a destination.

**c — "Emberheart"** (Warm super-Earth)
- Orbital: 0.45 AU | Ecc: 0.04 | Period: 109 days
- Physical: 3.50 M⊕ | Radius: 1.43 R⊕ | Gravity: 1.71 g | Temp: 395 K
- Atmosphere: 1.5 atm — CO₂ 55%, N₂ 30%, H₂O 10%, SO₂ 3% | Scale height: 7.2 km
- Habitability: 12/100
- Science: 40
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Rare Earth Elements, Lithium Ore, Uranium Ore, Atmospheric CO₂
- Solar Power: 6.02×
> A massive, hot super-Earth with a thick, steamy atmosphere. Too hot for surface water (except as superheated steam), but the atmospheric dynamics are vigorous: towering convective cells, perpetual overcast, and acidic rain that evaporates before reaching the surface in the hottest regions. The terrain is volcanic and tectonically active. Emberheart might have been habitable earlier in the star's life, when Delta Pavonis was dimmer — now, as the star brightens during its subgiant evolution, the planet is being slowly cooked.

**c-II — "Pavonis Reach"** (Temperate super-Earth, habitable zone)
- Orbital: 0.90 AU | Ecc: 0.03 | Period: 309 days
- Physical: 2.80 M⊕ | Radius: 1.33 R⊕ | Gravity: 1.58 g | Temp: 270 K (equilibrium); surface 275-300 K
- Atmosphere: 1.0 atm — N₂ 68%, CO₂ 16%, H₂O 10%, Ar 3%, O₂ 0.7% | Scale height: 7.0 km
- Habitability: 58/100
- Science: 150
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Water (deep oceans), Rare Earth Elements, Atmospheric CO₂
- Solar Power: 1.51×
> The SETI target's prize: a super-Earth in the habitable zone of a Sun-like star, with liquid oceans and near-Earth temperatures. Pavonis Reach has a 25-hour day-night cycle, a 15-degree axial tilt, and surface conditions that are, on paper, outstanding. Oceans cover 38% of the surface — deep, cold, and mineral-rich, with a slight greenish tint from dissolved iron compounds. The atmosphere is the most Earth-like of any planet in Zone 2: nitrogen-dominated, with moderate CO₂ and trace oxygen. No life has been detected, despite exhaustive surveys driven by decades of SETI interest. The absence is scientifically significant: this world has had 6.6 billion years of stable, Earth-like conditions, and yet remains sterile. Either the origin of life is rarer than optimists hope, or something specific to this world's chemistry inhibits it. Pavonis Reach is a humbling reminder that habitability and inhabited are not synonyms.

**d — "Graycape"** (Cold rocky)
- Orbital: 1.6 AU | Ecc: 0.06 | Period: 740 days
- Physical: 0.70 M⊕ | Radius: 0.90 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.86 g | Temp: 195 K
- Atmosphere: 0.03 atm — CO₂ 78%, N₂ 15%, Ar 5% | Scale height: 9.2 km
- Habitability: 22/100
- Science: 45
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Water (polar ice), Rare Earth Elements, Atmospheric CO₂
- Solar Power: 0.48×
> A cold Mars-analog. Thin atmosphere, polar ice caps, ancient terrain. Graycape's modest gravity and abundant ice make it a natural staging base. The surface preserves a clean impact record stretching back billions of years — useful for studying the bombardment history of the Delta Pavonis system.

**e — "Outrider"** (Cold mini-Neptune)
- Orbital: 4.0 AU | Ecc: 0.10 | Period: 2,900 days
- Physical: 10 M⊕ | Radius: 2.6 R⊕ | Temp: 120 K (cloud-top)
- Atmosphere: Hydrogen-helium, methane, ammonia ice clouds | Scale height: 21 km
- Habitability: 0/100 (no solid surface)
- Science: 0
- Resources: Gas Giant Atmosphere
- Solar Power: 0.08×
> A small ice giant in the outer system. Blue-gray with faint banding, Outrider is a standard helium-3 harvesting target. Its orbit is stable and well-characterized.

### 34. 82 Eridani
- **Distance:** 19.7 ly
- **Galactic Position:** (16.4, -21010.4) ly
- **Galactic Velocity:** (198.8, 16.3) km/s
- **Galactic Orbit:** SMA: 19,281.0 ly | Ecc: 0.121 | ω: 127.6° | M₀: 133.3° | Period: 166.4 Myr
- **Spectral Type:** G6V
- **Stars:**
  - 82 Eridani: Mass 0.97 M☉ | Radius 0.92 R☉ | Luminosity 0.74 L☉

A near-solar analog with three confirmed super-Earths and a prominent dust disk extending from 20-100 AU. 82 Eridani is slightly less luminous than the Sun but very similar in mass and spectral type, with a mature age (~5.4 billion years) and low activity. The dust disk is one of the densest known around a Sun-like star within 25 light-years, suggesting an active outer system of asteroids and comets.

#### Planets

**b** (Hot super-Earth, confirmed)
- Orbital: 0.12 AU | Ecc: 0.04 | Period: 18.3 days
- Physical: 2.82 M⊕ | Radius: 1.31 R⊕ | Gravity: 1.64 g | Temp: 590 K
- Atmosphere: 0.20 atm — CO₂ 78%, N₂ 14%, SO₂ 5%, Na 2% | Scale height: 8.5 km
- Habitability: 5/100
- Science: 35
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Rare Earth Elements, Lithium Ore, Uranium Ore, Atmospheric CO₂
- Solar Power: 51.39×
> The innermost confirmed planet, a dense, hot super-Earth. Tidally locked, with a dayside that reaches 640 K with greenhouse warming. The thin atmosphere is volcanic — CO₂ and SO₂ maintained by relentless outgassing from a geologically hyperactive interior. The surface shows fresh lava flows and extensive caldera systems. Mining potential for platinum-group metals.

**c** (Hot super-Earth, confirmed)
- Orbital: 0.20 AU | Ecc: 0.05 | Period: 40.1 days
- Physical: 2.52 M⊕ | Radius: 1.27 R⊕ | Gravity: 1.56 g | Temp: 450 K
- Atmosphere: 0.35 atm — CO₂ 65%, N₂ 25%, H₂O 5%, SO₂ 3% | Scale height: 8.0 km
- Habitability: 8/100
- Science: 35
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Rare Earth Elements, Lithium Ore, Uranium Ore, Atmospheric CO₂
- Solar Power: 18.50×
> The middle confirmed planet, slightly cooler but still too hot for liquid water. A thick, volcanic world with a more substantial atmosphere than b — water vapor is present as steam, creating a permanent overcast that diffuses the star's light into a dim, yellowish haze. Active volcanic chains and tectonic rift valleys dominate the terrain.

**d — "Copper Shore"** (Warm super-Earth, confirmed)
- Orbital: 0.36 AU | Ecc: 0.03 | Period: 90.4 days
- Physical: 3.57 M⊕ | Radius: 1.43 R⊕ | Gravity: 1.75 g | Temp: 348 K
- Atmosphere: 1.0 atm — CO₂ 45%, N₂ 35%, H₂O 12%, Ar 5%, SO₂ 2% | Scale height: 7.0 km
- Habitability: 15/100
- Science: 50
- Resources: Metal Ore (rich, copper and iron oxides), Regolith, Rare Earth Elements, Atmospheric CO₂
- Solar Power: 5.71×
> The outermost confirmed planet, and the most interesting of the three. Copper Shore sits at the inner edge of the Venus zone — too hot for surface water at sea level, but conditions improve with altitude. Mountain plateaus above 8 km elevation experience temperatures of 310 K, and orographic clouds deposit warm, acidic rain. The planet's copper-orange color comes from extensive deposits of iron and copper oxide minerals on the surface, visible from orbit as striking bands of rust-red, verdigris, and ochre. The name is apt: the planet gleams like a tarnished copper coin.

**e — "Pale Strand"** (Temperate rocky, habitable zone)
- Orbital: 0.70 AU | Ecc: 0.04 | Period: 250 days
- Physical: 1.60 M⊕ | Radius: 1.13 R⊕ | Gravity: 1.25 g | Temp: 265 K (equilibrium); surface 270-292 K
- Atmosphere: 0.70 atm — N₂ 66%, CO₂ 20%, H₂O 8%, Ar 3%, O₂ 0.4% | Scale height: 7.3 km
- Habitability: 50/100
- Science: 150
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Water (shallow seas, lagoons), Rare Earth Elements, Atmospheric CO₂
- Solar Power: 1.51×
> A temperate world in the habitable zone, and 82 Eridani's best prospect for colonization. Pale Strand has a 22-hour day, a 12-degree axial tilt, and surface temperatures that permit liquid water across 25% of the surface — shallow, clear seas and brackish coastal lagoons. The "pale strand" refers to the extensive beaches of white carbonate sand that ring the volcanic island continents, visible from orbit as bright arcs against the darker ocean. The atmosphere is substantial and nitrogen-dominated; the CO₂ requires breathing equipment but is lower than many super-Earths. Gravity is moderate. The dust disk is visible from the surface as a faint zodiacal band, brighter and broader than anything seen from Earth.

**f — "Deepcast"** (Cold gas giant)
- Orbital: 3.5 AU | Ecc: 0.08 | Period: 2,675 days
- Physical: 1.2 M_J (381 M⊕) | Radius: 1.1 R_J | Temp: 108 K (cloud-top)
- Atmosphere: Hydrogen-helium, ammonia and water clouds | Scale height: 26 km
- Habitability: 0/100 (no solid surface)
- Science: 0
- Resources: Gas Giant Atmosphere
- Solar Power: 0.06×
> A Jupiter-class gas giant in the outer system, orbiting just inside the inner edge of the dust disk. Deepcast's gravity shepherds the inner edge of the disk, maintaining the gap between the rocky inner planets and the asteroid/comet belt beyond. The atmosphere shows broad ammonia cloud bands and occasional storm systems. A fuel depot and the gravitational gatekeeper of the 82 Eridani system.

> **Moon: "Crucian"** (Volcanic Io-analog, of Deepcast)
> - Orbital: 350,000 km from Deepcast | Period: 1.6 days
> - Physical: 0.012 M⊕ | Radius: 0.27 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.16 g | Temp: 115 K (surface avg; eruption sites 1,500+ K)
> - Atmosphere: 0.00001 atm — SO₂ sublimation
> - Habitability: 1/100
> - Science: 50
> - Resources: Regolith
> - Solar Power: 0.06×
> > The innermost moon of Deepcast, and the most volcanically active body in the 82 Eridani system. Tidal forces from Deepcast, amplified by orbital resonance with Tideglass, pump energy into Crucian's interior at a rate comparable to Io. The surface is a patchwork of sulfur flows and silicate lava plains, constantly resurfaced by eruptions that fountain material into space. A sulfur dioxide plasma torus encircles Deepcast, fed by Crucian's relentless outgassing. The proximity to the dust disk means fresh impactors add to the geological violence — some eruptions may be impact-triggered.

> **Moon: "Tideglass"** (Large icy Europa-analog, of Deepcast)
> - Orbital: 650,000 km from Deepcast | Period: 3.5 days
> - Physical: 0.020 M⊕ | Radius: 0.36 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.15 g | Temp: 95 K (surface); ~270 K (subsurface ocean)
> - Atmosphere: 0.0002 atm — O₂ from ice radiolysis | Scale height: 16 km
> - Habitability: 12/100
> - Science: 55
> - Resources: Regolith, Water (subsurface ocean)
> - Solar Power: 0.06×
> > A Europa-class ocean world with a smooth, young ice shell showing extensive fracture networks and very few impact craters — evidence of rapid resurfacing. The ice shell is estimated at 15-25 km thick, overlying a global liquid water ocean 60-90 km deep. Tidal heating from Deepcast, locked in resonance with Crucian, maintains ocean temperatures above freezing. The surface shows double-ridged lineae, chaotic terrain, and smooth refrozen plains — classic signatures of a thin ice shell over warm water. Tideglass is the best subsurface ocean target in the 82 Eridani system and a prime astrobiology candidate.

> **Moon: "Drift"** (Ancient icy Callisto-analog, of Deepcast)
> - Orbital: 1,500,000 km from Deepcast | Period: 10.0 days
> - Physical: 0.005 M⊕ | Radius: 0.18 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.15 g | Temp: 80 K
> - Atmosphere: None
> - Habitability: 1/100
> - Science: 20
> - Resources: Regolith, Water (surface ice)
> - Solar Power: 0.06×
> > The quiet outer moon — a heavily cratered ice body far enough from Deepcast to escape significant tidal heating. Drift's surface is ancient and dark, a palimpsest of overlapping impact basins that records the bombardment history of the 82 Eridani system. The interior is undifferentiated: a cold mixture of rock and ice with no subsurface ocean. Drift serves as a stable observation platform for the dust disk, which is spectacularly visible from this vantage — a broad, luminous band spanning the sky.

> **Moon: "Vagabond"** (Distant captured irregular, of Deepcast)
> - Orbital: 4,000,000 km from Deepcast | Period: 35.0 days (retrograde)
> - Physical: 0.001 M⊕ | Radius: 0.08 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.16 g | Temp: 75 K
> - Atmosphere: None
> - Habitability: 0/100
> - Science: 15
> - Resources: Regolith
> - Solar Power: 0.06×
> > A small, dark body on a retrograde orbit at the boundary of Deepcast's gravitational influence — a captured fragment from the dust disk that pervades the 82 Eridani system. Vagabond's surface is carbonaceous and impact-saturated. The orbit is highly inclined and slowly precessing, characteristic of gravitational capture. A tracer of the system's dynamical history, otherwise unremarkable.

### 35. Gliese 581
- **Distance:** 20.5 ly
- **Galactic Position:** (18.8, -20972.1) ly
- **Galactic Velocity:** (238.1, 11.0) km/s
- **Galactic Orbit:** SMA: 22,844.1 ly | Ecc: 0.093 | ω: 238.6° | M₀: 26.2° | Period: 196.0 Myr
- **Spectral Type:** M3V
- **Stars:**
  - Gliese 581: Mass 0.31 M☉ | Radius 0.30 R☉ | Luminosity 0.013 L☉

Once the most famous exoplanet system in the world. Gliese 581 was the site of multiple planet claims in the late 2000s, including the celebrated (and later disputed) "Gliese 581 g" — allegedly the first rocky planet in a habitable zone. The dust has settled: three planets are now firmly confirmed (e, b, c), while d remains disputed. The system is a cautionary tale in the history of exoplanet science, but the confirmed planets are interesting in their own right.

#### Planets

**e** (Hot sub-Earth, confirmed)
- Orbital: 0.028 AU | Ecc: 0.02 | Period: 3.15 days
- Physical: 1.70 M⊕ | Radius: 1.14 R⊕ | Gravity: 1.31 g | Temp: 510 K
- Atmosphere: 0.02 atm — CO₂ 80%, SO₂ 12%, Na 5% | Scale height: 10.5 km
- Habitability: 4/100
- Science: 25
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Rare Earth Elements, Uranium Ore, Atmospheric CO₂
- Solar Power: 16.58×
> The innermost confirmed planet, a hot, dense world with a thin volcanic atmosphere. Tidally locked, with a dayside that glows faintly in thermal infrared. The nightside is frozen. Surface mineralogy shows elevated concentrations of rare earths — a potential mining target for automated extraction.

**b — "Redwatch"** (Hot Neptune-mass, confirmed)
- Orbital: 0.041 AU | Ecc: 0.03 | Period: 5.37 days
- Physical: 15.8 M⊕ | Radius: 2.8 R⊕ | Gravity: 2.01 g | Temp: 430 K
- Atmosphere: 5.0 atm — CO₂ 35%, H₂O steam 30%, N₂ 20%, H₂ 10%, SO₂ 3% | Scale height: 9.5 km
- Habitability: 3/100
- Science: 30
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Rare Earth Elements, Atmospheric CO₂
- Solar Power: 7.73×
> The most massive confirmed planet, and a world at the boundary between super-Earth and mini-Neptune. Gliese 581 b has a thick, steamy atmosphere over a dense rocky-ice core. Surface conditions (if a surface exists beneath the thick envelope) are extreme: hundreds of degrees, tens of atmospheres, perpetual steam. The upper atmosphere is a photochemically active cauldron, with complex sulfur and water chemistry. Named Redwatch for its position as the most prominent object in the Gliese 581 system when observed in infrared — it glows like a hot coal, a red beacon against the cold sky.

**c** (Warm super-Earth, confirmed)
- Orbital: 0.073 AU | Ecc: 0.07 | Period: 12.9 days
- Physical: 5.36 M⊕ | Radius: 1.60 R⊕ | Gravity: 2.09 g | Temp: 310 K
- Atmosphere: 2.5 atm — CO₂ 48%, N₂ 30%, H₂O 15%, Ar 4%, SO₂ 2% | Scale height: 6.5 km
- Habitability: 18/100
- Science: 50
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Rare Earth Elements, Atmospheric CO₂
- Solar Power: 2.44×
> A massive super-Earth near the inner edge of the habitable zone — arguably inside it, arguably out, depending on atmospheric modeling. Gliese 581 c has a thick, greenhouse-heavy atmosphere that pushes surface temperatures well above equilibrium. The result is a hot, humid, high-pressure world: surface conditions resemble a steamier version of Venus at high altitudes. Liquid water may exist at the poles, where temperatures are lowest, but the crushing gravity (2.09 g) and thick atmosphere make this a difficult colonization target. The famous "first habitable exoplanet" press releases of 2007 were overly optimistic — c is habitable only in the loosest sense.

**d — "Phantom"** (Disputed — modeled as cold rocky)
- Orbital: 0.22 AU | Ecc: 0.06 | Period: 66.8 days
- Physical: 5.94 M⊕ | Radius: 1.63 R⊕ | Gravity: 2.24 g | Temp: 195 K
- Atmosphere: 1.8 atm — CO₂ 55%, N₂ 32%, Ar 8%, H₂O 3% | Scale height: 5.5 km
- Habitability: 20/100
- Science: 45
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Rare Earth Elements, Atmospheric CO₂
- Solar Power: 0.27×
> The ghost planet. Gliese 581 d was announced in 2007, disputed in 2014, partially rehabilitated, and disputed again. If it exists, it is a massive super-Earth beyond the habitable zone — too cold for surface water, with a thick CO₂ atmosphere providing a strong but insufficient greenhouse effect. Surface temperatures hover around 210 K with greenhouse warming. The crushing gravity (2.24 g) makes this a punishing world to work on. Phantom is included here as a conditional entry: if the radial velocity signal is real, this is what it looks like. If not, the Gliese 581 system is even more compact than it appears.

**f — "Coldmark"** (Frozen outer rocky)
- Orbital: 0.50 AU | Ecc: 0.10 | Period: 229 days
- Physical: 0.85 M⊕ | Radius: 0.95 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.94 g | Temp: 115 K
- Atmosphere: 0.01 atm — CO₂ 80%, N₂ 15% | Scale height: 8.0 km
- Habitability: 6/100
- Science: 20
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Water (surface ice), Rare Earth Elements
- Solar Power: 0.05×
> A cold, nearly airless world at the outer edge of the Gliese 581 system. Coldmark has a thin CO₂ atmosphere and a surface of ancient, cratered ice and rock. Water ice is abundant. A refueling waypoint and the outermost significant body in a system that history will remember more for the planets it imagined than the planets it found.

### 36. HD 219134 (HR 8832)
- **Distance:** 21.3 ly
- **Galactic Position:** (44.1, -20998.4) ly
- **Galactic Velocity:** (219.7, -8.5) km/s
- **Galactic Orbit:** SMA: 21,077.5 ly | Ecc: 0.041 | ω: 357.2° | M₀: 277.6° | Period: 181.3 Myr
- **Spectral Type:** K3V
- **Stars:**
  - HD 219134: Mass 0.81 M☉ | Radius 0.78 R☉ | Luminosity 0.27 L☉

The most prolific known exoplanet host within 25 light-years. Six confirmed planets orbit this orange K-dwarf, spanning a wide range of masses and orbital distances — from a scorching lava world at 0.039 AU to a sub-Saturn at 3.1 AU. HD 219134 is a mature, quiet star with low chromospheric activity, providing a stable environment for its planetary family. Planet b was the first transiting exoplanet discovered around a star visible to the naked eye.

#### Planets

**b** (Ultra-hot rocky, confirmed, transiting)
- Orbital: 0.039 AU | Ecc: 0.01 | Period: 3.09 days
- Physical: 4.74 M⊕ | Radius: 1.60 R⊕ | Gravity: 1.85 g | Temp: 1,015 K
- Atmosphere: 0.003 atm — SiO, Na, K, Fe vapor | Scale height: 38 km
- Habitability: 1/100
- Science: 30
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Rare Earth Elements, Lithium Ore, Uranium Ore
- Solar Power: 177.51×
> The innermost planet, and a world in perpetual torment. HD 219134 b orbits so close to its star that the dayside is a permanent ocean of magma. The "atmosphere" is rock vapor — silicon monoxide, sodium, potassium, and iron — that condenses on the nightside as a rain of metallic and mineral crystals. Surface temperatures exceed 1,000 K. This planet was historically important as the first transiting world around a nearby naked-eye star, providing precise radius measurements that confirmed its rocky, iron-rich composition. Scientifically valuable. Operationally, a lava lake.

**c** (Hot rocky, confirmed, transiting)
- Orbital: 0.065 AU | Ecc: 0.02 | Period: 6.77 days
- Physical: 4.36 M⊕ | Radius: 1.51 R⊕ | Gravity: 1.91 g | Temp: 755 K
- Atmosphere: 0.02 atm — CO₂ 70%, SO₂ 18%, SiO 8% | Scale height: 12 km
- Habitability: 2/100
- Science: 30
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Rare Earth Elements, Uranium Ore, Atmospheric CO₂
- Solar Power: 63.91×
> The second transiting planet, slightly cooler than b but still hellishly hot. The thin atmosphere is maintained by intense volcanic outgassing — the surface is a landscape of active lava flows, calderas, and volcanic plains. At 755 K, the dayside rocks glow a dull red visible from orbit during the nightside transit. Like b, planet c provided early precision measurements of rocky exoplanet densities.

**f** (Warm super-Earth, confirmed)
- Orbital: 0.143 AU | Ecc: 0.05 | Period: 22.7 days
- Physical: 7.30 M⊕ | Radius: 1.82 R⊕ | Gravity: 2.20 g | Temp: 465 K
- Atmosphere: 2.0 atm — CO₂ 50%, N₂ 28%, H₂O steam 14%, SO₂ 5% | Scale height: 6.5 km
- Habitability: 6/100
- Science: 40
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Rare Earth Elements, Uranium Ore, Atmospheric CO₂
- Solar Power: 13.20×
> A massive, hot super-Earth with a thick, steamy atmosphere. At nearly 7.5 Earth masses, HD 219134 f is one of the most massive rocky-composition planets in Zone 2. The atmosphere is a dense fog of carbon dioxide and water steam, with sulfur dioxide haze layers creating a permanent yellowish overcast. Surface temperatures are too high for liquid water at sea level, but high-altitude plateaus experience slightly more moderate conditions. The crushing 2.2 g gravity makes this world feel oppressive even before accounting for the heat.

**d** (Warm super-Earth, confirmed)
- Orbital: 0.237 AU | Ecc: 0.04 | Period: 46.7 days
- Physical: 16.17 M⊕ | Radius: 2.4 R⊕ | Gravity: 2.81 g | Temp: 365 K
- Atmosphere: 8.0 atm — CO₂ 38%, N₂ 28%, H₂O 20%, H₂ 8%, He 4% | Scale height: 6.0 km
- Habitability: 4/100
- Science: 40
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Rare Earth Elements, Atmospheric CO₂
- Solar Power: 4.81×
> The largest inner planet by mass, and possibly a borderline mini-Neptune despite its rocky-heavy composition. HD 219134 d is massive enough to retain a substantial hydrogen-helium component in its atmosphere, giving it a thick, multi-layered atmosphere with water clouds at various altitudes. Surface conditions — if a solid surface exists beneath the deep atmosphere — are extreme: 400+ K, 20+ atm, perpetual twilight beneath the cloud decks. This world tests the boundary between super-Earth and gas dwarf.

**e** (Warm super-Earth, confirmed)
- Orbital: 0.37 AU | Ecc: 0.05 | Period: 79.1 days
- Physical: 5.10 M⊕ | Radius: 1.58 R⊕ | Gravity: 2.04 g | Temp: 305 K
- Atmosphere: 1.8 atm — CO₂ 42%, N₂ 35%, H₂O 14%, Ar 5%, SO₂ 2% | Scale height: 6.5 km
- Habitability: 18/100
- Science: 50
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Rare Earth Elements, Uranium Ore, Atmospheric CO₂
- Solar Power: 1.97×
> The outermost of the rocky inner planets, and the only one where surface temperatures approach the habitable range. HD 219134 e sits at the inner edge of the habitable zone, with a thick greenhouse atmosphere pushing surface temperatures to 340 K at the equator. Liquid water may exist at the poles, where temperatures drop to 300 K. The atmosphere is thick and humid, with persistent cloud cover and moderate rainfall — warm, acidic rain that erodes the basaltic surface into rounded, weathered terrain. At 2.04 g, the gravity is challenging. This is the system's best (and only) prospect for human presence, though "best" is relative when the surface feels like a Turkish bath in a pressure cooker.

**g — "Sentinel's Keep"** (Cold sub-Saturn, confirmed)
- Orbital: 3.11 AU | Ecc: 0.06 | Period: 2,247 days
- Physical: 0.34 M_J (108 M⊕) | Radius: 0.75 R_J | Temp: 95 K (cloud-top)
- Atmosphere: Hydrogen-helium, methane, ammonia ice clouds | Scale height: 20 km
- Habitability: 0/100 (no solid surface)
- Science: 0
- Resources: Gas Giant Atmosphere
- Solar Power: 0.03×
> The outer sentinel — a sub-Saturn gas giant far from the crowded inner system. Sentinel's Keep has a banded atmosphere of ammonia and methane ice clouds, with a pale blue-tan color from methane absorption. Its orbit is circular and stable, providing gravitational influence over the outer system. The planet's relatively low mass (roughly one-third Jupiter) gives it a deep but not crushing gravity well — atmospheric skimming for helium-3 is straightforward. The view from the vicinity is remarkable: the inner five planets are all visible at various times, a cascade of worlds strung along the ecliptic like beads on a wire.

### 37. Gliese 667 C
- **Distance:** 22.0 ly
- **Galactic Position:** (24.4, -20968.5) ly
- **Galactic Velocity:** (245.9, -9.1) km/s
- **Galactic Orbit:** SMA: 23,631.7 ly | Ecc: 0.119 | ω: 290.9° | M₀: 343.6° | Period: 202.6 Myr
- **Spectral Type:** M1.5V (in triple system with K3V + K5V)
- **Stars:**
  - Gliese 667 A: Mass 0.73 M☉ | Radius 0.68 R☉ | Luminosity 0.13 L☉ (K3V)
  - Gliese 667 B: Mass 0.69 M☉ | Radius 0.64 R☉ | Luminosity 0.09 L☉ (K5V)
  - Gliese 667 C: Mass 0.33 M☉ | Radius 0.34 R☉ | Luminosity 0.014 L☉ (M1.5V)

A hierarchical triple system: the bright K-dwarf pair (A and B) orbit each other at roughly 12 AU, while the M-dwarf C orbits the pair at approximately 230 AU. The wide separation ensures C's planetary system is dynamically independent. Gliese 667 C attracted intense attention in the 2010s when up to seven planets were claimed, including three super-Earths in the habitable zone — the most ever proposed for a single star. Subsequent analysis has reduced the firm count to two or three, but the system remains one of the most planet-rich M-dwarfs known. From any planet of C, the A-B pair appears as a brilliant close double star in the night sky.

#### Planets of Gliese 667 C

**b** (Hot super-Earth, confirmed)
- Orbital: 0.050 AU | Ecc: 0.03 | Period: 7.20 days
- Physical: 5.60 M⊕ | Radius: 1.62 R⊕ | Gravity: 2.13 g | Temp: 395 K
- Atmosphere: 1.2 atm — CO₂ 55%, N₂ 28%, H₂O 10%, SO₂ 5% | Scale height: 6.8 km
- Habitability: 8/100
- Science: 35
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Rare Earth Elements, Uranium Ore, Atmospheric CO₂
- Solar Power: 5.60×
> The innermost confirmed planet, a massive, dense super-Earth with a thick atmosphere. Too hot for liquid water at sea level — the steam atmosphere creates a strong greenhouse effect that pushes surface temperatures above 420 K. The terrain is volcanic and tectonically active, with extensive rift systems visible from orbit. Gliese 667 Cb is the base case for the system: confirmed, characterized, and uninhabitable.

**c — "Trilight"** (Temperate super-Earth, habitable zone, confirmed)
- Orbital: 0.125 AU | Ecc: 0.05 | Period: 28.1 days
- Physical: 3.80 M⊕ | Radius: 1.46 R⊕ | Gravity: 1.78 g | Temp: 255 K (equilibrium); surface 260-285 K
- Atmosphere: 0.85 atm — N₂ 60%, CO₂ 25%, H₂O 8%, Ar 4% | Scale height: 6.6 km
- Habitability: 42/100
- Science: 120
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Water (shallow seas), Rare Earth Elements, Lithium Ore, Atmospheric CO₂
- Solar Power: 0.90×
> The habitable zone super-Earth that made Gliese 667 C famous. Trilight orbits in the center of C's habitable zone, receiving enough flux for liquid water given moderate greenhouse warming. Tidally locked, with a substellar region where temperatures reach 285 K and shallow, cold seas cover 18% of the dayside. The atmosphere is thick enough for meaningful pressure and heat transport, but the CO₂ is too high for human respiration. The gravity is heavy at 1.78 g. Named Trilight for the remarkable night sky: Gliese 667 A and B appear as a brilliant close double star (visual magnitude approximately -6 and -5 respectively from this distance), providing enough light during "night" to cast faint double shadows. Combined with the dim red primary, this world is never truly dark. A viable colonization target, though the heavy gravity and tidal locking make it less attractive than worlds around brighter stars.

> **Moon: "Lantern"** (Bright icy captured body, of Trilight)
> - Orbital: 30,000 km from Trilight | Period: 1.5 days
> - Physical: 0.003 M⊕ | Radius: 0.14 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.15 g | Temp: 240 K
> - Atmosphere: None
> - Habitability: 1/100
> - Science: 15
> - Resources: Regolith, Water (surface ice)
> - Solar Power: 0.90×
> > A bright, icy captured body that gleams in the combined light of three stars. Lantern's surface is fresh water ice with a geometric albedo of 0.7 — it catches and reflects the light of Gliese 667 C's dim red glow, the brilliant orange double star of A and B, and on rare occasions, all three simultaneously. From Trilight's surface, Lantern is the brightest object in the night sky (such as it is on a world that is never truly dark), its icy face cycling through phases as it orbits the tidally locked planet. The moon may have originated in the outer reaches of the C system and migrated inward through gravitational interactions.

**d** (Temperate super-Earth, outer habitable zone)
- Orbital: 0.21 AU | Ecc: 0.06 | Period: 62 days
- Physical: 2.70 M⊕ | Radius: 1.30 R⊕ | Gravity: 1.60 g | Temp: 215 K (equilibrium); surface 225-250 K with greenhouse
- Atmosphere: 0.55 atm — N₂ 50%, CO₂ 36%, Ar 7%, H₂O 4% | Scale height: 6.8 km
- Habitability: 28/100
- Science: 80
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Water (seasonal brine ponds), Rare Earth Elements, Atmospheric CO₂
- Solar Power: 0.32×
> A cold super-Earth at the outer edge of the habitable zone. Gliese 667 Cd receives marginal stellar flux — liquid water is possible only with substantial greenhouse warming, and even then, only at the warmest substellar point. Seasonal brine ponds form and refreeze in irregular cycles. The higher CO₂ concentration (36%) provides more greenhouse effect but makes the atmosphere even less breathable. A harder, colder version of Trilight.

**e — "Farwatch"** (Cold rocky)
- Orbital: 0.40 AU | Ecc: 0.08 | Period: 163 days
- Physical: 1.40 M⊕ | Radius: 1.10 R⊕ | Gravity: 1.16 g | Temp: 150 K
- Atmosphere: 0.015 atm — CO₂ 80%, N₂ 14%, Ar 4% | Scale height: 8.5 km
- Habitability: 10/100
- Science: 35
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Water (surface frost, permafrost), Rare Earth Elements, Atmospheric CO₂
- Solar Power: 0.09×
> A cold, nearly airless world beyond the habitable zone. Farwatch's thin atmosphere barely qualifies as one — surface conditions approach vacuum. Water ice is abundant as surface frost and subsurface permafrost. The planet serves as a quiet staging base for the Gliese 667 C system, far from the inner worlds' tidal and thermal stresses. From the surface, the A-B binary pair is a stunning sight: two orange stars, close enough to resolve individually on good nights, bright enough to illuminate the frozen landscape in a warm, amber glow.

**f** (Frozen dwarf)
- Orbital: 0.85 AU | Ecc: 0.12 | Period: 505 days
- Physical: 0.10 M⊕ | Radius: 0.48 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.43 g | Temp: 90 K
- Atmosphere: None
- Habitability: 1/100
- Science: 20
- Resources: Regolith, Water (surface ice)
- Solar Power: 0.02×
> A tiny, frozen body at the system's edge. Water ice and dark carbonaceous rock, ancient and featureless. Gliese 667 Cf is the outermost significant body in a system that was once thought to hold seven planets. The reality is more modest — but a triple star system with two or three habitable zone candidates is remarkable enough without inflation.

---

## Zone 3: The Middle Distance (25-50 light-years)

The frontier expands. Zone 3 reaches from 25 to 50 light-years — far enough that even fusion drives measure transit in decades, close enough that the stars are still individually known and named. This is the zone of the famous: Vega, Fomalhaut, Arcturus, Pollux — names from myth and navigation, now revealed as places with worlds of their own. Here the catalog encounters its first A-type debris disk systems, its first evolved giant stars with endangered planets, and the extraordinary TRAPPIST-1 septuplet. Zone 3 contains 14 star systems, 3 life-bearing worlds, and over 70 planets and moons ranging from lava oceans to snowball worlds to the purple-black photosynthetic mats of TRAPPIST-1 e.

---

### 38. Vega (Alpha Lyrae)
- **Distance:** 25.0 ly
- **Galactic Position:** (46.9, -20982.5) ly
- **Galactic Velocity:** (224.5, -9.3) km/s
- **Galactic Orbit:** SMA: 21,521.7 ly | Ecc: 0.050 | ω: 332.8° | M₀: 302.4° | Period: 185.0 Myr
- **Spectral Type:** A0V
- **Stars:**
  - Vega: Mass 2.13 M☉ | Radius 2.82 R☉ (equatorial; oblate) | Luminosity 40.1 L☉

The brightest star in the northern summer sky and the former pole star (~12,000 BCE). Vega is a young, hot A-type star spinning so fast — one rotation every 12.5 hours — that it has deformed itself into an oblate spheroid, its equatorial radius 19% larger than its polar radius. Earth views Vega nearly pole-on, which means the star appears brighter and hotter than it would from the side. Vega was the first star discovered to have an infrared excess, revealing a debris disk of dust and planetesimals that made it the prototype for the "Vega phenomenon." The habitable zone lies far out (5-7 AU), but the star's youth (~450 Myr) and intense UV output make habitability unlikely regardless.

#### Planets

**b — "Kiln"** (Hot rocky, tidally disrupted)
- Orbital: 0.10 AU | Ecc: 0.06 | Period: 7.8 days
- Physical: 3.5 M⊕ | Radius: 1.42 R⊕ | Gravity: 1.74 g | Temp: 1,760 K
- Atmosphere: 0.002 atm — Na, K, SiO, Fe vapor | Scale height: 52 km
- Habitability: 1/100
- Science: 30
- Resources: Metal Ore (iron-rich), Regolith, Rare Earth Elements
- Solar Power: 4010.00×
> A scorched super-Earth orbiting deep in Vega's radiation field. At 0.10 AU from a star 40 times more luminous than Sol, Kiln receives roughly 4,000 times Earth's insolation. The dayside is a permanent ocean of magma — silicate rock liquefied to a depth of hundreds of kilometers, glowing white-hot and radiating enough thermal energy to be detectable from interstellar distances. The tenuous mineral atmosphere is continuously boiled off the magma surface: sodium, potassium, silicon monoxide, and iron vapor form a thin, incandescent shroud that condenses on the cooler nightside as a rain of metalite glass beads. Tidal locking keeps one face permanently in the furnace. The terminator is a wall of solidifying lava, cracking and collapsing as convective currents drag fresh molten rock to the surface. Kiln is a laboratory for studying magma ocean dynamics and atmospheric mass loss in real time — the planet is visibly evaporating, losing mass to Vega's radiation pressure and stellar wind at a rate that will strip it to a bare iron core within a billion years.

**c — "Empyrean"** (Warm gas giant)
- Orbital: 1.8 AU | Ecc: 0.10 | Period: 504 days
- Physical: 1.4 M_J (445 M⊕) | Radius: 1.30 R_J | Temp: 490 K (cloud-top)
- Atmosphere: Hydrogen-helium, silicate clouds, TiO/VO absorption | Scale height: 48 km
- Habitability: 0/100 (no solid surface)
- Science: 0
- Resources: Gas Giant Atmosphere
- Solar Power: 12.38×
> A warm Jupiter bathed in Vega's blue-white brilliance. Empyrean receives enough radiation to heat its upper atmosphere to nearly 500 K, placing it in the regime where titanium oxide and vanadium oxide absorb strongly in the optical, creating a stratospheric temperature inversion. The result is a planet with an unusual thermal profile — hotter at altitude than at depth — and a correspondingly puffed-up radius. The dayside atmosphere glows a deep bronze-orange, with silicate condensate clouds forming luminous bands at 0.1-bar pressure. The nightside is darker, with iron condensate rain falling into the depths. Wind speeds at the day-night terminator exceed 3 km/s, driving a superrotating equatorial jet. No moons — Vega's radiation pressure destabilizes satellite orbits at this distance. Empyrean's primary value is as a spectroscopic target: its inflated, hot atmosphere is ideal for atmospheric characterization studies.

**d — "Haldane"** (Cold ice giant, debris disk shepherd)
- Orbital: 8.5 AU | Ecc: 0.08 | Period: 5,190 days
- Physical: 28 M⊕ (0.088 M_J) | Radius: 4.0 R⊕ | Temp: 115 K (cloud-top)
- Atmosphere: Hydrogen-helium, methane, ammonia ice | Scale height: 30 km
- Habitability: 0/100 (no solid surface)
- Science: 0
- Resources: Gas Giant Atmosphere
- Solar Power: 0.56×
> A Neptune-class ice giant orbiting inside Vega's debris disk, likely responsible for sculpting the disk's inner edge through gravitational clearing. Haldane's atmosphere is standard ice-giant fare — hydrogen and helium over water-ammonia-methane ices — but the methane content gives it a striking teal-blue color that contrasts vividly against the diffuse glow of the surrounding debris disk when viewed from a distance. The disk itself is visible from Haldane's vicinity as a broad, luminous band across the sky, far brighter than any zodiacal light seen from Earth. Dust particles from the disk occasionally impact the upper atmosphere, producing transient bright streaks — a permanent meteor shower. Haldane serves as the gravitational anchor of the outer system and a fuel depot for expeditions studying the debris disk.

> **Moon: "Scar"** (Impact-scarred debris-disk moon, of Haldane)
> - Orbital: 200,000 km from Haldane | Period: 2.0 days
> - Physical: 0.004 M⊕ | Radius: 0.14 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.20 g | Temp: 105 K
> - Atmosphere: None
> - Habitability: 0/100
> - Science: 20
> - Resources: Regolith
> - Solar Power: 0.56×
> > The most battered moon in the catalog. Orbiting within Vega's debris disk, Scar endures impact rates millions of times higher than moons around mature stars. The surface is a palimpsest of overlapping craters — fresh ones with bright ejecta rays cutting across older, degraded basins. Several recent impacts are still thermally active: infrared observations reveal warm spots where newly exposed subsurface material radiates residual heat. The leading hemisphere is visibly more damaged than the trailing, a textbook demonstration of directional bombardment. Scar is being ground down in real time, its surface recycled by impacts on million-year timescales. In the deep future, if the debris disk dissipates, whatever remains of Scar will bear the scars (in name and fact) of Vega's violent youth.

### 39. Fomalhaut (Alpha Piscis Austrini)
- **Distance:** 25.1 ly
- **Galactic Position:** (32.6, -20968.5) ly
- **Galactic Velocity:** (262.1, -7.3) km/s
- **Galactic Orbit:** SMA: 25,371.2 ly | Ecc: 0.176 | ω: 281.3° | M₀: 352.2° | Period: 217.2 Myr
- **Spectral Type:** A4V (primary)
- **Stars:**
  - Fomalhaut A: Mass 1.92 M☉ | Radius 1.84 R☉ | Luminosity 16.6 L☉
  - Fomalhaut B (TW Piscis Austrini): Mass 0.73 M☉ | Radius 0.69 R☉ | Luminosity 0.19 L☉ (at ~57,000 AU)
  - Fomalhaut C (LP 876-10): Mass 0.18 M☉ | Radius 0.21 R☉ | Luminosity 0.004 L☉ (at ~158,000 AU)

The "Autumn Star" — one of the brightest stars in Earth's sky and the centerpiece of the most spectacular debris disk system within 50 light-years. Fomalhaut A is ringed by a complex, multi-belt debris structure: a warm inner belt at 8-15 AU, a gap from 15-130 AU sculpted by unseen planets, and the famous outer ring at 130-160 AU — a narrow, eccentric, sharply defined belt that screamed "planet" to astronomers for decades. The infamous Fomalhaut b detection in 2008 turned out to be an expanding dust cloud from a collision between planetesimals, not a planet. But the architecture of the debris belts demands planetary shepherds, and here they are.

#### Planets

**b — "Pyre"** (Hot super-Earth)
- Orbital: 0.15 AU | Ecc: 0.05 | Period: 13.7 days
- Physical: 4.2 M⊕ | Radius: 1.50 R⊕ | Gravity: 1.87 g | Temp: 1,190 K
- Atmosphere: 0.005 atm — Na, K, SiO₂ vapor, Fe | Scale height: 45 km
- Habitability: 1/100
- Science: 35
- Resources: Metal Ore (iron-rich), Regolith, Rare Earth Elements, Uranium Ore
- Solar Power: 737.78×
> A dense, iron-rich super-Earth deep in Fomalhaut A's radiation field. Pyre's dayside is a magma ocean under a tenuous rock-vapor atmosphere, glowing orange-white at over 1,100 K. The planet has likely migrated inward from a more distant formation site, losing its volatile envelope to photoevaporation along the way. What remains is a bare, refractory core — rich in iron, nickel, and calcium-aluminum inclusions. The nightside is marginally cooler (~800 K) but still incandescent, the heat redistributed by magma currents beneath a thin, dark basaltic crust that forms and founders within hours. Pyre is scientifically interesting as a stripped planetary core, offering a window into the deep interiors of larger worlds.

**c — "Galathorn"** (Cold gas giant, inner belt shepherd)
- Orbital: 12.0 AU | Ecc: 0.12 | Period: 18,400 days
- Physical: 3.2 M_J (1,017 M⊕) | Radius: 1.10 R_J | Temp: 140 K (cloud-top)
- Atmosphere: Hydrogen-helium, ammonia and water ice clouds | Scale height: 28 km
- Habitability: 0/100 (no solid surface)
- Science: 0
- Resources: Gas Giant Atmosphere
- Solar Power: 0.12×
> The inner shepherd — a massive gas giant whose gravity maintains the outer edge of the warm debris belt at 15 AU and clears the inner boundary of the great gap. Galathorn is a cold Jupiter, its cloud tops decorated with broad ammonia ice bands in cream and ochre. The planet is old enough to have cooled substantially, radiating only slightly more heat than it receives from Fomalhaut A. Its mass — over three Jupiters — makes it the dominant gravitational influence in the inner system. A system of at least four moons orbits Galathorn, the largest of which is substantial enough to warrant description.

> **Moon: "Alaris"** (Large icy moon, of Galathorn)
> - Orbital: 620,000 km from Galathorn | Period: 4.5 days
> - Physical: 0.018 M⊕ | Radius: 0.34 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.16 g | Temp: 130 K
> - Atmosphere: 0.0002 atm — N₂, trace CO₂ | Scale height: 24 km
> - Habitability: 5/100
> - Science: 40
> - Resources: Regolith, Water (subsurface brine)
> - Solar Power: 0.12×
> > A Titan-class icy moon with a thin nitrogen atmosphere maintained by cryovolcanic outgassing. Alaris has a differentiated interior — silicate core, ice mantle, possible subsurface brine layer — and shows extensive resurfacing from cryovolcanic activity. The surface is a patchwork of ancient, dark, cratered terrain and younger, smooth, pale ice plains. Nitrogen geysers erupt periodically from subsurface reservoirs, depositing frost in radial fans around the vent sites. From Alaris's surface, Galathorn fills a substantial arc of the sky, its banded clouds rotating visibly over hours, while the debris disk's warm inner belt glows as a diffuse band of light brighter than the Milky Way.

> **Moon: "Ironfast"** (Dense rocky inner moon, of Galathorn)
> - Orbital: 280,000 km from Galathorn | Period: 1.2 days
> - Physical: 0.006 M⊕ | Radius: 0.15 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.27 g | Temp: 135 K
> - Atmosphere: None
> - Habitability: 1/100
> - Science: 20
> - Resources: Metal Ore (iron-rich), Regolith
> - Solar Power: 0.12×
> > A small, dense, iron-rich moon in a close orbit — the heaviest body per unit volume in the Fomalhaut system outside of stellar remnants. Ironfast's surface is dark metallic basalt, polished by micrometeorite bombardment and fractured by tidal stresses. The iron content is extreme, nearly 70% by mass, giving the moon a surface gravity disproportionate to its small size. Mild tidal heating from Galathorn's enormous mass maintains a warm interior but no volcanism. Ironfast's origin is debated: either a stripped core from a shattered differentiated body, or a rare iron-dominated condensate from the metal-rich inner disk.

> **Moon: "Wayward"** (Distant retrograde captured body, of Galathorn)
> - Orbital: 3,500,000 km from Galathorn | Period: 30.0 days (retrograde)
> - Physical: 0.001 M⊕ | Radius: 0.08 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.16 g | Temp: 80 K
> - Atmosphere: None
> - Habitability: 0/100
> - Science: 15
> - Resources: Regolith
> - Solar Power: 0.12×
> > A dark, tumbling fragment on a distant retrograde orbit — one of the "at least four moons" detected around Galathorn. Wayward's orbit is highly inclined and retrograde, confirming its origin as a captured body from the debris disk. The surface is ancient, carbonaceous, and featureless. Its primary interest is dynamical: Wayward's chaotic orbit traces the boundary of Galathorn's gravitational sphere of influence, providing constraints on the planet's mass and the perturbative effect of the debris disk environment.

**d — "Sentinel"** (Saturn-mass outer ring shepherd)
- Orbital: 120 AU | Ecc: 0.10 | Period: 581,000 days (~1,590 years)
- Physical: 0.3 M_J (95 M⊕) | Radius: 0.85 R_J | Temp: 32 K (cloud-top)
- Atmosphere: Hydrogen-helium, methane ice, nitrogen frost hazes | Scale height: 18 km
- Habitability: 0/100 (no solid surface)
- Science: 0
- Resources: Gas Giant Atmosphere
- Solar Power: 0.00×
> The outer shepherd — a Saturn-mass planet orbiting just inside the famous outer debris ring, maintaining its sharp inner edge through gravitational resonance clearing. Sentinel is cold, dark, and remote: at 120 AU from Fomalhaut A, the star appears as a brilliant point only 40 times brighter than a full Moon seen from Earth. The planet's atmosphere is dominated by hydrogen and helium, with deep methane ice clouds giving it a dark blue color that fades to near-black in the dim light. From Sentinel's vicinity, the outer debris ring is spectacular — a sharp, bright arc curving across the sky, individual icy bodies visible as glinting points within the ring, the whole structure tilted and eccentric, sculpted by the very planet from which you observe it. Sentinel is a destination for debris disk science and a fuel depot for deep-system exploration.

> **Moon: "Shiver"** (Ultra-cold methane-frosted moon, of Sentinel)
> - Orbital: 250,000 km from Sentinel | Period: 3.0 days
> - Physical: 0.002 M⊕ | Radius: 0.11 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.17 g | Temp: 28 K
> - Atmosphere: None
> - Habitability: 0/100
> - Science: 20
> - Resources: Regolith, Water (water ice)
> - Solar Power: 0.00×
> > One of the coldest solid surfaces in the catalog. At 28 K, Shiver's surface is coated in methane frost over nitrogen ice over water ice — a layer cake of frozen volatiles, each condensing at a different temperature as the surface cooled over billions of years. The methane frost gives the surface a faintly translucent, bluish quality under artificial illumination; in natural light (what little reaches 120 AU from Fomalhaut A), the moon is nearly invisible. The landscape is ancient and featureless — at these temperatures, even geological timescales have effectively frozen. From Shiver's surface, the outer debris ring is the dominant visual feature: a bright, sharp arc spanning the sky, individual icy bodies glinting within it, the whole structure sculpted by the planet from which you stand.
