### 40. GJ 357
- **Distance:** 31.0 ly
- **Galactic Position:** (-6.6, -20987.9) ly
- **Galactic Velocity:** (218.8, -3.8) km/s
- **Galactic Orbit:** SMA: 20,976.9 ly | Ecc: 0.017 | ω: 2.7° | M₀: 269.2° | Period: 180.5 Myr
- **Spectral Type:** M2.5V
- **Stars:**
  - GJ 357: Mass 0.34 M☉ | Radius 0.33 R☉ | Luminosity 0.016 L☉

A relatively bright M-dwarf in Hydra with an unusually well-characterized planetary system. GJ 357 b was discovered by TESS as a transiting hot Earth — one of the nearest transiting planets to Sol. Two additional planets were confirmed by radial velocity, including GJ 357 d near the outer habitable zone. A quiet star with modest flare activity, making its outer planets more hospitable than those around more violent red dwarfs.

#### Planets

**b — "Scald"** (Hot transiting Earth, confirmed)
- Orbital: 0.035 AU | Ecc: 0.03 | Period: 3.93 days
- Physical: 1.84 M⊕ | Radius: 1.22 R⊕ | Gravity: 1.24 g | Temp: 525 K
- Atmosphere: None (stripped by stellar proximity)
- Habitability: 3/100
- Science: 30
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Rare Earth Elements
- Solar Power: 13.06×
> The transiting planet that put GJ 357 on the map. Scald orbits so close to its star that it completes a year every 3.9 days, tidally locked with one hemisphere staring into the red furnace. The dayside temperature exceeds 500 K — hot enough to glow faintly in thermal infrared. The nightside plunges below 100 K. No atmosphere survives at this distance; any volatiles were stripped long ago. The surface is dark, radiation-weathered basalt on the dayside and frost-dusted rock on the nightside. Scald's transit geometry makes it one of the best-characterized rocky exoplanets in Zone 3 — its density, size, and surface composition are known with unusual precision. A calibration target for instruments, not a destination for crews.

**c — "Ashward"** (Warm super-Earth, confirmed)
- Orbital: 0.061 AU | Ecc: 0.04 | Period: 9.12 days
- Physical: 3.40 M⊕ | Radius: 1.40 R⊕ | Gravity: 1.73 g | Temp: 401 K
- Atmosphere: 0.18 atm — CO₂ 82%, N₂ 12%, SO₂ 4% | Scale height: 7.8 km
- Habitability: 6/100
- Science: 40
- Resources: Metal Ore (rich), Regolith, Atmospheric CO2, Rare Earth Elements, Uranium Ore
- Solar Power: 4.30×
> A dense super-Earth with a thin, toxic CO₂ atmosphere maintained by volcanic outgassing. Ashward is too hot for liquid water but cool enough to retain a tenuous gaseous envelope. The surface is dominated by volcanic terrain — shield volcanoes, lava channels, and sulfurous fumaroles that vent continuously into the coppery sky. The high gravity (1.73 g) compresses the atmosphere to a thin, dense layer clinging to the surface. From orbit, the planet has a ruddy-brown appearance, the basaltic surface tinged by iron oxide and sulfur deposits. Mineral extraction — particularly platinum-group metals concentrated in volcanic intrusions — is the primary economic interest.

**d — "Amaranth"** (Rocky world near outer habitable zone, confirmed)
- Orbital: 0.204 AU | Ecc: 0.07 | Period: 55.7 days
- Physical: 6.1 M⊕ | Radius: 1.72 R⊕ | Gravity: 2.06 g | Temp: 220 K
- Atmosphere: 1.8 atm — N₂ 52%, CO₂ 34%, H₂O 8%, Ar 4% | Scale height: 5.2 km
- Habitability: 30/100
- Science: 100
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Water (polar ice caps, subsurface brine aquifers), Lithium Ore, Atmospheric CO2, Rare Earth Elements, Uranium Ore
- Solar Power: 0.38×
> The prize of the GJ 357 system. Amaranth sits near the outer edge of the habitable zone, receiving about a third of the insolation Earth does from Sol. The thick atmosphere compensates: a strong CO₂ greenhouse pushes surface temperatures to approximately 255 K with regional variation from 240-270 K. This is cold — permanently below freezing across most of the surface — but not fatally so. Equatorial lowlands reach 270 K during local noon, and subsurface brine aquifers remain liquid year-round. The planet is massive enough to retain a substantial atmosphere and drive active plate tectonics, with young mountain ranges and deep rift valleys crossing the equatorial band. Water ice covers 45% of the surface as extensive polar caps and highland glaciers. The atmosphere is unbreathable (34% CO₂), and the gravity is punishing at 2.06 g, but the pressure and temperature are within engineering tolerances. A difficult colony site, but a possible one. The name reflects the planet's persistence at the edge of habitability — always almost, never quite.

**e — "Glint"** (Cold outer rocky)
- Orbital: 0.42 AU | Ecc: 0.09 | Period: 165 days
- Physical: 1.2 M⊕ | Radius: 1.06 R⊕ | Gravity: 1.07 g | Temp: 138 K
- Atmosphere: 0.008 atm — CO₂ 88%, N₂ 8% | Scale height: 9.5 km
- Habitability: 7/100
- Science: 30
- Resources: Regolith, Water (ice)
- Solar Power: 0.09×
> A cold, nearly airless world beyond the habitable zone. Glint's surface is a frozen expanse of CO₂ ice and water ice, cratered and ancient. The thin atmosphere provides no meaningful greenhouse warming. Temperatures hover around 138 K — cold enough for CO₂ to freeze out at the poles, creating seasonal cycles of sublimation and deposition. The planet's name comes from the way its icy surface catches Fomalhaut's light (visible from GJ 357 as a moderately bright star) and the distant Sun (a faint point) — brief specular reflections from smooth ice patches that flash like signals. A refueling waypoint with accessible water ice, nothing more.

### 41. Wolf 1069
- **Distance:** 31.2 ly
- **Galactic Position:** (32.6, -21022.0) ly
- **Galactic Velocity:** (230.8, -22.2) km/s
- **Galactic Orbit:** SMA: 22,252.9 ly | Ecc: 0.112 | ω: 336.1° | M₀: 305.3° | Period: 191.1 Myr
- **Spectral Type:** M5V
- **Stars:**
  - Wolf 1069: Mass 0.17 M☉ | Radius 0.19 R☉ | Luminosity 0.0035 L☉

An unusually quiet red dwarf. Wolf 1069 rotates slowly — a period of 150-170 days — which suppresses magnetic activity and flare production to levels far below typical M-dwarfs. This stellar calm is what makes its habitable-zone planet one of the most remarkable discoveries in Zone 3: a world where life has not merely survived, but colonized the sky.

#### Planets

**b — "Zephyria"** (Temperate rocky — LIFE WORLD)
- Orbital: 0.067 AU | Ecc: 0.05 | Period: 15.6 days
- Physical: 1.26 M⊕ | Radius: 1.08 R⊕ | Gravity: 1.08 g | Temp: 250 K (equilibrium); surface 235-290 K
- Atmosphere: 0.85 atm — N₂ 64%, CO₂ 21%, H₂O 6%, Ar 3%, O₂ 0.3%, trace fluorocarbons (CF₄, C₂F₆) | Scale height: 7.5 km
- Habitability: 38/100
- Science: 350
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Water (dayside seas), Atmospheric CO2, Rare Earth Elements
- Solar Power: 0.78×
> A tidally locked Earth-mass world in the habitable zone of one of the quietest M-dwarfs known. Zephyria's substellar hemisphere receives constant, gentle illumination from its dim red sun — a permanent ruddy noon over a landscape of shallow, mineral-rich seas and volcanic archipelagos. The antistellar hemisphere is a frozen waste, locked in permanent night. Between them, the terminator ring hosts powerful convective winds that redistribute heat, maintaining a broad temperate zone where surface temperatures range from 260-290 K.
>
> The atmosphere is thick enough for meaningful greenhouse warming and thin enough for manageable surface pressure. Liquid water is abundant on the dayside. The CO₂ content (21%) makes the air unbreathable to humans. All of this is within the normal range for M-dwarf habitable zone worlds — unremarkable.
>
> What is not unremarkable is the fluorine.
>
> Atmospheric spectroscopy detected tetrafluoromethane (CF₄) and hexafluoroethane (C₂F₆) at concentrations of 0.8 and 0.2 parts per million, respectively. These are stable, long-lived compounds — but they have no plausible geological source on a world this size. Fluorine is a reactive element that should be locked in mineral fluorides (fluorite, fluorapatite), not floating in the atmosphere as perfluorocarbons. Volcanic outgassing can produce trace hydrogen fluoride, but not saturated fluorocarbons. The molecules were there, and they should not have been.
>
> The source is biological.
>
> **Aeroplankton** — single-celled organisms, oblate spheroids roughly 5-15 micrometers in diameter, that live suspended in the cloud layer between 4 and 12 km altitude on the dayside. They do not fly — they float, buoyed by internal gas vacuoles filled with molecular hydrogen (produced metabolically), which reduces their bulk density below that of the surrounding atmosphere. They drift in slow, convective circuits: rising in the warm updrafts over the substellar point, carried outward by high-altitude winds, sinking in the cooler air toward the terminator, then recirculated inward at lower altitude.
>
> Their membranes are extraordinary. Instead of the lipid bilayers used by all known terrestrial life, Aeroplankton enclose themselves in fluoropolymer membranes — long-chain perfluorinated carbon compounds cross-linked into a flexible, chemically inert sheet. This membrane is essentially biological Teflon. It is resistant to UV radiation, chemically stable against the oxidizing conditions of the upper atmosphere, impermeable to water loss, and transparent to the infrared wavelengths that dominate Wolf 1069's stellar output. The fluoropolymer membrane is what allows these organisms to survive in the cloud layer, where UV flux (though low by solar standards) and desiccation would destroy conventional lipid membranes within hours.
>
> Their metabolism is atmospheric chemistry. Aeroplankton catalyze reactions between trace atmospheric gases — hydrogen sulfide, carbon monoxide, and water vapor — using fluorine-containing enzymes (fluorinases) that have no terrestrial analog. The enzymes use fluorine atoms in their active sites where terrestrial enzymes use oxygen or nitrogen, exploiting fluorine's extreme electronegativity to drive reactions that would otherwise require high temperatures. The metabolic waste products include the perfluorocarbons that first betrayed their presence, along with elemental sulfur (which precipitates as fine yellow dust, contributing to the faint haze in the upper atmosphere) and molecular hydrogen (which fills the gas vacuoles and eventually escapes to space).
>
> From orbit, the Aeroplankton are visible as faint iridescent patches in the dayside cloud layer — not bright enough to see in casual imaging, but detectable in polarized light. The fluoropolymer membranes scatter light with a slight spectral dispersion, creating rainbow-fringed regions that shift and drift with the atmospheric circulation. At dawn and dusk along the terminator, when starlight passes through the cloud layer at a shallow angle, these patches briefly intensify into shimmering, opalescent bands — the most visually striking biosignature in Zone 3.
>
> The Aeroplankton represent a fourth independent origin of life, and the most biochemically alien yet encountered. Fluorine-based biochemistry was considered theoretically possible but practically unlikely — fluorine's reactivity was thought to preclude its incorporation into stable biological molecules. Zephyria's organisms prove otherwise. Their fluoropolymer membranes, fluorinase enzymes, and perfluorocarbon waste products constitute an entirely novel chemical basis for life, expanding the boundaries of what biology can do with the periodic table. The implications for prebiotic chemistry on fluorine-rich worlds are profound.
>
> Zephyria is not comfortable for humans. The CO₂ is lethal, the gravity is Earth-normal, and the tidal locking creates extreme weather at the terminator. But the stellar environment is calm, the water is accessible, and the biology is there to study. A pressurized research station on the dayside, positioned to observe the Aeroplankton in their cloud-layer habitat, would be straightforward to construct. Contamination risk is low — terrestrial organisms cannot survive in the fluorine-rich cloud layer, and the Aeroplankton cannot survive at the surface. Two biospheres, separated by altitude, sharing a sky.

**c — "Duskfall"** (Cold rocky outer)
- Orbital: 0.14 AU | Ecc: 0.08 | Period: 48 days
- Physical: 0.52 M⊕ | Radius: 0.83 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.75 g | Temp: 148 K
- Atmosphere: 0.003 atm — CO₂ | Scale height: 10.8 km
- Habitability: 5/100
- Science: 30
- Resources: Regolith, Water (permafrost)
- Solar Power: 0.18×
> A small, cold world beyond the habitable zone. Duskfall's surface is a cratered expanse of frozen regolith dusted with CO₂ frost. No geological activity is evident — the planet is too small to retain internal heat. Water ice exists in permanently shadowed craters and as subsurface permafrost. Duskfall serves as a contamination-free staging area for Zephyria research operations — far enough from the life-bearing world to avoid cross-contamination, close enough for easy transit. The surface offers a stark view of Wolf 1069 as a dim red ember, barely illuminating the landscape.

**d — "Cairn"** (Frozen dwarf)
- Orbital: 0.30 AU | Ecc: 0.12 | Period: 148 days
- Physical: 0.08 M⊕ | Radius: 0.44 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.41 g | Temp: 89 K
- Atmosphere: None
- Habitability: 1/100
- Science: 20
- Resources: Regolith, Water (water ice)
- Solar Power: 0.04×
> A Ceres-class ice dwarf at the outer edge of the system. Cairn is a ball of water ice and carbonaceous rock, dark and ancient. Its surface bears the scars of billions of years of micrometeorite bombardment without geological renewal. A waypoint and water depot, its only distinction being its position in a system made famous by the world closer in.

### 42. HD 260655
- **Distance:** 33.0 ly
- **Galactic Position:** (19.0, -21024.3) ly
- **Galactic Velocity:** (223.9, -8.8) km/s
- **Galactic Orbit:** SMA: 21,505.6 ly | Ecc: 0.046 | ω: 333.2° | M₀: 301.4° | Period: 184.8 Myr
- **Spectral Type:** M0V
- **Stars:**
  - HD 260655: Mass 0.44 M☉ | Radius 0.44 R☉ | Luminosity 0.033 L☉

A bright M-dwarf in Gemini with two confirmed transiting rocky planets, both too close to their star for liquid water. HD 260655 is moderately active but not a violent flare star, and its relative brightness makes the system an excellent target for atmospheric characterization. The transiting geometry means both confirmed planets have well-measured radii and masses — rare precision for rocky worlds.

#### Planets

**b — "Cinnabar"** (Hot rocky, confirmed transiting)
- Orbital: 0.029 AU | Ecc: 0.02 | Period: 2.77 days
- Physical: 2.14 M⊕ | Radius: 1.24 R⊕ | Gravity: 1.39 g | Temp: 710 K
- Atmosphere: None (stripped)
- Habitability: 2/100
- Science: 30
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Rare Earth Elements
- Solar Power: 39.24×
> The inner planet, orbiting so close to HD 260655 that it completes a year in under three days. Cinnabar is tidally locked, airless, and baked. The dayside temperature exceeds 700 K — hot enough for surface minerals to undergo thermal decomposition, releasing sulfur and metal vapors that briefly cling to the surface before being swept away by the stellar wind. The result is a planet with a faintly ruddy surface — iron and sulfur oxides staining the dark basalt like rust on iron. The name is apt: cinnabar, the mercury sulfide mineral, shares the same bloody-red hue. Transit spectroscopy confirms no atmospheric features — the planet is bare rock, its spectrum a pure thermal emission curve.

**c — "Greyshale"** (Warm rocky, confirmed transiting)
- Orbital: 0.047 AU | Ecc: 0.03 | Period: 5.71 days
- Physical: 3.09 M⊕ | Radius: 1.53 R⊕ | Gravity: 1.32 g | Temp: 557 K
- Atmosphere: 0.04 atm — CO₂ 90%, SO₂ 6%, trace Na | Scale height: 10.2 km
- Habitability: 4/100
- Science: 35
- Resources: Metal Ore (iron-rich), Regolith, Atmospheric CO2, Rare Earth Elements, Uranium Ore
- Solar Power: 14.94×
> Slightly farther out and slightly cooler, Greyshale retains a wisp of volcanic atmosphere that transit observations have partially characterized. The thin CO₂-SO₂ envelope is maintained by continuous volcanic outgassing — the planet's interior is kept molten by a combination of residual formation heat and tidal dissipation. Surface features include vast basaltic plains fractured by extensional rift systems, and active volcanic regions concentrated along the terminator where thermal stresses are greatest. The planet's density suggests an iron-rich composition with a proportionally large core. Greyshale's atmosphere is too thin and too hot for habitability, but it provides valuable data on volcanic atmospheric retention around M-dwarf planets.

**d — "Wintermere"** (Rocky world near habitable zone)
- Orbital: 0.13 AU | Ecc: 0.05 | Period: 28.4 days
- Physical: 1.5 M⊕ | Radius: 1.12 R⊕ | Gravity: 1.20 g | Temp: 268 K
- Atmosphere: 0.38 atm — N₂ 58%, CO₂ 30%, H₂O 7%, Ar 3% | Scale height: 7.2 km
- Habitability: 40/100
- Science: 120
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Water (equatorial lakes and seas), Lithium Ore, Atmospheric CO2, Rare Earth Elements, Uranium Ore
- Solar Power: 1.95×
> The most interesting planet in the system. Wintermere sits in the habitable zone, and conditions are genuinely promising: surface temperature averages 280 K with greenhouse warming, and liquid water exists in equatorial basins — cold, mineral-laden lakes and shallow seas covering roughly 20% of the surface. The atmosphere is unbreathable (30% CO₂) but the pressure and temperature are manageable. The planet is not tidally locked — its orbital period of 28 days and mild eccentricity allow a slow rotation that creates day-night cycles, reducing the extreme temperature gradients that plague tidally locked worlds. No life has been detected. The lakes are sterile, their waters a pale greenish-brown from dissolved iron and magnesium salts. With atmospheric conversion, Wintermere could support a pressurized colony — the gravity is near-Earth, the water is accessible, and the stellar environment is stable enough for long-term habitation.

**e — "Farpost"** (Cold mini-Neptune)
- Orbital: 0.35 AU | Ecc: 0.07 | Period: 105 days
- Physical: 9.5 M⊕ | Radius: 2.5 R⊕ | Temp: 128 K (cloud-top)
- Atmosphere: Hydrogen-helium, water and ammonia ice clouds | Scale height: 18 km
- Habitability: 0/100 (no solid surface)
- Science: 0
- Resources: Gas Giant Atmosphere
- Solar Power: 0.27×
> A small ice giant at the outer edge of the system. Farpost is enveloped in pale, hazy clouds of water ice over deeper ammonia compounds, giving it a washed-out blue-gray appearance. Its hydrogen-helium envelope is thin relative to its mass, suggesting it formed late or lost envelope mass to photoevaporation. A fuel depot and gravitational anchor for the outer system.

### 43. Pollux (Beta Geminorum)
- **Distance:** 33.8 ly
- **Galactic Position:** (16.6, -21025.1) ly
- **Galactic Velocity:** (204.2, 12.4) km/s
- **Galactic Orbit:** SMA: 19,729.4 ly | Ecc: 0.089 | ω: 129.0° | M₀: 134.3° | Period: 170.1 Myr
- **Spectral Type:** K0III (orange giant)
- **Stars:**
  - Pollux: Mass 1.91 M☉ | Radius 8.8 R☉ | Luminosity 33 L☉

The brightest star in Gemini and the closest giant star to Sol with a confirmed exoplanet. Pollux is an evolved orange giant — a star that was once a hot A-type main-sequence star, now swollen to nearly nine times the Sun's radius as it burns helium in its core. The expansion has pushed the habitable zone out to ~5 AU and heated the inner system to incandescence. Any planets that once orbited within 1-2 AU are now engulfed or baking in the expanding stellar envelope. The confirmed planet, Thestias, orbits at a safe distance — for now.

#### Planets

**b / Thestias — "Thestias"** (Gas giant, confirmed)
- Orbital: 1.64 AU | Ecc: 0.02 | Period: 589 days
- Physical: 2.3 M_J (731 M⊕) | Radius: 1.12 R_J | Temp: 410 K (cloud-top)
- Atmosphere: Hydrogen-helium, water vapor, silicate haze at depth | Scale height: 35 km
- Habitability: 0/100 (no solid surface)
- Science: 0
- Resources: Gas Giant Atmosphere
- Solar Power: 12.27×
> The confirmed planet of Pollux, a massive warm Jupiter orbiting at what was once a comfortable distance from its main-sequence parent — and is now dangerously close to the bloated giant. Pollux's luminosity of 33 L☉ heats Thestias's cloud tops to 410 K, creating a complex atmospheric chemistry with water vapor at altitude and silicate condensates in the deeper layers. The planet's atmosphere shows broad banding in cream, gold, and russet — warmer and more vivid than Jupiter's palette, heated by the orange giant's radiation. As Pollux continues to evolve, it will expand further. Within 100 million years, the star's radius will approach Thestias's orbital distance, and the planet will spiral inward through tidal drag, its atmosphere stripped layer by layer until only a bare core remains — a future chthonian planet. For now, Thestias endures, a gas giant slowly being cooked by its dying star. From the cloud tops, Pollux fills an enormous arc of the sky — a vast, warm, orange disc that would span roughly 5 degrees, ten times the apparent size of Sol from Earth.

> **Moon: "Requiem"** (Melting icy moon, doomed, of Thestias)
> - Orbital: 700,000 km from Thestias | Period: 4.0 days
> - Physical: 0.025 M⊕ | Radius: 0.38 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.17 g | Temp: 380 K
> - Atmosphere: 0.02 atm — H₂O 60%, CO₂ 25%, N₂ 10% | Scale height: 18 km (and growing)
> - Habitability: 2/100
> - Science: 20
> - Resources: Regolith, Water (meltwater, sublimating ice)
> - Solar Power: 12.27×
> > A world in the act of dying. Requiem was once a Ganymede-class icy moon — frozen, quiet, ancient. That was before Pollux began to swell. As the star expanded from a main-sequence A-type to its current orange giant state, its luminosity increased a hundredfold, and the ice that had been stable for billions of years began to sublime. Today, Requiem is actively melting. The surface is a chaotic landscape of collapsing ice cliffs, steaming crevasses, and dark pools of meltwater that flash-evaporate in the thin, growing atmosphere. Water vapor and carbon dioxide — liberated from the warming ice — are building an atmosphere in real time, thickening measurably over decades. The mass loss is visible from orbit: a comet-like tail of water vapor and ice particles streams behind Requiem in its orbit, fed by the continuous sublimation of its surface. Within 50 million years — perhaps sooner — the ice will be gone entirely, leaving behind a bare, desiccated rocky core. Requiem is a time-lapse of planetary death, a monument to the impermanence of ice in the face of stellar evolution. Its name is its epitaph.

**c — "Emberglow"** (Warm rocky remnant, inner)
- Orbital: 0.55 AU | Ecc: 0.04 | Period: 109 days
- Physical: 0.6 M⊕ | Radius: 0.88 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.77 g | Temp: 1,070 K
- Atmosphere: 0.001 atm — Na, K, trace Fe vapor | Scale height: 48 km
- Habitability: 1/100
- Science: 35
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Rare Earth Elements
- Solar Power: 109.09×
> A dying world. Emberglow once orbited at a comfortable distance from the main-sequence Pollux, perhaps in or near the habitable zone. That was billions of years ago. As Pollux expanded into a giant, its luminosity increased a hundredfold, incinerating any atmosphere, boiling away any oceans, and melting the surface to slag. Today Emberglow is a glowing cinder at 1,070 K, its surface a slowly churning magma ocean under the bloated orange disc of its star. The tenuous atmosphere of metal vapor is being steadily stripped by the giant's enhanced stellar wind. Emberglow is not long for this universe — within tens of millions of years, Pollux will expand to engulf it entirely. It is a world in the process of being consumed by its parent star, a reminder that stellar evolution is not kind to close-in planets.

**d — "Frosthollow"** (Cold gas giant, outer)
- Orbital: 6.5 AU | Ecc: 0.15 | Period: 4,350 days
- Physical: 0.8 M_J (254 M⊕) | Radius: 1.0 R_J | Temp: 150 K (cloud-top)
- Atmosphere: Hydrogen-helium, ammonia and methane ice clouds | Scale height: 26 km
- Habitability: 0/100 (no solid surface)
- Science: 0
- Resources: Gas Giant Atmosphere
- Solar Power: 0.78×
> A cold Jupiter in the outer system, far enough from Pollux to remain relatively unaffected by the star's expansion — at least for now. Frosthollow receives about the same insolation Earth gets from Sol, but at this distance from the giant star, the radiation environment is mild. The atmosphere shows typical cold-Jupiter banding: ammonia ice cirrus over deeper water clouds, with methane absorption darkening the blue-green tint. Frosthollow formed when Pollux was still on the main sequence and has survived the transition to giant status by virtue of distance. Its orbit is slowly widening as Pollux loses mass through its stellar wind — in the deep future, Frosthollow will drift outward into interstellar space, an orphan planet carrying its moons into the dark.

> **Moon: "Clinker"** (Small rocky moon, of Frosthollow)
> - Orbital: 250,000 km from Frosthollow | Period: 1.3 days
> - Physical: 0.004 M⊕ | Radius: 0.14 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.20 g | Temp: 140 K
> - Atmosphere: None
> - Habitability: 1/100
> - Science: 20
> - Resources: Metal Ore (iron oxide), Regolith
> - Solar Power: 0.78×
> > A small, dense, rocky body in a close orbit — the relic core of a differentiated moon that lost its icy mantle when Pollux expanded and the local temperature regime shifted upward. Clinker's surface is dark basalt and iron oxide, cratered and inert. The moon that exists today is roughly half the volume of the moon that formed — the other half sublimated and escaped as Pollux brightened, leaving behind this dense, hardened remnant. A geological record of atmospheric stripping in miniature.

> **Moon: "Hollowmere"** (Large icy moon with stellarly-heated ocean, of Frosthollow)
> - Orbital: 550,000 km from Frosthollow | Period: 3.5 days
> - Physical: 0.018 M⊕ | Radius: 0.34 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.16 g | Temp: 145 K (surface); ~270 K (subsurface)
> - Atmosphere: 0.001 atm — N₂ 85%, CO₂ 10%, trace H₂O | Scale height: 20 km
> - Habitability: 10/100
> - Science: 70
> - Resources: Regolith, Water (subsurface ocean)
> - Solar Power: 0.78×
> > An unusual moon with a subsurface ocean maintained not by tidal heating but by stellar radiation — Pollux's 33 solar luminosities provide enough warmth at 6.5 AU to heat the ice shell from the outside in. The surface temperature of 145 K is warmer than any ice moon at equivalent distance from a main-sequence star. Beneath a 30 km ice shell, a liquid water ocean exists at ~270 K, fed by heat conducting inward through the thinning ice. As Pollux continues to expand, the ice will thin further, and Hollowmere may eventually become an ocean world — exposed liquid water at the surface. For now, it is a unique case study in stellar-driven ocean formation.

> **Moon: "Furthest"** (Tiny captured irregular with widening orbit, of Frosthollow)
> - Orbital: 2,500,000 km from Frosthollow | Period: 20.0 days
> - Physical: 0.001 M⊕ | Radius: 0.08 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.16 g | Temp: 100 K
> - Atmosphere: None
> - Habitability: 0/100
> - Science: 15
> - Resources: Regolith
> - Solar Power: 0.78×
> > A tiny, dark body in a distant orbit that is measurably widening as Pollux loses mass through its stellar wind. As the star sheds material, its gravitational grip weakens, and Frosthollow's orbit expands — carrying Furthest with it, but at a rate that also loosens Frosthollow's hold on its outermost moon. Furthest is slowly spiraling outward, gaining orbital energy from the three-body mass-loss dynamics. In the deep future, when Frosthollow becomes an orphan planet, Furthest may be the first moon to break free — escaping into the dark ahead of its parent.

### 44. L 98-59
- **Distance:** 34.6 ly
- **Galactic Position:** (-10.5, -20987.8) ly
- **Galactic Velocity:** (219.6, -30.3) km/s
- **Galactic Orbit:** SMA: 21,236.5 ly | Ecc: 0.137 | ω: 3.0° | M₀: 282.7° | Period: 182.6 Myr
- **Spectral Type:** M3V
- **Stars:**
  - L 98-59: Mass 0.31 M☉ | Radius 0.30 R☉ | Luminosity 0.011 L☉

A compact M-dwarf system in Volans that has become one of the most intensively studied planetary systems in the sky. Four transiting planets were confirmed by TESS and radial velocity, with JWST detecting sulfur-rich volcanic atmospheres on the inner worlds — the first direct detection of volcanic activity on exoplanets. A fifth planet in the habitable zone was confirmed in 2025, making L 98-59 one of the richest known systems around a small star.

#### Planets

**b — "Brimstone"** (Ultra-short-period volcanic, confirmed)
- Orbital: 0.022 AU | Ecc: 0.01 | Period: 2.25 days
- Physical: 0.40 M⊕ | Radius: 0.85 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.55 g | Temp: 600 K
- Atmosphere: 0.008 atm — SO₂ 62%, SO₃ 18%, Na 8%, trace S₂ | Scale height: 18 km
- Habitability: 2/100
- Science: 50
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Rare Earth Elements, Uranium Ore
- Solar Power: 22.73×
> The innermost and smallest planet, a volcanic marble orbiting its star every two days. JWST's detection of sulfur dioxide and sulfur trioxide in transit spectroscopy was a landmark — the first unambiguous identification of volcanic atmospheric species on a rocky exoplanet. Brimstone's surface is a patchwork of active lava flows and sulfur-crusted basalt, the entire world heated by tidal dissipation and stellar proximity to a state of continuous eruption. The sulfur-rich atmosphere is thin but spectrally loud — absorption features dominate the transit signal despite the low surface pressure. The planet is slowly losing mass to atmospheric escape, the sulfur compounds photodissociated by UV and swept away by the stellar wind, only to be replenished by the next eruption. A world balanced between volcanic creation and stellar destruction.

**c — "Calx"** (Volcanic super-Earth, confirmed)
- Orbital: 0.032 AU | Ecc: 0.02 | Period: 3.69 days
- Physical: 2.22 M⊕ | Radius: 1.35 R⊕ | Gravity: 1.22 g | Temp: 510 K
- Atmosphere: 0.12 atm — SO₂ 45%, CO₂ 38%, H₂O 8%, S₂ 5% | Scale height: 10.5 km
- Habitability: 3/100
- Science: 50
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Atmospheric CO2, Uranium Ore
- Solar Power: 10.74×
> Larger and more geologically vigorous than Brimstone. Calx has enough mass to retain a more substantial volcanic atmosphere, and JWST spectra reveal a complex chemistry of sulfur and carbon oxides — a world exhaling through a thousand fumaroles. The surface is hot, cratered by volcanic calderas rather than impacts (the thin atmosphere provides no protection, but the surface is too young to retain craters — volcanic resurfacing erases them within thousands of years). The name, from the Latin for limestone or calcium oxide, reflects the planet's calcium-rich surface mineralogy detected in the transit spectrum. A natural laboratory for sulfur photochemistry.

**d — "Crucis"** (Warm rocky, confirmed)
- Orbital: 0.050 AU | Ecc: 0.03 | Period: 7.45 days
- Physical: 1.94 M⊕ | Radius: 1.52 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.84 g | Temp: 384 K
- Atmosphere: 0.22 atm — CO₂ 58%, N₂ 25%, H₂O 10%, SO₂ 4% | Scale height: 9.8 km
- Habitability: 10/100
- Science: 40
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Atmospheric CO2, Uranium Ore
- Solar Power: 4.40×
> The third planet shows the transition from sulfur-dominated to carbon-dominated atmospheric chemistry. At 0.05 AU, Crucis is far enough from the star that volcanic sulfur compounds are partially converted to CO₂ and water by photochemical processes. The atmosphere is thicker and more stable than the inner planets, supported by a larger mass and lower stellar irradiation. Surface conditions are hot (400 K with greenhouse) but not magma-ocean hot — solid basaltic crust covers the planet, fractured by tectonic rifts and dotted with active volcanoes. The density is anomalously low for its mass, suggesting a water-rich interior or a thick volatile mantle — this may be a "steam world" with a supercritical water layer beneath the rocky surface.

**e — "Halide"** (Rocky, confirmed non-transiting)
- Orbital: 0.074 AU | Ecc: 0.05 | Period: 12.8 days
- Physical: 3.06 M⊕ | Radius: 1.38 R⊕ | Gravity: 1.61 g | Temp: 310 K
- Atmosphere: 0.55 atm — CO₂ 42%, N₂ 38%, H₂O 12%, Ar 4%, SO₂ 2% | Scale height: 7.0 km
- Habitability: 18/100
- Science: 35
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Lithium Ore, Atmospheric CO2, Rare Earth Elements, Uranium Ore
- Solar Power: 2.01×
> The first non-transiting planet, detected by radial velocity and confirmed by transit-timing variations of the inner planets. Halide is a dense super-Earth at the inner edge of the habitable zone — warm but no longer scorching. Surface temperatures average 330 K with greenhouse warming: hot, uncomfortable, but below the boiling point of water at this atmospheric pressure. Liquid water could theoretically exist in the polar regions or in deep valleys where pressure raises the boiling point. The atmosphere is the most Earth-like in the system — nitrogen and CO₂ dominate, with significant water vapor. The sulfur dioxide content decreases with distance from the star as volcanic activity moderates. Named for its unusual halogen chemistry: fluorine and chlorine mineral salts are abundant in the surface crust, a legacy of the system's halogen-rich protoplanetary disk.

**f — "Amaryllis"** (Habitable zone rocky, confirmed 2025)
- Orbital: 0.121 AU | Ecc: 0.04 | Period: 23.2 days
- Physical: 2.4 M⊕ | Radius: 1.26 R⊕ | Gravity: 1.51 g | Temp: 240 K
- Atmosphere: 0.72 atm — N₂ 60%, CO₂ 24%, H₂O 9%, Ar 4%, O₂ 0.4% | Scale height: 6.8 km
- Habitability: 42/100
- Science: 120
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Water (polar ice, brine seas), Lithium Ore, Atmospheric CO2, Rare Earth Elements, Uranium Ore
- Solar Power: 0.75×
> The 2025 confirmation of L 98-59 f made headlines: a rocky planet in the habitable zone with a detectable atmosphere. Amaryllis sits near the center of the habitable zone, with greenhouse warming pushing surface temperatures to 265 K — cold, but above freezing in equatorial lowlands where shallow brine seas persist. The atmosphere is substantial, with a pressure comfortable for pressurized habitation and enough water vapor for cloud formation and precipitation. Trace oxygen (0.4%) is present but photochemical, not biological — extensive searches have found no biosignatures. The surface is geologically active: young volcanic terrain dominates, with few impact craters and evidence of recent tectonic resurfacing. Ice caps cover the polar regions, and seasonal CO₂ frost cycles drive mild atmospheric pressure variations. Named Amaryllis for the blooming of possibility it represents — the first genuinely habitable-zone world confirmed in the L 98-59 system, with conditions that teeter on the edge of habitability.

### 45. Denebola (Beta Leonis)
- **Distance:** 35.9 ly
- **Galactic Position:** (-9.9, -21004.0) ly
- **Galactic Velocity:** (200.3, 4.7) km/s
- **Galactic Orbit:** SMA: 19,346.6 ly | Ecc: 0.089 | ω: 104.2° | M₀: 163.1° | Period: 166.9 Myr
- **Spectral Type:** A3V
- **Stars:**
  - Denebola: Mass 1.75 M☉ | Radius 1.73 R☉ | Luminosity 15.0 L☉

A hot, young A-type star in Leo's tail, surrounded by a prominent infrared-excess debris disk — a "Vega-class" disk at an estimated age of 100-300 Myr. Denebola is younger than Vega and rotating rapidly (though not as extremely), and its planetary system is correspondingly immature: surfaces are fresh, geological activity is intense, and the debris disk is actively evolving. No confirmed planets existed before direct imaging campaigns, but the disk structure demands shepherding bodies.

#### Planets

**b — "Ashcrown"** (Hot rocky)
- Orbital: 0.20 AU | Ecc: 0.06 | Period: 17.6 days
- Physical: 2.8 M⊕ | Radius: 1.30 R⊕ | Gravity: 1.66 g | Temp: 1,230 K
- Atmosphere: 0.003 atm — Na, K, SiO, Mg vapor | Scale height: 48 km
- Habitability: 1/100
- Science: 30
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Rare Earth Elements, Uranium Ore
- Solar Power: 375.00×
> A scorched super-Earth in the inner system, baking under Denebola's blue-white glare. At 0.20 AU from a star 15 times more luminous than Sol, Ashcrown's dayside is a permanent magma ocean. The tenuous rock-vapor atmosphere is continuously replenished by evaporation from the molten surface and stripped by photodissociation and stellar wind — a cycle of creation and destruction measured in weeks. The planet is young, still differentiating, its interior a churning mix of silicate and iron that has not yet fully separated into core and mantle. From orbit, the dayside glows white-hot, the nightside is a dark crescent of barely-solidified basalt, and the terminator is a jagged line of lava fountains and collapsing crust.

**c — "Paladin"** (Warm gas giant)
- Orbital: 3.5 AU | Ecc: 0.08 | Period: 2,810 days
- Physical: 2.0 M_J (636 M⊕) | Radius: 1.20 R_J | Temp: 260 K (cloud-top)

- Habitability: 0/100 (no solid surface)
- Science: 0

> A warm Jupiter orbiting near the habitable zone — though no solid surface exists to make that relevant. Paladin's cloud-top temperature of 260 K places it in the water-cloud regime: extensive white cloud decks of water ice dominate the upper atmosphere, giving the planet a brilliant, high-albedo appearance. The banding is young and chaotic — not yet settled into the stable zonal jet patterns of older gas giants. Storm systems form and dissipate rapidly, their lifetimes measured in weeks. Paladin's gravity sculpts the inner edge of the debris disk, clearing a gap and accumulating a modest ring system of captured dust particles.

> **Moon: "Vigil"** (Rocky-icy moon, of Paladin)
> - Orbital: 480,000 km from Paladin | Period: 3.2 days
> - Physical: 0.012 M⊕ | Radius: 0.27 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.16 g | Temp: 250 K

> - Habitability: 8/100
> - Science: 35
> - Resources: Regolith, Water (cryovolcanic water vapor)
> - Solar Power: 1.22×
> > A rocky-icy moon heated by a combination of stellar irradiation and mild tidal forces from Paladin. Vigil's surface shows active cryovolcanism — geysers of water vapor and CO₂ erupt from fracture zones, maintaining a wisp of atmosphere. The surface temperature of 250 K puts it just below freezing, but equatorial regions may experience transient liquid water during peak tidal heating events. A fascinating young moon, still geologically active and evolving.

> **Moon: "Ember"** (Small rocky inner moon, of Paladin)
> - Orbital: 200,000 km from Paladin | Period: 1.0 days
> - Physical: 0.003 M⊕ | Radius: 0.13 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.18 g | Temp: 270 K
> - Atmosphere: None
> - Habitability: 1/100
> - Science: 20
> - Resources: Regolith, Uranium Ore
> - Solar Power: 1.22×
> > A warm, small rocky body close to Paladin, heated by the combination of Denebola's considerable luminosity and tidal friction. Ember's surface is dark basalt with a reddish tint from iron oxide — a color that intensifies under Denebola's blue-white light, creating an unexpectedly warm hue against the cold backdrop of space. The surface shows scattered volcanic features: low shield structures and fissure vents, some still thermally active. Ember is geologically young, like everything in the Denebola system, and still cooling from its formation.

**d — "Coronal"** (Cold ice giant, disk shepherd)
- Orbital: 15.0 AU | Ecc: 0.10 | Period: 25,200 days
- Physical: 20 M⊕ (0.063 M_J) | Radius: 3.7 R⊕ | Temp: 95 K (cloud-top)

> A Neptune-class ice giant embedded in the outer region of Denebola's debris disk, serving as a gravitational shepherd that concentrates dust and planetesimals in its orbital neighborhood. Coronal's methane-blue atmosphere is periodically brightened by dust impacts — impacts are more frequent here than in older systems, as the debris disk is still dense and dynamically active. From the planet's vicinity, the disk is visible as a luminous band across the sky, with occasional bright points marking individual planetesimals catching Denebola's light. The planet's name reflects the corona-like halo of captured dust that surrounds it — a miniature debris ring gleaned from the larger disk.

> **Moon: "Nimbus"** (Young accreting moon, of Coronal)
> - Orbital: 200,000 km from Coronal | Period: 2.0 days
> - Physical: 0.003 M⊕ | Radius: 0.13 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.18 g | Temp: 90 K
> - Atmosphere: None
> - Habitability: 0/100
> - Science: 20
> - Resources: Regolith
> - Solar Power: 0.07×
> > A moon still growing. Nimbus orbits within the corona of captured dust that surrounds Coronal, and it is actively accreting material from this mini-disk — gaining mass measurably over geological timescales. The surface is a chaotic jumble of fresh impact craters and partially buried older terrain, the geological clock constantly reset by new material raining down. Some craters still show warm infrared signatures from recent impacts. Nimbus is a snapshot of satellite formation in progress, the same process that built the Galilean moons four billion years ago now playing out in slow motion around a young Neptune-class world.

### 46. Arcturus (Alpha Bootis)
- **Distance:** 36.7 ly
- **Galactic Position:** (33.4, -20956.7) ly
- **Galactic Velocity:** (204.4, 40.9) km/s
- **Galactic Orbit:** SMA: 20,009.2 ly | Ecc: 0.200 | ω: 155.4° | M₀: 92.6° | Period: 172.4 Myr
- **Spectral Type:** K1.5III (orange giant)
- **Stars:**
  - Arcturus: Mass 1.08 M☉ | Radius 25.4 R☉ | Luminosity 170 L☉

The fourth brightest star in Earth's sky and one of the most luminous stars within 50 light-years. Arcturus is an ancient red giant — approximately 7.1 billion years old — that has swollen to 25 times the Sun's radius and brightened to 170 times its luminosity. The star may have originated in a satellite galaxy absorbed by the Milky Way long ago, based on its high proper motion and metal-poor composition. Any inner planets were consumed when Arcturus expanded off the main sequence. What survives orbits at great distance, baked by the giant's prodigious luminosity.

#### Planets

**b — "Remnant"** (Distant gas giant)
- Orbital: 8.5 AU | Ecc: 0.18 | Period: 9,200 days
- Physical: 4.5 M_J (1,430 M⊕) | Radius: 1.05 R_J | Temp: 310 K (cloud-top)
- Atmosphere: Hydrogen-helium, water vapor, iron-poor cloud chemistry | Scale height: 38 km
- Habitability: 0/100 (no solid surface)
- Science: 0
- Resources: Gas Giant Atmosphere
- Solar Power: 2.35×
> The sole surviving giant planet of an ancient system. Remnant orbits at 8.5 AU — a distance that would be freezing around the Sun but is bathed in warmth by Arcturus's enormous luminosity. Cloud-top temperatures of 310 K place this in a rare regime: warm enough for liquid water droplets in the upper atmosphere, too warm for ammonia or methane ice. The result is an atmosphere dominated by water clouds — brilliant white decks over deeper layers of alkali metals — giving Remnant a pearlescent appearance unique among the gas giants cataloged so far. The planet is old, its interior fully cooled, its atmospheric chemistry approaching equilibrium. The metal-poor composition (inherited from Arcturus's metal-poor birth environment) means fewer heavy elements in the clouds — the atmosphere is chemically simpler than younger, metal-rich gas giants. Remnant's eccentric orbit suggests gravitational scattering in the system's past — other planets may have been ejected or consumed.

> **Moon: "Crypt"** (Large icy moon, of Remnant)
> - Orbital: 1,200,000 km from Remnant | Period: 6.8 days
> - Physical: 0.030 M⊕ | Radius: 0.42 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.17 g | Temp: 200 K
> - Atmosphere: 0.005 atm — N₂ 80%, CO₂ 12%, H₂O 5% | Scale height: 22 km
> - Habitability: 12/100
> - Science: 55
> - Resources: Regolith, Water (possible subsurface ocean), Hydrocarbons (methane/ethane liquids)
> - Solar Power: 2.35×
> > A large icy moon — Titan-sized — with a substantial nitrogen atmosphere, warmed to a surprisingly mild 200 K by Arcturus's luminosity. Crypt has a differentiated interior (silicate core, ice mantle, likely subsurface ocean) and a surface of water ice and dark organic tholins under a hazy nitrogen sky. The 200 K surface temperature is warm enough for exotic cryogenic chemistry but too cold for liquid water at the surface. Methane and ethane may exist as liquids in equatorial basins — Titan-like but warmer. The atmosphere is thick enough for weather: nitrogen-methane clouds form at altitude, producing a thin hydrocarbon rain. Crypt is the most habitable body in the Arcturus system by a significant margin — not for humans (the cold and alien chemistry preclude it), but as a laboratory for prebiotic chemistry in a metal-poor environment, offering a window into how organic complexity develops around ancient, evolved stars.

> **Moon: "Shard"** (Dense metallic captured body, of Remnant)
> - Orbital: 400,000 km from Remnant | Period: 1.8 days
> - Physical: 0.008 M⊕ | Radius: 0.16 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.31 g | Temp: 280 K
> - Atmosphere: None
> - Habitability: 0/100
> - Science: 25
> - Resources: Metal Ore (iron-nickel), Regolith
> - Solar Power: 2.35×
> > An anomalously dense body on a close, mildly eccentric orbit — Shard is almost certainly a captured object, its orbital inclination and eccentricity inconsistent with co-formation. The surface is metallic: exposed iron-nickel alloy with a mirror-bright sheen under Arcturus's warm orange light, interrupted by dark silicate veins and ancient impact scars. The composition is metal-poor compared to typical iron meteorites — consistent with formation in Arcturus's metal-poor protoplanetary disk. Shard's angular shape (roughly 1,000 km long, 600 km wide) suggests it is a collision fragment, possibly from the same scattering event that gave Remnant its eccentric orbit.

**c — "Exile"** (Cold super-Earth)
- Orbital: 22 AU | Ecc: 0.12 | Period: 38,200 days
- Physical: 5.5 M⊕ | Radius: 1.62 R⊕ | Gravity: 2.10 g | Temp: 165 K
- Atmosphere: 0.45 atm — N₂ 68%, CO₂ 20%, Ar 8%, trace CH₄ | Scale height: 5.8 km
- Habitability: 10/100
- Science: 40

- Solar Power: 0.35×
> A cold, dense super-Earth in the far outer system — one of the most distant rocky planets from its star in Zone 3. At 22 AU, Exile receives the equivalent of roughly 0.35 times Earth's insolation from Arcturus — enough for a modest greenhouse effect but not enough for liquid water. The surface is frozen: water ice, CO₂ ice, and nitrogen frost cover most of the terrain. The atmosphere is thin but real, maintained by a combination of geological outgassing and the planet's high gravity. Exile is a survivor — it formed far enough from the original main-sequence star to avoid destruction during the red giant expansion, and it has maintained its atmosphere against the enhanced stellar wind. The planet's metal-poor composition and ancient age make it a relic of the galaxy's early planet-formation epoch. Standing on Exile, Arcturus would appear as a brilliant orange disc roughly 1 degree across — twice the Sun's apparent size from Earth — dominating a sky otherwise dark and starless in the giant's glare.

### 47. TRAPPIST-1
- **Distance:** 40.7 ly
- **Galactic Position:** (-16.8, -20990.5) ly
- **Galactic Velocity:** (216.2, 5.1) km/s
- **Galactic Orbit:** SMA: 20,736.1 ly | Ecc: 0.027 | ω: 151.8° | M₀: 115.3° | Period: 178.5 Myr
- **Spectral Type:** M8V (ultracool dwarf)
- **Stars:**
  - TRAPPIST-1: Mass 0.09 M☉ | Radius 0.12 R☉ | Luminosity 0.0005 L☉

The most famous planetary system in the sky. TRAPPIST-1 is an ultracool red dwarf — barely massive enough to sustain hydrogen fusion — orbited by seven rocky, Earth-sized planets packed into a region smaller than Mercury's orbit around the Sun. The planets form a near-resonant chain: their orbital periods relate to each other as near-integer ratios (8:5, 5:3, 3:2, 3:2, 4:3, 3:2), a configuration that stabilizes the system through mutual gravitational interactions. Three of the seven planets (e, f, g) orbit within the habitable zone. The system was announced in 2017 and has been the subject of intensive characterization ever since, including JWST atmospheric measurements that confirmed thin atmospheres on some worlds and ruled out hydrogen envelopes on all of them. This is humanity's best laboratory for comparative planetology — seven worlds, one star, nearly identical ages and compositions, separated only by their distance from a dim red ember.

#### Planets

**b — "Caldera"** (Hot rocky, innermost)
- Orbital: 0.0115 AU | Ecc: 0.006 | Period: 1.51 days
- Physical: 1.37 M⊕ | Radius: 1.12 R⊕ | Gravity: 1.09 g | Temp: 400 K
- Atmosphere: 0.02 atm — CO₂ 70%, SO₂ 18%, trace Na | Scale height: 12.5 km
- Habitability: 4/100
- Science: 30
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Atmospheric CO2, Rare Earth Elements, Uranium Ore
- Solar Power: 3.78×
> The innermost world, tidally locked and baked. Caldera orbits so close to TRAPPIST-1 that the star fills 4 degrees of sky from the substellar point — eight times the apparent size of Sol from Earth. The dayside temperature exceeds 400 K, and the thin volcanic atmosphere is in a constant state of production and loss: volcanic vents exhale CO₂ and SO₂ faster than the stellar wind can strip it, but not by much. The surface is dominated by a massive volcanic province centered on the substellar point, where tidal heating is most intense — a region of perpetual eruption, lava lakes, and sulfurous plumes. The nightside is frozen and airless, the atmosphere condensing and freezing out in the permanent dark. JWST confirmed the absence of a hydrogen envelope and detected thermal emission consistent with a dark, rocky surface. Caldera is the TRAPPIST-1 equivalent of Io — a world kept geologically alive by tidal forces in the resonant chain.

**c — "Pumice"** (Hot rocky)
- Orbital: 0.0158 AU | Ecc: 0.007 | Period: 2.42 days
- Physical: 1.38 M⊕ | Radius: 1.10 R⊕ | Gravity: 1.14 g | Temp: 342 K
- Atmosphere: 0.06 atm — CO₂ 82%, N₂ 10%, SO₂ 5% | Scale height: 9.8 km
- Habitability: 6/100
- Science: 30
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Atmospheric CO2, Rare Earth Elements, Uranium Ore
- Solar Power: 2.00×
> Slightly cooler and slightly farther, Pumice retains a more substantial atmosphere than Caldera — JWST detected CO₂ absorption in transit. The surface is hot but no longer magma-ocean territory: solid basaltic crust covers most of the planet, though volcanic activity is extensive. The equatorial region shows vast shield volcanoes and collapsed calderas filled with solidified lava. The atmosphere is thick enough for some heat redistribution, narrowing the day-night temperature contrast. The name reflects the planet's density — slightly lower than expected for a purely rocky composition, suggesting a partially vesicular (porous) upper crust, like pumice stone. Pumice is the first TRAPPIST-1 planet where atmospheric characterization succeeded in detail, providing a benchmark for the rest of the system.

**d — "Dusthaven"** (Warm rocky, inner habitable zone edge)
- Orbital: 0.0223 AU | Ecc: 0.008 | Period: 4.05 days
- Physical: 0.39 M⊕ | Radius: 0.79 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.63 g | Temp: 288 K
- Atmosphere: 0.15 atm — CO₂ 55%, N₂ 30%, H₂O 8%, Ar 4% | Scale height: 10.5 km
- Habitability: 22/100
- Science: 80
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Atmospheric CO2, Rare Earth Elements
- Solar Power: 1.01×
> The smallest of the seven and the most tantalizing near-miss. Dusthaven sits at the inner edge of the habitable zone, with an equilibrium temperature of 288 K — almost exactly Earth's. But its low mass (0.39 M⊕, roughly Mars-plus) means it can barely hold an atmosphere. The thin CO₂ envelope provides modest greenhouse warming, pushing equatorial dayside temperatures to ~300 K. Liquid water could exist in brief, transient pools after volcanic outgassing events, but the low gravity allows rapid atmospheric escape, and the dayside surface is mostly dry. Wind-eroded basalt and fine dust cover the terrain — hence the name. The terminator hosts the most interesting geology: tectonic fractures where subsurface volatiles seep to the surface, creating localized patches of frost and mineral efflorescence. Dusthaven is what Mars might be if it were warmer — tantalizingly close to habitability, forever falling short.

**e — "Umbraverde"** (Temperate rocky — LIFE WORLD)
- Orbital: 0.0293 AU | Ecc: 0.005 | Period: 6.10 days
- Physical: 0.69 M⊕ | Radius: 0.92 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.82 g | Temp: 251 K (equilibrium); surface 245-285 K
- Atmosphere: 0.62 atm — N₂ 66%, CO₂ 18%, H₂O 8%, Ar 3%, O₂ 2.8%, trace CH₄ | Scale height: 8.0 km
- Habitability: 50/100
- Science: 350
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Water (dayside seas), Atmospheric CO2, Rare Earth Elements, Uranium Ore
- Solar Power: 0.58×
> The crown jewel of the TRAPPIST-1 system, and one of the most important worlds in the catalog. Umbraverde sits squarely in the habitable zone, tidally locked, with a permanent dayside of moderate temperatures and a frozen nightside. The substellar region is a shallow, mineral-rich sea — warm (285 K), iron-stained, and teeming with life. Atmospheric circulation drives winds from the warm dayside outward, carrying heat and moisture to the terminator, where clouds form a permanent ring of fog and drizzle. The nightside is an ice cap, its glaciers slowly flowing toward the terminator where they melt and return water to the atmospheric cycle.
>
> The atmosphere is the most Earth-like of any world in the TRAPPIST-1 system: nitrogen-dominated, with CO₂ providing greenhouse warming and enough water vapor for weather. But the oxygen — 2.8%, rising steadily over geological time — has no abiotic explanation. It is biogenic.
>
> **Infrared Phototrophs** — the first alien photosynthesizers ever discovered, and organisms that have rewritten the textbook on photosynthesis.
>
> TRAPPIST-1 emits the vast majority of its radiation in the near-infrared, peaking around 1 micron wavelength. Visible light — the spectrum used by terrestrial plants — constitutes less than 1% of the star's output. Any photosynthetic organism here must harvest infrared photons, which are individually less energetic than visible photons. Earth's chlorophyll cannot do this efficiently. Evolution on Umbraverde found a different solution.
>
> The Infrared Phototrophs use retinal-based pigments — molecular structures related to the retinal chromophore found in Earth's halobacteria (the archaeal group responsible for the "purple Earth" hypothesis). These pigments absorb broadly across the 700-1100 nm range, capturing the peak of TRAPPIST-1's spectral output. The absorption is so broad and so efficient that the organisms appear nearly black to human eyes — they absorb almost all visible light as a side effect of their IR-optimized pigmentation. In direct view, colonies have a deep purple-black color with a faint reddish undertone.
>
> Individual organisms are flat, disc-shaped cells 8-12 micrometers in diameter, maximizing surface area for light capture. They form mat-like colonies on rocks in the shallow tidal pools of the dayside, layered 5-20 cells deep, the upper cells shading the lower ones in a stratified canopy analogous to a forest. The mats cover every illuminated rock surface in the substellar sea, extending from the shoreline to depths of 15 meters where sufficient infrared light penetrates the water.
>
> Their photosynthesis is water-splitting: they use infrared photons to cleave water molecules, extracting hydrogen for biosynthesis and releasing molecular oxygen as waste — the same net reaction as terrestrial oxygenic photosynthesis, but powered by lower-energy photons. To compensate for the reduced energy per photon, the Infrared Phototrophs use a four-photon reaction center (versus the two-photon system in Earth's plants), capturing four infrared photons to drive each water-splitting event. This is less efficient per photon but more efficient per unit of available radiation — a trade-off optimized for a star that offers plentiful IR and almost no visible light.
>
> The ecological consequences are profound. Oxygen production has been accumulating for at least a billion years, building the atmospheric O₂ concentration to 2.8% — low by Earth standards (21%), but transformative for the planet's chemistry. The ozone layer that forms from this oxygen provides UV protection for the dayside surface. Iron in the oceans is being oxidized, creating the rust-brown color of the substellar sea — Umbraverde is undergoing its own Great Oxidation Event, billions of years after Earth's.
>
> From orbit, the Infrared Phototrophs are visible as dark patches in the substellar sea — regions where the water appears nearly black, surrounded by the rust-brown of oxidized mineral sediment. The contrast between the dark biological mats and the ruddy mineral water is striking. On the ground, the mats are extraordinary: dense, velvety, purple-black films coating every rock surface, wet and glistening under the dim red light. In the shallow pools, they form undulating sheets that sway with the gentle currents. Where they grow thickest, oxygen bubbles rise continuously from the mat surface, creating a soft fizzing sound — the breath of an alien biosphere.
>
> The discovery of retinal-based photosynthesis on Umbraverde confirmed a long-standing prediction: that infrared-dominated stellar environments would select for purple/black photosynthetic pigments rather than the green of Earth's chlorophyll. The Infrared Phototrophs are not related to Earth's halobacteria — the retinal pigments evolved convergently from completely alien biochemistry. Their genetic material uses a base-4 coding system with nucleotide analogs that have no terrestrial equivalent. This is a fifth independent origin of life.
>
> Umbraverde is habitable, with caveats. The atmosphere is breathable with supplemental oxygen (2.8% O₂ is too low for unassisted human respiration but not toxic). The gravity is comfortable at 0.82 g. The temperature in the substellar region is pleasant. The tidal locking means no day-night cycle, which is disorienting but manageable. The real constraint is contamination: introducing terrestrial organisms to a world mid-oxidation event could catastrophically disrupt the biosphere. Umbraverde is a nature preserve first and a colony site second — the most biologically productive alien world yet discovered, and the only one where photosynthesis has been observed.

**f — "Shivermere"** (Cold rocky, habitable zone outer edge)
- Orbital: 0.0385 AU | Ecc: 0.010 | Period: 9.21 days
- Physical: 1.04 M⊕ | Radius: 1.05 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.94 g | Temp: 219 K
- Atmosphere: 0.48 atm — N₂ 62%, CO₂ 26%, H₂O 5%, Ar 4% | Scale height: 7.4 km
- Habitability: 28/100
- Science: 80
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Water (ice, briny lakes), Atmospheric CO2, Rare Earth Elements, Uranium Ore
- Solar Power: 0.34×
> The sixth planet straddles the outer habitable zone. Shivermere is cold — surface temperatures average 235 K with greenhouse warming, below freezing everywhere except in geothermally heated lowlands near the substellar point. Water ice covers 60% of the surface. The dayside hosts a few shallow, briny lakes kept liquid by a combination of CO₂ greenhouse warming and dissolved salts that depress the freezing point. These lakes are small, transient, and ice-covered for most of their existence. The atmosphere is thinner than Umbraverde's, with higher CO₂ and less water vapor. No life has been detected — the cold and limited liquid water preclude the photosynthetic mats found on e. Shivermere is a Mars-analog with better gravity and more atmosphere: cold, dry, and tantalizingly close to the conditions needed for sustained surface water. A potential terraforming target if the CO₂ greenhouse could be enhanced.

**g — "Icemantle"** (Cold rocky, outer habitable zone)
- Orbital: 0.0469 AU | Ecc: 0.005 | Period: 12.35 days
- Physical: 1.32 M⊕ | Radius: 1.13 R⊕ | Gravity: 1.03 g | Temp: 199 K
- Atmosphere: 0.70 atm — N₂ 58%, CO₂ 30%, Ar 6%, H₂O 3%, trace CH₄ | Scale height: 6.5 km
- Habitability: 20/100
- Science: 80
- Resources: Metal Ore, Regolith, Water (ice), Atmospheric CO2, Rare Earth Elements, Uranium Ore
- Solar Power: 0.23×
> The most massive TRAPPIST-1 planet by a thin margin, and a world of ice and thick air. Icemantle has retained a substantial CO₂-rich atmosphere, but the distance from the star means greenhouse warming can only raise surface temperatures to about 225 K — well below freezing. The entire surface is covered in water and CO₂ ice, with the thickest accumulations on the nightside. The substellar point is the warmest region (240 K), but even here the ground is frozen. The atmosphere supports weather — CO₂ cloud formation, frost deposition, and wind-driven ice crystal storms — but no liquid water. Tidal heating from the resonant chain contributes modest internal heat, driving cryovolcanic activity: nitrogen-water geysers and CO₂ eruptions have been detected, resurfacing the ice locally. Icemantle's thick atmosphere makes it a candidate for greenhouse-enhanced terraforming, and its near-Earth gravity and substantial volatile inventory make it a long-term resource hub for the TRAPPIST-1 system.

**h — "Terminus"** (Cold rocky, outermost)
- Orbital: 0.0619 AU | Ecc: 0.006 | Period: 18.77 days
- Physical: 0.33 M⊕ | Radius: 0.76 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.57 g | Temp: 173 K
- Atmosphere: 0.02 atm — CO₂ 85%, N₂ 10%, Ar 3% | Scale height: 11 km
- Habitability: 5/100
- Science: 30
- Resources: Regolith, Water (permafrost), Atmospheric CO2
- Solar Power: 0.13×
> The outermost planet, cold and small. Terminus has barely enough mass to retain its thin CO₂ atmosphere, which provides negligible greenhouse warming. Surface temperatures average 175 K — CO₂ freezes out at the poles, creating small dry-ice caps. The surface is ancient, cratered, and geologically quiet: too small for vigorous internal heat, too far from TRAPPIST-1 for significant tidal heating (it sits at the end of the resonant chain, where tidal forces are weakest). Water ice exists as subsurface permafrost. Terminus is the system's frozen bookend — a boundary marker where the TRAPPIST-1 system fades into the interstellar dark. From the surface, the star appears as a dim red disc, barely providing illumination. The six inner planets are visible as moving points of light, their transits across the star's face visible to the naked eye on clear nights.

### 48. 55 Cancri (Copernicus)
- **Distance:** 41.0 ly
- **Galactic Position:** (12.1, -21030.6) ly
- **Galactic Velocity:** (234.1, 2.5) km/s
- **Galactic Orbit:** SMA: 22,485.3 ly | Ecc: 0.065 | ω: 260.7° | M₀: 8.2° | Period: 193.0 Myr
- **Spectral Type:** K0IV-V + M3.5V (binary)
- **Stars:**
  - 55 Cancri A (Copernicus): Mass 0.91 M☉ | Radius 0.94 R☉ | Luminosity 0.57 L☉
  - 55 Cancri B: Mass 0.25 M☉ | Radius 0.26 R☉ | Luminosity 0.006 L☉ (at ~1,065 AU)

One of the first stars found to host a multi-planet system, and still one of the richest. Five planets orbit 55 Cancri A, spanning from an ultra-short-period lava world to a cold gas giant at Jupiter-like distances. The innermost, Janssen, became a sensation in 2024 when JWST detected an atmosphere — CO/CO₂ — on a world once thought too hot to retain one. The distant M-dwarf companion B has no known planets.

#### Planets

**Ab / Janssen — "Janssen"** (Ultra-short-period lava world, confirmed)
- Orbital: 0.0154 AU | Ecc: 0.05 | Period: 0.74 days (17.7 hours)
- Physical: 8.08 M⊕ | Radius: 1.88 R⊕ | Gravity: 2.29 g | Temp: 2,300 K (dayside); ~1,400 K (nightside)
- Atmosphere: ~0.01 atm — CO₂ 40%, CO 35%, SiO 12%, Na 5%, trace H₂O, SO₂ | Scale height: 22 km
- Habitability: 1/100
- Science: 50
- Resources: Metal Ore (carbon-rich), Regolith, Rare Earth Elements
- Solar Power: 2403.10×
> The diamond planet — or rather, the carbon-rich lava world that launched a thousand headlines. Janssen orbits 55 Cancri A every 17.7 hours, tidally locked, its dayside a hemisphere of molten rock at 2,300 K. The 2024 JWST detection of a CO/CO₂ atmosphere was groundbreaking: this is a world hot enough to vaporize rock, yet it maintains a thin gaseous envelope continuously replenished by magma ocean outgassing. The dayside is a globe of incandescent magma — orange-white, turbulent, with convective cells visible from orbit as darker patches in the glow. The nightside is cooler but still molten at 1,400 K, the atmosphere carrying enough heat to prevent solidification. Silicon monoxide and sodium vapor condense on the nightside as fine mineral rain. Janssen's bulk density suggests a carbon-rich interior rather than an iron-rich one — not literally diamond, but a world where carbides and graphite may dominate the mantle instead of silicates. The implications for planetary formation in carbon-rich environments are profound. Landing is obviously impossible — this is a world to observe from orbit, where the dayside fills the view with a lake of glowing stone stretching to every horizon.

**Ac / Galileo — "Galileo"** (Hot gas giant, confirmed)
- Orbital: 0.115 AU | Ecc: 0.01 | Period: 14.65 days
- Physical: 0.82 M_J (261 M⊕) | Radius: 1.05 R_J | Temp: 690 K (cloud-top)
- Atmosphere: Hydrogen-helium, silicate clouds, alkali metals | Scale height: 40 km
- Habitability: 0/100 (no solid surface)
- Science: 0
- Resources: Gas Giant Atmosphere
- Solar Power: 43.10×
> A hot Jupiter in a near-circular orbit, heated to the point where its atmosphere is a soup of silicate condensates and alkali metal vapors. Galileo's cloud tops show sodium and potassium absorption — the atmosphere is hot enough for these metals to exist as free atoms in the gas phase. Silicate clouds form at lower altitudes, creating a layered atmospheric structure: clear, metal-rich gas above, opaque mineral clouds below. The planet appears as a dark, bronze sphere — low albedo due to the alkali absorption, with occasional brighter patches where silicate clouds breach to higher altitudes. Galileo's tight orbit and substantial mass make it the dominant gravitational influence in the inner system, likely responsible for Janssen's close-in orbit through past dynamical interactions.

**Ad / Brahe — "Brahe"** (Warm gas giant, confirmed)
- Orbital: 0.240 AU | Ecc: 0.05 | Period: 44.4 days
- Physical: 0.17 M_J (54 M⊕) | Radius: 0.72 R_J | Temp: 500 K (cloud-top)
- Atmosphere: Hydrogen-helium, water vapor, alkali haze | Scale height: 32 km
- Habitability: 0/100 (no solid surface)
- Science: 0
- Resources: Gas Giant Atmosphere
- Solar Power: 9.90×
> A warm Saturn — less massive than Jupiter but inflated by stellar heating. Brahe occupies the gap between the hot inner planets and the cooler outer system, receiving enough irradiation for water vapor to dominate the upper atmospheric chemistry. The cloud deck is a mix of water vapor and silicate haze, creating a pale gold appearance. Transit observations show a puffy, extended atmosphere with a large scale height — Brahe is slowly losing its hydrogen envelope to photoevaporation, though the process will take billions of years to strip it significantly. The planet has a nearly circular orbit, suggesting it formed in place rather than migrating.

**Ae / Harriot — "Harriot"** (Cool gas giant in habitable zone, confirmed)
- Orbital: 0.781 AU | Ecc: 0.07 | Period: 260 days
- Physical: 0.17 M_J (54 M⊕) | Radius: 0.68 R_J | Temp: 270 K (cloud-top)
- Atmosphere: Hydrogen-helium, water clouds, ammonia | Scale height: 24 km
- Habitability: 0/100 (no solid surface)
- Science: 0
- Resources: Gas Giant Atmosphere
- Solar Power: 0.93×
> A gas giant orbiting in the habitable zone of 55 Cancri A — the zone where a rocky planet might support liquid water. Harriot itself has no surface, but its cloud-top temperature of 270 K means the upper atmosphere hosts extensive water-cloud decks, giving the planet a brilliant white appearance. This is one of the most reflective planets in Zone 3, its high albedo making it visible as a bright point in telescopic observations. Any rocky moons would receive Earth-like insolation and could potentially support liquid water. Two small moons have been confirmed — modest in size, but orbiting in a region where conditions are tantalizing.

> **Moon: "Digges"** (Small rocky moon with trace atmosphere, of Harriot)
> - Orbital: 180,000 km from Harriot | Period: 1.5 days
> - Physical: 0.012 M⊕ | Radius: 0.28 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.15 g | Temp: 260 K
> - Atmosphere: 0.002 atm — CO₂ 70%, N₂ 20%, trace H₂O | Scale height: 14 km
> - Habitability: 8/100
> - Science: 100
> - Resources: Regolith, Water (polar ice)
> - Solar Power: 0.93×
> > Named for Thomas Digges, the Elizabethan astronomer and contemporary of Thomas Harriot. Digges is a small rocky moon orbiting in the habitable zone of 55 Cancri A — it receives Earth-like insolation, and the surface temperature of 260 K is cold but not impossibly so. A wisp of atmosphere clings to the surface, maintained by volcanic outgassing from tidal heating. Water ice exists at the poles and in permanently shadowed craters. Under different circumstances — with more mass, more atmosphere, more time — Digges could have been a habitable world. As it stands, it is a tantalizing near-miss: the right location, the wrong size.

> **Moon: "Frost"** (Tiny icy body, of Harriot)
> - Orbital: 400,000 km from Harriot | Period: 4.0 days
> - Physical: 0.002 M⊕ | Radius: 0.11 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.17 g | Temp: 220 K
> - Atmosphere: None
> - Habitability: 1/100
> - Science: 20
> - Resources: Regolith, Water (water ice)
> - Solar Power: 0.93×
> > A small, bright ice body in a stable outer orbit. Frost's surface is clean water ice with a high albedo, fractured by mild tidal stresses but showing no cryovolcanic activity — the tidal heating at this distance from the low-mass Harriot is negligible. The surface is ancient and cratered. Frost's primary interest is as a water source for any future operations in the 55 Cancri habitable zone.

**Af / Lipperhey — "Lipperhey"** (Cold gas giant, outermost, confirmed)
- Orbital: 5.45 AU | Ecc: 0.09 | Period: 5,218 days (~14.3 years)
- Physical: 3.82 M_J (1,214 M⊕) | Radius: 1.08 R_J | Temp: 100 K (cloud-top)
- Atmosphere: Hydrogen-helium, ammonia and methane ice clouds | Scale height: 22 km
- Habitability: 0/100 (no solid surface)
- Science: 0
- Resources: Gas Giant Atmosphere
- Solar Power: 0.02×
> The outermost and most massive planet, a cold super-Jupiter at a distance similar to Jupiter's orbit around the Sun. Lipperhey anchors the 55 Cancri system gravitationally, its mass dominating the outer region. The atmosphere shows typical cold-giant chemistry: ammonia cirrus clouds in the upper atmosphere, water clouds deeper, with methane absorption giving the planet a faint blue-green tint beneath the white ammonia bands. Storm systems are large but slow-evolving, with banding patterns that shift over years. Lipperhey was one of the first exoplanets discovered by radial velocity (in 2002) and remains one of the best-characterized Jupiter-analog planets known.

> **Moon: "Aperture"** (Icy moon, of Lipperhey)
> - Orbital: 750,000 km from Lipperhey | Period: 4.2 days
> - Physical: 0.020 M⊕ | Radius: 0.36 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.15 g | Temp: 95 K
> - Atmosphere: 0.0002 atm — N₂, trace CO₂
> - Habitability: 6/100
> - Science: 30
> - Resources: Regolith, Water (subsurface ocean)
> - Solar Power: 0.02×
> > A Ganymede-class icy moon with a differentiated interior and likely subsurface ocean. Aperture's surface shows a mix of ancient cratered terrain and younger grooved terrain — evidence of past tectonic resurfacing driven by tidal heating. Cryovolcanic features dot the surface, including smooth, refrozen plains and low-profile shield structures built from repeated water-ice eruptions. The thin nitrogen atmosphere is maintained by outgassing from these features. A prime target for subsurface ocean exploration in the 55 Cancri system.

> **Moon: "Cruciform"** (Volcanic inner moon, of Lipperhey)
> - Orbital: 250,000 km from Lipperhey | Period: 0.9 days
> - Physical: 0.004 M⊕ | Radius: 0.14 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.20 g | Temp: 110 K (surface avg; eruption sites 1,200+ K)
> - Atmosphere: 0.00001 atm — SO₂ sublimation
> - Habitability: 1/100
> - Science: 50
> - Resources: Regolith, Uranium Ore
> - Solar Power: 0.02×
> > A small Io-analog moon in a tight orbit around Lipperhey, driven to volcanic activity by tidal resonance with Aperture. Cruciform's surface is a patchwork of sulfur deposits and silicate lava flows, with active eruption sites reaching 1,200 K. The name derives from a distinctive cross-shaped pattern of four major volcanic provinces arranged symmetrically around the equator — a coincidence of crustal weakness zones that gives the moon a recognizable appearance in low-resolution imaging. Cruciform feeds a faint sulfur torus around Lipperhey's orbit.

> **Moon: "Helix"** (Ancient icy Callisto-analog, of Lipperhey)
> - Orbital: 1,800,000 km from Lipperhey | Period: 12.0 days
> - Physical: 0.015 M⊕ | Radius: 0.30 R⊕ | Gravity: 0.17 g | Temp: 85 K
> - Atmosphere: None
> - Habitability: 2/100
> - Science: 20
> - Resources: Regolith
> - Solar Power: 0.02×
> > The outermost significant moon of Lipperhey, and a body of profound antiquity. Helix's surface has not been resurfaced in over four billion years — it is a geological time capsule, its dark, heavily cratered terrain recording the full bombardment history of the 55 Cancri system. The surface is uniformly ancient: no bright terrain, no fractures, no cryovolcanic features. The interior is cold and undifferentiated. The name comes from a spiral pattern of concentric multi-ring impact basins on the leading hemisphere — three overlapping ancient impacts that create a helix-like structure visible from orbit.
